Everything you need to know

  • Normal blood pH is 7.35–7.45. Below = acidosis, above = alkalosis.
  • pH is inversely related to H⁺; each unit is a tenfold change.
  • Negative feedback opposes the stimulus; positive feedback amplifies it.
  • Ionic = electrons transferred. Covalent = electrons shared (equally = nonpolar, unequally = polar).
  • Diffusion moves solute; osmosis moves water. Neither costs ATP.
  • Water moves toward the higher solute concentration.
  • Glycolysis: cytoplasm, anaerobic, ~2 ATP. Aerobic respiration: mitochondria, ~30–32 ATP.
  • Transcription = nucleus (DNA→mRNA). Translation = ribosome (mRNA→protein).
  • Simple squamous = diffusion/filtration. Stratified squamous = protection. Transitional = stretch.
  • Epidermal layers deep→superficial: basale, spinosum, granulosum, lucidum, corneum.
  • The epidermis is avascular; the dermis carries the vessels and nerves.
  • Vitamin D: UVB on skin → liver (calcidiol) → kidney (calcitriol, active).
  • Osteoblasts build. Osteoclasts carve.
  • PTH raises blood calcium; calcitonin lowers it.
  • Endochondral (cartilage model) → long bones. Intramembranous (membrane) → flat bones.
  • Bone is ~2/3 calcium phosphate (hardness) and ~1/3 collagen (tensile strength).
  • Cardiac muscle is the most fatigue-resistant — highest mitochondrial density.
  • Only smooth muscle regenerates well; skeletal and cardiac scar.
  • ATP is needed to detach myosin from actin — hence rigor mortis.
  • Myelin: Schwann cells in the PNS, oligodendrocytes in the CNS.
  • Resting −70 mV → threshold −55 mV → Na⁺ in (depolarize) → K⁺ out (repolarize) → hyperpolarize −90 mV.
  • Sympathetic = thoracolumbar, fight-or-flight. Parasympathetic = craniosacral, rest-and-digest.
  • Olfaction is the only sense that bypasses the thalamus.
  • Spicy is not a taste — it is a pain/heat sensation.
  • Near vision = lens rounds up. Far vision = lens flattens.
  • Rods = dim light, no color, periphery. Cones = color and detail, fovea.
  • Semicircular canals = rotation. Utricle/saccule + otoliths = gravity and linear acceleration.
  • 20/200 is far worse than 20/20; 20/15 is better than average.
  • Lipid-soluble hormones → intracellular receptors (slow, long-lasting, alter transcription).
  • Ion channel-linked receptors are the fastest (e.g. ACh at the NMJ).
  • FSH grows the follicle; LH triggers ovulation.
  • Insulin (beta cells) lowers glucose; glucagon (alpha cells) raises it.
  • Hemostasis: 1 vascular spasm → 2 platelet plug → 3 coagulation.
  • Intrinsic and extrinsic pathways converge at Factor X; thrombin then converts fibrinogen to fibrin.
  • Neutrophils = first responders. Eosinophils = parasites/allergy. Basophils = histamine.
  • Arterioles are the main resistance vessels and set blood pressure.
  • S1 = AV valves closing (start of systole). S2 = semilunar valves closing (start of diastole).
  • CO = HR × stroke volume (~5 L/min at rest).
  • Conduction: SA node → AV node → Bundle of His → bundle branches → Purkinje fibers.
  • P = atrial depolarization, QRS = ventricular depolarization, T = ventricular repolarization.
  • Innate = fast, non-specific, no memory. Adaptive = slower, specific, has memory.
  • NK cells are lymphocytes but act innately.
  • Plasma cells (activated B cells) make the antibodies; helper T cells coordinate everything.
  • The lymphatic system returns leaked interstitial fluid to the blood — blockage causes edema.
  • Inhalation: diaphragm contracts → thoracic volume ↑ → pressure ↓ → air in (Boyle's law).
  • Surfactant (Type II cells) lowers surface tension and prevents alveolar collapse.
  • O₂ ~98% on hemoglobin; CO₂ ~70% as bicarbonate.
  • Retained CO₂ → respiratory acidosis; blown off CO₂ → respiratory alkalosis.
  • Carbohydrate digestion starts in the mouth; protein in the stomach; fat mainly in the small intestine.
  • Bile emulsifies (mechanical); pancreatic lipase digests (chemical). Bile is not an enzyme.
  • Jejunum = main absorption. Ileum = vitamin B12, bile salts, fat-soluble vitamins.
  • CCK ← fat/protein (contracts gallbladder). Secretin ← acid (releases bicarbonate).
  • Filtration happens only at the glomerulus; the PCT does most reabsorption.
  • Descending limb = water out. Ascending limb = ions out, impermeable to water.
  • Reabsorption = filtrate→blood. Secretion = blood→filtrate.
  • In menopause estrogen falls but FSH and LH rise — lost negative feedback.

Everything explained, module by module

What physiology actually asks

Anatomy asks what is this and where is it. Physiology asks what does it do and how. Every question in this course is really the same question in different clothing: here is a structure, here is its job, and here is what goes wrong when it fails.

You will see three scales. Cell physiology looks inside a single cell — how it makes ATP, how it moves glucose across its membrane. Organ physiology zooms out to one organ, like how the kidney filters blood. Systemic physiology looks at organs cooperating, like the heart and lungs together during exercise. Pathophysiology is what this becomes when something breaks.

Bonds, and why water behaves the way it does

Atoms bond to fill their outer shell, and there are only really two ways to do it. In an ionic bond one atom hands an electron over completely — sodium gives its lone outer electron to chlorine, both end up charged, and opposite charges cling together. In a covalent bond they share instead. If they share evenly it is nonpolar; if one atom pulls harder it is polar, and the molecule ends up with a slightly negative end and a slightly positive end.

Water is the polar molecule that matters. Oxygen pulls the shared electrons toward itself, so oxygen sits slightly negative and the hydrogens slightly positive. That lopsidedness is why water dissolves salts — it surrounds ions and pulls them apart — and why water molecules stick to each other through hydrogen bonds. Hydrogen bonds are weak, and that weakness is the point: they form and break easily, which is exactly what DNA needs to unzip and what proteins need to change shape.

Homeostasis is a thermostat, not a freeze-frame

Homeostasis does not mean values stay still. It means they wobble around a set point and get pulled back. Every loop has the same five parts: a stimulus knocks a value off target, a receptor notices, a control center decides, an effector acts, and the response feeds back.

In negative feedback the response opposes the stimulus and therefore switches itself off. You get too warm, you sweat, you cool down, you stop sweating. Nearly everything in your body works this way.

In positive feedback the response amplifies the stimulus, so it escalates until something external stops it. Labor is the classic case — contractions release oxytocin, oxytocin strengthens contractions, and it only ends with delivery. Clotting is the other. Positive feedback is not "bad"; it is just the rarer design, used when the body needs to finish something decisively.

pH, and why 7.35–7.45 is so tight

pH measures hydrogen ions, and it runs backwards: more H⁺ means a lower number. It is also logarithmic, so each unit is a tenfold change — pH 2 has ten million times more H⁺ than pH 9.

Blood sits between 7.35 and 7.45. That window is narrow because protein shape depends on pH, and enzyme function depends on protein shape. Drift outside it and a great many reactions degrade at once. Below 7.35 is acidosis; above 7.45 is alkalosis.

Buffers are what hold the line. A buffer soaks up H⁺ when acid rises and releases it when acid falls — it works in both directions. The main one in blood is the bicarbonate system, which is also why breathing (CO₂) and the kidneys (bicarbonate) both end up controlling your pH.

Electrons, shells, and why ions matter

An atom is a nucleus of protons and neutrons surrounded by electrons in shells, and only the outermost — the valence shell — does chemistry. An atom is stable when that shell is full and reactive when it is not, so it gives away, takes, or shares electrons until it is full. Lose an electron and the atom becomes a positive cation; gain one and it becomes a negative anion.

Your electrolyte panel is that rule applied. Sodium holds water in the extracellular fluid, potassium sets the resting membrane potential, calcium triggers contraction and clotting, magnesium activates enzymes, chloride follows sodium, and bicarbonate and phosphate buffer acid. Charge is what makes each of these do work — which is why a shift in potassium too small to notice can stop a heart outright.

The buffers, and the three lines of defense

A buffer is a pair of molecules that can absorb a hydrogen ion or release one, so pH barely moves when acid arrives. You run three of them. The bicarbonate system handles extracellular fluid and does most of the work; the phosphate system works inside cells and in the kidney tubules. Protein buffers, hemoglobin above all, work wherever protein is, taking up hydrogen and handing it back.

Buffers act in seconds, but they only bind the acid, they do not remove it. The lungs follow within minutes, blowing off carbon dioxide before it becomes carbonic acid. The kidneys take hours to days, but they alone excrete fixed acid and rebuild the bicarbonate you spent — which is why acid-base trouble in kidney failure deepens for days instead of correcting itself.

Positive feedback is not the villain

Negative feedback reverses a change, and it holds nearly every variable steady. Positive feedback amplifies a change instead, driving the variable further from its start, which students read as pathology. It is not. It is what the body uses when a job needs finishing, not balancing.

There are only a handful. In labor, cervical stretch drives oxytocin, which drives stronger contractions, which stretch the cervix further. Activated platelets recruit more platelets in clotting, sodium entry opens more sodium channels in the action potential, rising estrogen tips the pituitary into the LH surge, and suckling drives the milk ejection reflex. Each loop ends because something outside it removes the stimulus — the baby is delivered, the vessel is sealed — which is why positive feedback is dangerous only when that end never arrives.

Set points can move

A thermostat has one setting. You have several, and they move.

In fever, pyrogens raise the hypothalamic set point to thirty-nine degrees Celsius; your temperature has not escaped control, it is being controlled to a higher target — which is why you shiver under blankets while already hot. At altitude, erythropoietin resets red cell mass upward over weeks. In chronic hypertension, baroreceptors reset and treat the raised pressure as normal. Even across a day, temperature, cortisol and blood pressure follow a circadian swing.

Homeostasis does not mean a value never changes; it means a value is defended. When a number sits outside the range, ask whether control has failed or the target has moved — cooling a fever fights the body's own target, while cooling heat stroke saves a life.

Gradients are stored energy

Ions do not drift at random. The body spends roughly a fifth of its resting energy running the sodium-potassium pump, which pushes three sodium out for every two potassium in, against both concentrations. That looks wasteful until you see what it buys.

The result is a gradient — sodium high outside, potassium high inside — and a gradient is stored energy, like water held behind a dam. Opening a sodium channel releases it, and that release is the action potential and the muscle twitch; the same gradient drags glucose into the gut lining alongside sodium. Nothing is built on demand; it is already charged and waiting.

Which is why cells swell within minutes of losing blood flow and die soon after — no oxygen, no ATP, no pump, and the dam drains.

Chemical bonds

Feedback loop components

ComponentRole
StimulusThe change that disturbs the set point

The membrane decides everything

A cell is defined by what it lets in. The plasma membrane is a phospholipid bilayer — water-loving heads out, water-fearing tails in — and it is selectively permeable: some things cross freely, some need help, some are refused.

Small nonpolar molecules like oxygen and carbon dioxide slip straight through. Charged ions and large polar molecules cannot, so the membrane studs itself with proteins: transport proteins move things, anchoring proteins hold the cytoskeleton, enzyme proteins catalyze, adhesion proteins join neighboring cells, and antigen proteins act as an identity badge saying this cell is one of ours.

Passive versus active — the only distinction that matters

Ask one question of any transport process: does it cost ATP?

If the answer is no, it is passive, and it must be moving down a gradient. Simple diffusion is solute drifting from crowded to empty. Facilitated diffusion is the same thing but through a protein, because the molecule cannot cross the lipid alone — it still costs nothing. Osmosis is the special case where water moves, and water always moves toward the higher solute concentration. The phrase to hold onto is: water follows salt.

If the answer is yes, it is active, and it can push against a gradient. The sodium–potassium pump throws three Na⁺ out and pulls two K⁺ in for every ATP. Because it exports more positive charge than it imports, it leaves the inside slightly negative — which is where the resting membrane potential comes from, and why this pump reappears in the nerve and muscle modules.

Organelles as jobs, not vocabulary

Learn each organelle as structure → function → what fails. The nucleus holds DNA and runs transcription. The nucleolus inside it builds ribosomes. Ribosomes translate mRNA into protein. Rough ER is studded with ribosomes and processes those proteins; smooth ER has none and instead makes lipids and detoxifies. The Golgi modifies and packages for shipping. Mitochondria make ATP. Lysosomes digest waste; peroxisomes handle fatty acids and free radicals.

The pattern predicts cell type. A cell secreting protein hormone will be crammed with rough ER and Golgi. A cell making steroids will be full of smooth ER. A muscle cell that never tires will be dense with mitochondria.

From gene to protein, and why mitosis matters

Transcription happens in the nucleus: DNA is copied into mRNA. Translation happens at the ribosome: mRNA is read three letters at a time and the matching amino acids are strung together. Three RNAs do the work — mRNA carries the message, tRNA ferries amino acids, and rRNA forms the ribosome itself and catalyzes the bonds.

Mitosis produces two genetically identical daughter cells. That is the whole point: growth and repair need faithful copies. Contrast this with meiosis in Module 14, which deliberately produces variety.

What osmosis actually moves

Osmosis is the diffusion of water, and only water. The solute stays where it is, because the membrane is selectively permeable — it lets water through and holds the solute back. The water crosses instead, moving toward the side with more solute — the side where water is scarce. Say it to yourself as: water follows salt.

Tonicity describes what a solution does to a cell placed in it. In an isotonic solution water moves equally both ways and the cell keeps its shape. In a hypotonic solution water floods in and a red cell swells and lyses. In a hypertonic solution water leaves and the cell shrivels, or crenates. Which is why free water infused fast is dangerous — a cell cannot refuse water, and a swelling brain has nowhere to go.

Facilitated diffusion is still passive

The word facilitated makes you assume energy is being spent. It is not. Facilitated diffusion uses a membrane protein — either a channel or a carrier — but the molecule still travels down its concentration gradient, from high to low, and the cell pays nothing for the trip. The protein is there only because the molecule is too large or too charged to cross the oily interior of the bilayer alone. Glucose slips into most cells on a carrier; potassium leaks out through channels.

What separates it from simple diffusion is saturation: transporters are finite, so once they are all occupied, raising the concentration moves nothing extra. Any carrier can be saturated, which is why glucose spills into the urine once blood sugar climbs — the kidney's glucose carriers are already full.

The pump that pays for everything

The sodium-potassium pump is the largest energy bill most cells pay, and it earns it. For every ATP it spends, it pushes three sodium ions out and pulls two potassium ions in, both against their gradients — primary active transport, ATP spent directly on what is moved.

Two things follow. The pump keeps potassium high inside and sodium low, and potassium leaking back out down that gradient is the main reason the interior stays negative — the resting membrane potential nerve and muscle depend on; the uneven exchange adds a little more. And sodium, held low, strains to rush back in, so the cell hitches a passenger to that rush — secondary active transport, how glucose is reclaimed in gut and kidney, and why a failing pump starves the cell of glucose too.

When the molecule is too big to transport

Channels and pumps move one small molecule at a time. Anything larger — a bacterium, a droplet of fluid, a finished protein — has to be wrapped in membrane instead.

Endocytosis brings material in, and it takes three forms. Phagocytosis is cell eating: the membrane pushes out arms — pseudopods — around a solid particle, which is how neutrophils and macrophages clear bacteria. Pinocytosis is cell drinking, an indiscriminate sip of extracellular fluid. Receptor-mediated endocytosis is the selective one — a receptor must bind its specific ligand before the membrane pits inward. Exocytosis runs the process backwards, fusing a vesicle with the membrane to release hormones, neurotransmitters or enzymes outward. Which is why a defective LDL receptor leaves cholesterol circulating — the cell cannot import what it cannot grip.

The cell cycle and its checkpoints

Most of a cell's life is not mitosis. Interphase occupies the great majority of it and has three parts: G1, where the cell grows and does its actual job; S phase, where the DNA is copied so each daughter cell will receive a complete set; and G2, where the copy is proofread and the machinery of division is built.

Mitosis then divides the nucleus in four stages — prophase, metaphase, anaphase, telophase — and cytokinesis pinches the cytoplasm into two cells. At fixed points in the cycle sit checkpoints that halt everything if DNA is damaged or chromosomes are misaligned, and cells beyond repair are told to self-destruct by apoptosis. Which is why cancer is at heart a checkpoint failure — damaged cells that should have stopped keep dividing.

Epithelium: shape tells you the job

Epithelium covers surfaces and lines cavities, and its shape is a direct clue to its function. Squamous cells are flat — thin means fast diffusion, which is why alveoli and capillaries use them. Cuboidal cells are boxy with room for machinery, so they secrete and absorb, as in kidney tubules. Columnar cells are tall with even more room, lining the digestive tract for absorption.

Then layers: simple means one layer and favors exchange; stratified means many layers and favors protection, which is why skin and esophagus are stratified squamous. Transitional epithelium changes shape as it stretches, which only the bladder and ureters need. Pseudostratified looks layered but is not, and in the airway it comes with cilia and goblet cells to trap and sweep debris.

Every epithelium is avascular. It is fed by diffusion from the connective tissue beneath, which is why the outermost skin cells eventually die.

Connective tissue is mostly matrix

Where epithelium is packed with cells, connective tissue is mostly the matrix between them, and the matrix determines the property. Dense collagen in parallel bundles gives one-directional tensile strength — tendons. Collagen in every direction gives strength from all sides — the dermis. Elastic fibers let a structure spring back — the ear. Mineralized matrix gives hardness — bone. And a liquid matrix gives transport — blood and lymph, which are connective tissues too, however odd that sounds at first.

Skin as an organ with a job list

The epidermis is the barrier. Cells are born in the stratum basale, and as they are pushed outward they fill with keratin, flatten, and die, ending as the stratum corneum — a sheet of tough, waterproof dead cells. That is not a design flaw; a dead, keratin-packed surface is exactly what resists water loss and blocks pathogens.

The dermis underneath carries the blood vessels, nerves, hair follicles and glands. Its vessels also do thermoregulation: dilate to dump heat, constrict to keep it. The hypodermis below is fat — insulation, cushioning, anchoring.

Melanin and vitamin D — the same UV, two opposite stories

Melanin, made by melanocytes in the basale, absorbs ultraviolet light before it can damage DNA. More melanin means more protection from UV.

But the skin also needs UV. UVB converts a cholesterol precursor into vitamin D3, the liver converts that to calcidiol, and the kidney makes the active form, calcitriol. Calcitriol's job is to increase calcium absorption from the gut — which is why a skin topic ends up controlling your bones, and why chronic kidney disease weakens the skeleton.

What holds epithelium together

Every epithelium is polarized — an apical surface facing the lumen or the outside world, and a basal surface anchored to a basement membrane. That anchoring matters, because epithelium is avascular: every nutrient must diffuse up from the connective tissue beneath.

Five junction types hold the sheet together. Tight junctions ring the cells near the apex and control what passes between them, keeping the lumen chemically separate from the tissue below. Adherens junctions and desmosomes rivet neighbors against mechanical stress, and hemidesmosomes pin the bottom row to the basement membrane. Gap junctions leave pores open so ions pass straight from cell to cell. And because epithelium divides constantly to replace what it loses, every division is another chance for a mutation — which is why most adult cancers are carcinomas.

The four body membranes

A body membrane is a sheet of epithelium plus the connective tissue under it — with one deliberate exception.

Mucous membranes line every passage open to the outside — respiratory, digestive, urinary, reproductive — and stay wet, mostly with mucus that traps debris and keeps the surface from drying. Serous membranes line closed cavities and wrap the organs inside — pleura, pericardium, peritoneum — each with a parietal layer on the wall, a visceral layer on the organ, and a film of serous fluid between so the two glide. The cutaneous membrane is the skin, and the only dry one. Synovial membranes line joint cavities and contain no epithelium — connective tissue only, the exception. Inflame a serous membrane and those layers roughen and grate — which is why pericarditis rubs and hurts with every heartbeat.

Walking up the epidermis

Start at the bottom. The stratum basale is where nearly all division happens, pushing daughters upward, and it houses Merkel cells and the melanocytes that hand pigment to keratinocytes to cap their nuclei; melanin is the shield against UV, not the cause of damage. The stratum spinosum is packed with desmosomes and carries immune Langerhans cells. In the stratum granulosum the cells fill with keratohyalin, waterproof themselves, and die. The stratum lucidum appears only in thick skin — palms and soles. The stratum corneum is twenty to thirty layers of dead, keratin-filled cells.

The epidermis has no vessels of its own — every nutrient diffuses up from dermal capillaries — which is why a burn that strips the basale cannot regrow from that layer and must resurface from follicles left in the dermis.

What the dermis carries

Two layers, two jobs. The papillary layer is loose areolar tissue thrown into dermal papillae that push up into the epidermis, carrying the capillary loops that feed it, the Meissner corpuscles that read light touch, and — on your fingertips — the ridges that make fingerprints. The reticular layer beneath is dense irregular connective tissue — interwoven collagen for strength, elastin for recoil — and it is the address for hair follicles, sweat and sebaceous glands, blood vessels, nerves, and deep-pressure Pacinian corpuscles.

Those follicles and glands are lined with epidermal cells, which is how burn depth is judged. A partial-thickness burn spares them, so new epithelium creeps outward from each until the surface closes. A full-thickness burn destroys them — which is why it has no source of new skin and needs a graft.

Glands, and the three ways they let go

Skin glands are named for how the cell releases its product, and only one of the three leaves the cell intact. Merocrine glands package the product into vesicles and let it out by exocytosis, losing nothing — the eccrine sweat glands that cool you by evaporation work this way, as do salivary and pancreatic glands.

Apocrine glands pinch off the apical part of the cell with the secretion; they empty into hair follicles in the axilla and groin, and ceruminous and mammary glands are counted with them. Their sweat is odorless until skin bacteria digest it — that is body odor. Holocrine glands rupture the whole cell — sebaceous glands do this, so sebum is lipid plus cell debris, which is why a plugged follicle keeps filling, inflames, and becomes acne.

Two bone shapes, two jobs

Flat bones are built like a sandwich — compact bone, spongy diploe, compact bone. That structure is light and impact-absorbing, which suits protection: skull, sternum, ribs.

Long bones are hollow tubes with a marrow cavity down the middle. A tube resists bending nearly as well as a solid rod at a fraction of the weight, which suits their job as levers for movement.

Bone also does two things students forget: red marrow makes blood cells, and the mineral matrix is the body's calcium bank.

Four cells, one balance

Osteoprogenitor cells are the stem cells. Osteoblasts build matrix. When an osteoblast gets walled into the matrix it made, it becomes an osteocyte, sitting in a pocket called a lacuna and staying in touch with its neighbors through tiny channels called canaliculi. Osteoclasts carve bone away and release calcium into the blood.

Remodeling is these last two working against each other for life. Tip the balance toward osteoblasts and bone thickens; tip it toward osteoclasts and bone thins. That single idea explains why loading a bone strengthens it, why bed rest weakens it, and why osteoporosis happens after menopause when falling estrogen releases the brake on osteoclasts.

Two ways to make a bone

Endochondral ossification lays down a hyaline cartilage model first and replaces it with bone. It makes long bones, and it leaves behind the epiphyseal plate — the cartilage growth zone that lets a bone lengthen until it ossifies at maturity.

Intramembranous ossification skips cartilage entirely and forms bone directly within a fibrous membrane. It makes flat bones.

Calcium is a blood value, not a bone value

The body does not regulate bone; it regulates blood calcium, and bone is the reserve it raids. When blood calcium falls, PTH is released and Pulls calcium into the blood — stimulating osteoclasts, increasing gut absorption via vitamin D, and reducing losses in urine. When blood calcium rises, calcitonin tones it down by inhibiting osteoclasts and favoring deposition. Two hormones, opposite directions, one narrow target range.

How bones get longer, and how they get thicker

A long bone lengthens and thickens by different mechanisms.

Length comes from the epiphyseal plate, five zones of cartilage. The resting zone anchors it to the epiphysis; in the proliferative zone chondrocytes divide into columns that shove the epiphysis away; in the hypertrophic zone they swell; in the calcification zone the hardening matrix starves them; and in the ossification zone osteoblasts lay bone over them. Cartilage is added above as fast as it is replaced below, so the plate never thins.

Width comes from appositional growth — osteoblasts under the periosteum add bone outside while osteoclasts widen the marrow cavity within. Until sex steroids close the plate at puberty's end it is that bone's only source of length, which is why a child's growth-plate injury can shorten the limb for life.

Remodeling, and why load decides where bone goes

Bone is not inert — about a tenth of your skeleton is dismantled and rebuilt every year.

The cycle runs in a fixed order. Osteocytes trapped in the matrix sense strain and microcracks and call for turnover. Osteoclasts arrive and dissolve mineral with acid and matrix with enzymes, leaving a pit. Osteoblasts then fill that pit with osteoid and mineralize it — weeks to dig out, months to fill back in. Removal and replacement are coupled, so osteoclasts never build anything; they only make room.

What decides where bone goes is mechanical load. Loaded bone thickens along its lines of stress; unloaded bone is resorbed within weeks, which is why a bedbound or casted patient, or an astronaut, loses density fast — and why weight-bearing exercise is the cheapest osteoporosis prevention there is.

The hormones that build and the hormones that thin

Growth and maintenance are hormonally driven, and the same hormone can do opposite things at different times.

Growth hormone, working through IGF-1 from the liver, drives cartilage proliferation at the plate; thyroid hormone is permissive, so untreated congenital hypothyroidism stunts growth. Sex steroids cause the pubertal spurt and then shut the plate — excess growth hormone before closure gives gigantism, after closure acromegaly.

Maintenance is the other half. Calcitriol, vitamin D activated by the kidney, lets the gut absorb the calcium mineralization needs. Estrogen restrains osteoclasts, which is why bone loss accelerates sharply after menopause. PTH held continuously high strips calcium from bone, while the same hormone in short daily pulses builds it. And glucocorticoids suppress osteoblasts directly — which is why months of prednisone quietly produce a fragile skeleton.

Two materials, two ways to break

Bone is a composite of two materials, and each fails differently.

The organic third is mostly collagen, which gives tensile strength and lets bone bend without snapping. The inorganic two-thirds is hydroxyapatite — calcium-phosphate crystals packed along those fibers for hardness and compressive strength. Collagen without mineral is rubbery; mineral without collagen is chalk.

That explains the diseases. Without vitamin D, osteoid is laid down but never properly mineralized — rickets in the growing child, whose soft bone bows, and osteomalacia in the adult, whose bone aches and cracks. In osteogenesis imperfecta the collagen is faulty, so bone is brittle and shatters. In osteoporosis both are lost together — what remains is normal bone, there is simply too little of it, which is why a hip gives way under an ordinary step.

How a fracture heals

A fracture heals in four overlapping stages, and each lays the scaffold for the next.

First a hematoma forms as torn vessels bleed into the break; the clot seals the gap while inflammatory cells clear debris, and the bone ends nearest the fracture die from lost blood supply. Next fibroblasts and chondroblasts build a fibrocartilaginous (soft) callus that splints the fragments within a week or two. Osteoblasts then convert it to a bony (hard) callus of spongy bone over the following weeks. Finally, months of remodeling replace that spongy bone with compact bone aligned to the loads the limb actually carries.

Every stage depends on that first bleed, which is why fractures in poorly perfused bone — the scaphoid, the femoral neck — are the ones you will see go to non-union.

Three muscles, three design briefs

Skeletal muscle is voluntary, striated, and multinucleated because its fibers fuse during development. Cardiac muscle is involuntary, striated, and packed with mitochondria — which is exactly why it is the most fatigue-resistant tissue you have, and why it never gets to rest. Smooth muscle is involuntary and non-striated, contracts slowly, and holds tone for long periods in vessels and hollow organs. Only smooth muscle regenerates well; skeletal and cardiac heal with scar, which is why a heart attack leaves permanent damage.

From nerve impulse to movement

The sequence is worth learning as a chain, because each link is a drug target. An action potential travels down the motor neuron. Calcium enters the terminal. That calcium makes vesicles fuse and release acetylcholine. ACh crosses the cleft and binds receptors on the muscle membrane, sodium enters, and the muscle depolarizes. Acetylcholinesterase then breaks the ACh down — without that off-switch the muscle would never stop.

Inside the fiber, the impulse runs down T-tubules so the whole cell contracts together, calcium is released from the sarcoplasmic reticulum, it binds troponin, tropomyosin shifts off the binding sites, and myosin grabs actin.

ATP is needed to let go, not to grab. That is why rigor mortis happens: no ATP, no release.

Where the energy comes from

Three systems, in order of speed. Creatine phosphate regenerates ATP instantly but lasts seconds. Anaerobic glycolysis runs in the cytoplasm without oxygen, yields only about 2 ATP, and converts pyruvate to lactic acid — fast, but it burns and it fades. Aerobic respiration runs in the mitochondria, needs oxygen, and yields about 30–32 ATP. Sprint versus marathon, in one paragraph.

Neurons signal; glia keep them alive

Only neurons transmit. Everything else supports them. Astrocytes maintain the blood–brain barrier. Microglia are the immune cells of the CNS. Ependymal cells make cerebrospinal fluid. Oligodendrocytes myelinate in the CNS and Schwann cells myelinate in the PNS — that split is worth memorizing because it decides which diseases hit where.

Myelin insulates the axon so the impulse jumps between gaps rather than crawling along, which is enormously faster. Strip the myelin, as multiple sclerosis does, and conduction slows or fails.

How neurons are classified twice

Neurons get classified twice, and the two systems tangle easily. The first question is structural — how many processes leave the cell body. Multipolar neurons have one axon and many dendrites, and they are nearly every motor neuron and interneuron you own. Bipolar neurons have one of each and are rare — retina and olfactory epithelium. Unipolar neurons, more precisely pseudounipolar, have one process that splits in two, and they carry sensory information inward. Anaxonic neurons have no distinguishable axon.

The second question is functional — direction of travel. Sensory (afferent) neurons run toward the CNS, motor (efferent) neurons run away from it, and interneurons stay inside. Interneurons are roughly ninety-nine percent of the total — which is why most of your nervous system is busy talking to itself, not to the world.

Crossing the synapse, and switching it off

An action potential cannot jump a gap. At the axon terminal, depolarization opens voltage-gated calcium channels; calcium rushing in drives vesicles to fuse with the membrane, and neurotransmitter crosses the synaptic cleft to bind receptors on the next cell.

Excitatory transmitters (glutamate, acetylcholine at the neuromuscular junction) let sodium in and push that cell toward threshold; inhibitory ones (GABA, glycine) let chloride in and push it away. No single synapse decides anything — the cell sums inputs arriving together and in quick succession, and fires only if the total reaches threshold. The signal must then be cleared: acetylcholinesterase splits ACh in the cleft, while norepinephrine, dopamine and serotonin are pumped back into the terminal — which is why a reuptake blocker leaves transmitter in place longer rather than making more of it.

Myelin, and who makes it where

Schwann cells myelinate the PNS, one cell wrapping one segment of one axon. Oligodendrocytes myelinate the CNS, and a single one reaches as many as fifty axons. That difference matters: the Schwann cell also leaves an outer sleeve that guides a severed axon back, so peripheral nerves can regrow where central ones cannot.

Between the wrapped segments sit bare gaps, the nodes of Ranvier, packed with voltage-gated sodium channels. The impulse regenerates only at the nodes and jumps between them — saltatory conduction — so a myelinated fiber far outpaces a bare one of the same diameter. Strip the myelin — multiple sclerosis does it centrally, Guillain-Barré syndrome peripherally — and conduction slows or fails, which is why these patients lose strength, sensation and coordination as signals arrive late or not at all.

Why rigor mortis is not contraction

ATP has two jobs at the cross-bridge, and the second one is the surprise. Myosin splits ATP to cock its head into a high-energy position. When calcium binds troponin and tropomyosin slides off the binding sites on actin, the head attaches and swings — the power stroke — dragging the thin filament inward. The head then stays locked to actin until a fresh ATP binds and prises it off. Detachment costs ATP, and so does relaxation, because calcium has to be pumped back into the sarcoplasmic reticulum against its gradient.

So when ATP production stops at death, calcium leaks out and cannot be pumped back, every attached head stays attached, and the muscle stiffens in place. Rigor mortis is not contraction — it is the absence of the ATP required to stop contracting.

Fast ATP is not more ATP

Anaerobic pathways are fast, not productive, and those two words get swapped. Anaerobic glycolysis yields two ATP per glucose and arrives in seconds; aerobic respiration yields roughly thirty-two from the same glucose but needs oxygen and minutes to reach full rate. Speed is what the sprint buys, at about a sixteenth of the yield.

The cost shows up as lactate, made when pyruvate has nowhere aerobic to go and later rebuilt into glucose by the liver, and as the oxygen debt you repay by breathing hard after the effort stops — restoring creatine phosphate, clearing lactate, refilling myoglobin. The same arithmetic explains rising lactate in a patient in shock: those cells are not working harder, they are working without oxygen, and two ATP per glucose will not keep a body alive.

The action potential, step by step

At rest the inside of a neuron sits at about −70 mV, held there by the sodium–potassium pump. A stimulus nudges it upward. Nothing happens until it reaches threshold at about −55 mV — and then everything happens at once, identically, every time. That is what "all-or-none" means.

At threshold, voltage-gated sodium channels fly open and Na⁺ rushes in, driving the inside to about +30 mV: depolarization. Those channels then inactivate and potassium channels open, so K⁺ moves out and the inside falls negative again: repolarization. Potassium is slow to close, so it overshoots to about −90 mV: hyperpolarization. The pump then restores the resting state.

Sodium in, potassium out. If you remember nothing else from this module, remember that.

Protecting and supplying the brain

Three membranes cover the brain — dura (tough, outer), arachnoid (web-like, middle), and pia (delicate, hugging the surface). Cerebrospinal fluid circulates in the subarachnoid space, cushioning the brain and making it effectively buoyant so it does not crush under its own weight.

Gray matter is cell bodies and does the processing. White matter is myelinated axons and does the transmitting — it looks white because myelin is fatty.

Who does what in the brain

Frontal lobe: decisions, planning, personality, voluntary movement. Parietal: touch, pressure, pain, temperature. Temporal: hearing, language comprehension, memory. Occipital: vision. Cerebellum: coordination and balance — damage causes clumsiness, not paralysis. Brainstem: breathing, heart rate, blood pressure, which is why brainstem injury is so often fatal. Thalamus: the relay station nearly all sensory input passes through. Hypothalamus: hunger, thirst, temperature, and the bridge to the endocrine system.

Two settings for the body

The sympathetic division is fight-or-flight: heart rate up, blood pressure up, airways open, pupils dilate, digestion suspended. The parasympathetic division is rest-and-digest and does the reverse.

Neither is good or bad. They are two settings, and most organs receive both. Losing parasympathetic tone does not make you calm — it leaves you tachycardic.

Smell and taste

Both need the chemical to dissolve first. In olfaction, dissolved odorants bind receptors on the olfactory epithelium, bipolar neurons fire, and the signal reaches the olfactory bulb and then the cortex — bypassing the thalamus entirely, the only sense that does. That direct line to the limbic system is why a smell can drop you into a memory.

Taste has five types: sweet, sour, salty, bitter and umami. Spicy is not among them — capsaicin triggers pain and heat receptors, not taste buds.

How a neuron decides to fire

Before any action potential exists, the neuron is adding up. Neurotransmitters open channels that produce graded potentials — small local voltage changes that fade with distance. An EPSP nudges the membrane toward threshold; an IPSP pushes it away. They combine by temporal summation when one synapse fires rapidly in succession, and by spatial summation when several synapses fire at once.

The running total arrives at the axon hillock, the trigger zone, where voltage-gated sodium channels sit densest. Reach threshold — about minus 55 millivolts — and an action potential fires at full size. Fall short and nothing happens at all. That is the all-or-none rule, which is why a stronger stimulus never makes a bigger impulse; it makes more per second, and frequency is how intensity is encoded.

Refractory periods, and why signals go one way

Nothing physically stops an impulse spreading backwards along the axon except time. During the absolute refractory period the sodium channels behind the impulse are inactivated rather than simply closed, and no stimulus of any strength will reopen them until the membrane repolarizes. The membrane just travelled through is therefore deaf, so the signal can only move forward into resting membrane.

The relative refractory period follows, while the membrane is still repolarizing and often hyperpolarized past resting because potassium is still leaving. Some sodium channels have reset, so firing is possible but needs a stronger stimulus than usual. Keep the directions straight: sodium in depolarizes, potassium out repolarizes. The absolute period also sets a ceiling on how fast a neuron can fire, which is why frequency coding cannot rise without limit.

Myelin, and what demyelination costs

Two things set conduction speed. The first is axon diameter — a wider axon offers less resistance to current, the way a wide pipe moves more water. The second, and far more powerful, is myelin: fatty insulation laid down by oligodendrocytes in the central nervous system and Schwann cells in the peripheral nervous system, which stops charge leaking out through the membrane.

Voltage-gated sodium channels cluster in the bare gaps between wraps, the nodes of Ranvier, so the impulse is regenerated only at the nodes and effectively jumps between them. That is saltatory conduction. Strip that myelin, as multiple sclerosis does in the central nervous system, and current bleeds away between nodes, so conduction slows or fails outright — which is why the symptoms are weakness, numbness and visual loss.

Gray matter, white matter, and why the pattern flips

Gray matter is cell bodies, dendrites and synapses, so it is where processing happens. White matter is myelinated axons, white because of the myelin, so it is where information travels. In the cerebrum and cerebellum the gray sits outside as cortex with white beneath, but in the spinal cord it inverts — gray in a central butterfly, white tracts wrapped around it.

White matter runs three ways: association fibers within one hemisphere, commissural fibers crossing between the hemispheres, mostly through the corpus callosum, and projection fibers running between cortex and cord. Deep gray includes the basal nuclei and the thalamus, which relays every sensory pathway to the cortex except olfaction. Smell reaches cortex directly, which is why an odor can summon a memory before you can name it.

The autonomic system at the receptor level

Both divisions use a two-neuron chain, and in both the preganglionic neuron releases acetylcholine onto nicotinic receptors in a ganglion — that much is identical. The difference comes afterwards. Parasympathetic fibers are craniosacral, with long preganglionic and short postganglionic neurons, ending in acetylcholine on muscarinic receptors. Sympathetic fibers are thoracolumbar, short then long, ending in norepinephrine on alpha and beta adrenergic receptors, with sweat glands the exception, taking acetylcholine on muscarinic receptors.

The adrenal medulla is a modified sympathetic ganglion whose cells have no axons, so instead of targeting one organ they pour epinephrine into the blood. Neither division is good or bad; both run constantly as autonomic tone, which is why blocking either is a drug strategy — beta blockers for a racing heart, atropine for a dangerously slow one.

RepolarizationK⁺ moves OUT+30 → −70 mV

How the eye focuses

The cornea does most of the bending of light; the lens does the fine adjustment. For a near object the ciliary muscle contracts and the lens rounds up, bending light more. For a distant object it flattens. This is accommodation, and it is the reverse of what most students first guess.

As the lens stiffens with age it can no longer round up, which is presbyopia — the reason reading glasses arrive in middle age.

Turning light into a signal

The retina has two photoreceptors with opposite specialities. Rods are extremely light-sensitive but give no color and poor detail, and sit in the periphery — they are your night vision. Cones need bright light but give color and sharp detail, and cluster at the fovea, which is why you turn your eyes toward whatever you want to see clearly.

Light strikes opsin, which changes shape and starts a cascade. The signal passes photoreceptor → bipolar cell → ganglion cell, and ganglion cell axons leave the eye as the optic nerve. Where they exit there are no photoreceptors at all — your blind spot.

Hearing is a chain of energy conversions

Sound starts as pressure waves in air. The pinna collects them. The tympanic membrane converts them into mechanical vibration. The three ossicles amplify that vibration and drive the oval window. That moves fluid inside the cochlea, which bends hair cells, and there the mechanical becomes electrical. Cranial nerve VIII carries it to the temporal lobe.

Break the chain early — wax, a perforated drum, stiff ossicles — and you get conductive loss. Break it at the hair cells or nerve and you get sensorineural loss.

Balance: two different questions

The inner ear answers two separate questions with two separate structures. Am I tilted, or accelerating in a straight line? is answered by the utricle and saccule, where calcium crystals called otoliths sit on a gel and drag on hair cells when gravity or acceleration pulls them.

Am I rotating? is answered by the three semicircular canals, set at right angles to each other. When your head turns, the fluid inside lags behind and bends hair cells in the ampulla. That lag is also why you still feel like you are spinning for a few seconds after you stop.

Where the image goes after the retina

Light becomes a signal in the retina, but you do not see anything until the occipital lobe assembles it. Axons of the ganglion cells leave the eye as the optic nerve. At the optic chiasm only the fibers from the nasal half of each retina cross, while the temporal fibers stay on their own side, so each optic tract carries the opposite half of the visual world rather than the opposite eye.

The signal then relays in the lateral geniculate nucleus of the thalamus and travels through the optic radiations to the primary visual cortex. Damage before the chiasm blinds one eye; damage at the chiasm — classically a pituitary tumor — removes both outer fields, which is why such a patient clips door frames on both sides.

Color, detail, and seeing in the dark

Where photoreceptors sit matters as much as how they work. Cones come in three kinds, tuned to short, medium and long wavelengths, and your brain reads color from the ratio of their firing, which is why losing one type distorts color rather than removing it. Cones crowd the fovea centralis, each wired almost one to one, giving fine detail but only in good light.

Rods pack the periphery and funnel many receptors onto one pathway, summing faint light into a usable signal at the cost of detail. Their rhodopsin bleaches the moment light hits it and takes twenty to thirty minutes to rebuild — which is why a dark room looks black when you first walk in, and why a faint star is easier to see just off to one side.

When the image lands in the wrong place

A normal eye focuses light on the retina itself. In myopia the eyeball is too long, so the image forms in front of the retina and distance blurs — a diverging concave lens pushes it back. In hyperopia the eyeball is too short, so the image forms behind the retina and near work blurs — a converging convex lens pulls it forward. Astigmatism is uneven corneal curvature, so nothing focuses sharply at any distance.

Presbyopia is different: the ageing lens stiffens and cannot round up for near vision, which is why reading glasses arrive in your forties. Acuity compares you with a normal eye: 20/200 means you read at twenty feet what a normal eye reads at two hundred, so a bigger bottom number is worse vision, not better.

How the cochlea codes pitch and loudness

Pitch and volume are carried by two different features of the same wave. The basilar membrane is narrow and stiff at the base and wide and floppy at the apex, so high frequencies peak near the base and low frequencies near the apex. Whichever region vibrates most bends the stereocilia of its hair cells against the tectorial membrane, and the temporal lobe reads pitch from which fibers of cranial nerve VIII fired. Loudness is those cells bending further and firing faster.

That anatomy also sorts hearing loss. Conductive loss blocks the chain before the receptors — wax, middle ear fluid, a fixed ossicle — so clearing it restores hearing. Sensorineural loss is dead hair cells or nerve, from noise, ageing or ototoxic drugs, and because hair cells never regrow it is permanent.

What the vestibular receptors can and cannot feel

Every vestibular receptor is a hair cell; what sits on top decides what it detects. In the three semicircular canals — one per plane of space — hair cells of the crista ampullaris project into a gel cupula, and only moving fluid bends it. When your head starts or stops rotating the endolymph lags behind and deflects the cupula, which makes these canals detectors of angular acceleration and nothing else.

Gravity and linear acceleration are the job of the utricle and saccule, whose maculae are capped by otoliths — crystals heavy enough to sag when you tilt or pull away in a car. Stop spinning and the endolymph keeps moving, so your canals still report turning while your eyes report stillness — which is why the room seems to spin.

Receptors decide the speed of everything

A hormone can only do what its receptor allows, and receptor type sets the timescale.

Ion channel-linked receptors are channels — binding opens them and ions flow immediately. This is the fastest option and it is what the neuromuscular junction uses. G-protein coupled receptors sit in the membrane and trigger second messengers inside, which amplifies enormously: one hormone molecule can produce thousands of downstream events. Enzyme-linked receptors activate intracellular enzymes.

Intracellular receptors are the outlier. Lipid-soluble hormones — steroids and thyroid hormone — cross the membrane and bind receptors inside, and the complex alters gene transcription. That takes hours, but the effect lasts. Water-soluble hormones cannot cross, so they must work from the surface. That single distinction predicts almost everything about how a hormone behaves.

Feedback is the whole subject

Nearly every endocrine axis is a negative feedback loop with three levels: hypothalamus → pituitary → target gland. The hypothalamus releases a releasing hormone, the anterior pituitary releases a tropic hormone, the target gland releases the final hormone — and that final hormone shuts off the two above it.

This is why lab interpretation works. High T4 with low TSH means the thyroid is overproducing and correctly suppressing the pituitary — primary hyperthyroidism. Students who expect both to move together get these questions wrong every time.

The hormones worth knowing cold

Group them by what they defend. Glucose: insulin lowers it (beta cells), glucagon raises it (alpha cells). Calcium: PTH raises, calcitonin lowers. Water and salt: ADH keeps water, aldosterone keeps sodium. Metabolism: T3 and T4. Stress: cortisol and epinephrine. Reproduction: FSH grows the follicle, LH triggers ovulation — the pair most often confused.

For each, learn the trigger and the effect as a sentence, not as two separate facts.

When the axis breaks

Endocrine disease is nearly always too much or too little of one hormone, and the clinical picture is just that hormone's normal job turned up or down.

Too much cortisol gives Cushing: high glucose, central weight gain, thin skin, weak bone — cortisol's ordinary effects, exaggerated. Too little gives Addison: fatigue, low blood pressure, low sodium, high potassium. Too much thyroid hormone speeds everything — weight loss, heat intolerance, fast heart. Too little slows everything — weight gain, cold intolerance, slow heart.

This is why you rarely need to memorize symptom lists. Work out what the hormone does, then push it in either direction.

Why steroids must be tapered

Give someone cortisol from outside for long enough and their own axis notices. Negative feedback shuts down CRH and ACTH, and the adrenal cortex, unused, shrinks.

Stop the drug suddenly and there is now no cortisol from either source — the tablets have gone and the gland cannot restart quickly. That is an adrenal crisis: hypotension, shock, potentially death. Tapering gives the axis weeks to wake back up. It is negative feedback with real consequences.

The three signals that make a gland fire

Glands do not secrete at random — something has to tell them to. There are only three kinds of trigger, and knowing which one a hormone uses tells you how fast it responds.

Humoral stimuli are changes in a blood value the gland itself measures. Parathyroid cells read falling calcium and release PTH; beta cells read rising glucose and release insulin. Neural stimuli are direct nerve input — sympathetic fibers firing onto the adrenal medulla dump epinephrine into the blood within seconds. Hormonal stimuli are one hormone triggering another, which is the whole hypothalamic-pituitary pattern: a releasing hormone tells the pituitary, the pituitary tells the target gland.

That layering is why your heart races almost instantly in a fright but cortisol, which travels the long hormonal route, takes many minutes to rise.

Sensory receptors, sorted by what they detect

The same word, receptor, covers two different things, and the exam expects both. Hormone receptors are proteins that bind a chemical messenger. Sensory receptors are structures that convert a stimulus into an electrical signal your nervous system can read, and they are classified by what they detect.

Mechanoreceptors respond to physical deformation — touch, stretch, the baroreceptors reading blood pressure, proprioceptors reporting joint position, and the hair cells of hearing and balance. Thermoreceptors detect temperature, nociceptors detect tissue damage as pain, chemoreceptors detect chemicals including taste, smell, blood gases and the osmoreceptors watching blood concentration, and photoreceptors detect light.

Most adapt — you stop noticing your watch within minutes. Nociceptors barely adapt at all, which is why pain keeps shouting until you deal with the cause.

How a hormone travels sets how long it lasts

A hormone reaches every cell, but how it travels depends on whether it dissolves in water or in fat.

Water-soluble hormones — the peptides and the catecholamines — dissolve straight into plasma, cannot cross the fatty cell membrane, and must bind surface receptors. Enzymes, the liver and the kidneys clear them within minutes. Lipid-soluble hormones — the steroids from the adrenal cortex and gonads, plus thyroid hormone — barely dissolve in plasma at all, so they travel bound to carrier proteins with only the small free fraction active. Being fat-soluble, they enter the cell and bind intracellular receptors, changing gene transcription directly.

Bound hormone is a reservoir, so steroid and thyroid half-lives run hours to days — which is why levothyroxine is a single daily tablet while insulin must be redosed or infused.

Why the same hormone level stops working

A hormone circulates everywhere, but only cells with the matching receptor respond — the receptor, not the hormone, defines the target tissue.

Cells adjust that sensitivity. Persistently high hormone levels cause down-regulation: the cell withdraws receptors, so the same level produces less effect. Scarce hormone causes up-regulation, making the cell more sensitive. Type 2 diabetes is that pattern — insulin is present, but the tissues answer weakly, through fewer receptors and faulty internal signaling.

Hormones interact three ways. Permissive means one hormone must be present for another to work fully — epinephrine needs thyroid hormone. Synergistic means two push the same way and amplify each other beyond a simple sum — glucagon, cortisol and epinephrine on blood glucose. Antagonistic means opposition — insulin against glucagon. That is why an untreated hypothyroid patient handles stress badly.

One pituitary, two completely different glands

The pituitary looks like one gland and behaves like two.

The anterior pituitary is true glandular tissue. It manufactures six hormones — GH, TSH, ACTH, FSH, LH and prolactin — controlled by releasing and inhibiting hormones arriving from the hypothalamus down a short private set of portal veins. FSH and LH are not interchangeable: FSH grows ovarian follicles and supports sperm production, while LH triggers ovulation and stimulates testosterone. TSH is pituitary output, not thyroid output — so a low TSH usually means the pituitary stopped shouting because thyroid hormone is already high.

The posterior pituitary manufactures nothing. ADH and oxytocin are made by hypothalamic neurons and stored in their nerve endings — which is why stalk damage can cut ADH off from the bloodstream and cause diabetes insipidus even with the lobe intact.

What blood is for

Blood transports, regulates and protects. It carries oxygen, nutrients, hormones and waste; it regulates temperature by shifting where blood flows and helps hold pH steady; and it protects through immunity and clotting.

It is about 55% plasma and 45% cells. The main plasma protein is albumin, and its job is osmotic: it holds fluid inside the vessel. Lose albumin and fluid leaks into tissue — edema.

The cells, and what each one is for

Erythrocytes carry oxygen on hemoglobin. They lose their nucleus to make room, which maximizes capacity but caps their lifespan at about 120 days.

Leukocytes defend, and each has a speciality. Neutrophils are the most numerous and arrive first at bacterial infection. Eosinophils handle parasites and allergy. Basophils release histamine and drive inflammation. Lymphocytes run adaptive immunity. Monocytes leave the blood and become tissue macrophages.

Platelets form the initial plug and provide the surface on which clotting happens.

Vessels and pressure

Every vessel has three layers, but the middle one does the work: the tunica media is smooth muscle, and its contraction or relaxation sets vessel diameter. Narrow the vessel and resistance rises, so pressure rises. Widen it and pressure falls.

Arterioles matter most here — they have the most muscle relative to their size, so they are the main resistance vessels and the main determinant of blood pressure. Capillaries are one cell thick for exchange. Veins are low-pressure and need valves plus skeletal muscle compression to push blood back uphill.

Stopping bleeding, in three steps

One: vascular spasm. The vessel constricts immediately and cuts flow.
Two: platelet plug. Von Willebrand factor bridges platelets onto exposed collagen and they aggregate into a temporary seal.
Three: coagulation. A cascade converts fibrinogen into fibrin, weaving a mesh that traps cells and turns the soft plug into a real clot.

The cascade has two entrances — the intrinsic pathway from damage inside the vessel and the extrinsic pathway from tissue injury outside — and they converge at Factor X. From there it is one road: Factor X → prothrombin becomes thrombin → thrombin converts fibrinogen to fibrin. Later, plasmin dissolves the clot once the vessel has healed.

What plasma proteins actually do

Blood is roughly 55 percent plasma, and plasma is about 92 percent water — but the small protein fraction, about 7 percent, does most of the specialized work. Albumin is the most abundant, and it generates the colloid osmotic pressure that pulls fluid back into the capillary at its venous end; it also ferries fatty acids, calcium and many drugs.

Globulins come in three families: alpha and beta transport iron, lipids and hormones, while gamma globulins are your antibodies. Fibrinogen is the soluble precursor that thrombin converts into fibrin. All of these except the gamma globulins, which come from plasma cells, are made in the liver — which is why liver failure or severe malnutrition produces edema and easy bleeding at the same time.

The life and death of a red cell

A red cell has no nucleus and no mitochondria — it gave both up for hemoglobin, and that trade leaves it unable to repair itself. So it survives roughly 120 days, then macrophages in the spleen and liver take it apart.

Nothing is wasted. Globin returns to the amino acid pool, iron is carried back on transferrin or stored as ferritin, and the leftover heme ring becomes biliverdin and then bilirubin, which the liver conjugates and excretes in bile. Unconjugated bilirubin is not water soluble and cannot leave in urine, so a failing liver or blocked duct backs it up and yellows skin and sclerae. Replacement is driven by erythropoietin from the kidney whenever oxygen falls — which is why chronic kidney disease causes an anemia that iron alone cannot fix.

Two pathways into one cascade

Vascular spasm comes first and the platelet plug second — coagulation is the third step, and it is what turns a fragile plug into a clot that holds. It has two entry points. The extrinsic pathway starts outside the vessel, when damaged tissue exposes tissue factor, and it works in seconds. The intrinsic pathway starts inside the vessel with contact activation and takes minutes.

Both converge on factor X, the head of the common pathway: activated X builds prothrombinase, which converts prothrombin into thrombin, and thrombin turns soluble fibrinogen into fibrin threads that factor XIII cross-links into a mesh. Factors II, VII, IX and X depend on vitamin K — which is exactly the step warfarin blocks, and why those patients have an INR checked rather than a bleeding time.

Why blood does not clot everywhere

Blood sits against the vessel wall constantly and does not clot, and that takes active work. Intact endothelium is smooth and releases nitric oxide and prostacyclin, which keep platelets from sticking. Circulating antithrombin III inactivates thrombin, heparin from basophils and mast cells accelerates that reaction, and protein C with protein S switches off the amplifying factors.

Once repair is underway, plasminogen trapped inside the clot is converted to plasmin by tissue plasminogen activator, and plasmin digests the fibrin mesh away — fibrinolysis. Clotting is therefore a balance, not a switch, which is why stasis, vessel injury and hypercoagulability — Virchow's triad — can tip a healthy person into a deep vein thrombosis after nothing more dramatic than a long flight.

How blood pressure is regulated

Mean arterial pressure is cardiac output multiplied by systemic vascular resistance. Both can change within seconds, but resistance gives the finest control, and it lives in the arterioles, where slight narrowing raises pressure sharply. That word systemic matters: an artery is named for its direction of flow, not its oxygen, and the pulmonary arteries carry deoxygenated blood through a much lower-pressure circuit.

Baroreceptors in the carotid sinus and aortic arch report stretch to the medulla; when pressure falls they fire less, sympathetic outflow rises, vessels constrict and heart rate climbs. Tissues overrule that order locally — carbon dioxide, potassium and low oxygen dilate their own arterioles. Hormones work over hours instead: RAAS, ADH and ANP adjust volume, which is why an older patient whose baroreflex is blunted by antihypertensives faints on standing.

The heart as a pump

Four chambers, two circuits. The right side receives deoxygenated blood from the body and sends it to the lungs — the pulmonary circuit. The left side receives oxygenated blood from the lungs and pushes it to the body — the systemic circuit. The left ventricle has by far the thickest wall because it has by far the longest push.

Valves make flow one-way. The AV valves (tricuspid right, mitral left) open during diastole to let the ventricles fill and slam shut when the ventricles contract — that closure is S1, the "lub". The semilunar valves (pulmonary and aortic) open during ejection and snap shut as the ventricles relax — that is S2, the "dub". The two heart sounds are simply two sets of valves closing.

Cardiac output, and the two ways to change it

Cardiac output = heart rate × stroke volume, around 5 liters a minute at rest. Anything that changes output must change one of those two numbers.

Heart rate is set by autonomic tone — sympathetic speeds it, vagal parasympathetic slows it. Stroke volume depends on preload (how full the ventricle got, which depends on venous return), contractility (how hard it squeezes), and afterload (the pressure it must push against). Stretch a ventricle more and it contracts harder — the Frank–Starling relationship — which is how the heart automatically matches output to return.

The heart as an electrical system

The SA node depolarizes spontaneously and fastest, so it sets the rhythm. The impulse spreads across the atria, then reaches the AV node, which deliberately delays it — that pause is what lets the atria finish emptying before the ventricles contract. From there it runs down the Bundle of His, splits into bundle branches, and spreads through the Purkinje fibers so the ventricles contract as one coordinated unit from the apex upward.

On an ECG: P is atrial depolarization, QRS is ventricular depolarization, and T is ventricular repolarization. Atrial repolarization does happen — it is simply buried inside the much larger QRS.

Preload, afterload, contractility

Three words govern stroke volume, and they are easy to keep straight if you picture the ventricle.

Preload is how full it got before it squeezed — it depends on venous return. More filling stretches the fibers, and a stretched ventricle contracts harder. That is the Frank–Starling relationship, and it is how the heart matches output to whatever comes back without needing instructions.

Contractility is how hard it squeezes at a given stretch — sympathetic stimulation raises it. Afterload is the pressure it must overcome to open the aortic valve and eject. Raise afterload, as chronic hypertension does, and the ventricle must work harder for every beat — which is why untreated hypertension eventually thickens and then fails the left ventricle.

Why the heart cannot cramp

Skeletal muscle can be stimulated repeatedly until the twitches fuse into one sustained contraction — tetanus. If the heart could do that, it would seize and never refill, and you would die.

It cannot, because cardiac muscle has an unusually long refractory period. The plateau phase — sustained by calcium entering while potassium leaves — keeps the cell unresponsive until the contraction is over and relaxation has begun. The heart is physically incapable of a second beat before the first has finished. That constraint is a safety feature.

The two heart sounds, and which valves make them

Put a stethoscope on a chest and you hear lub-dub: two sounds, four valves.

The first sound, S1, is the atrioventricular valves slamming shut — the tricuspid on the right, the mitral on the left. They close the moment ventricular pressure rises above atrial pressure, so S1 marks the start of systole. The second sound, S2, is the semilunar valves — pulmonary and aortic — snapping shut as the ventricles relax and arterial pressure pushes blood back toward them, marking the start of diastole.

No valve is pulled open or pushed shut by muscle — every one moves on a pressure difference alone, and the papillary muscles only anchor the AV valves so they cannot turn inside out. Which is why a stiff or leaking valve gives the turbulent flow you hear as a murmur.

Reading the ECG wave by wave

An ECG records electrical events, not contractions — each wave is a voltage change that a mechanical event follows.

The P wave is atrial depolarization. The flat PR segment after it is the signal being held at the AV node, giving the atria time to finish emptying. The QRS complex is ventricular depolarization — large, because the ventricles carry far more muscle. Atrial repolarization happens at the same moment, buried inside the QRS, which is why you never see a wave for it. The ST segment should sit flat during contraction, and the T wave is ventricular repolarization.

That flat segment matters clinically: starved of oxygen, myocardium shifts it — depressed in ischemia, elevated when muscle is injuring and infarcting — which is how a blocked coronary announces itself on the monitor.

Why the heart beats itself

A heart removed from the body keeps beating, and a transplanted heart beats before any nerve reaches it. That is because pacemaker cells have no stable resting potential — sodium leaks inward through funny channels until the cell drifts to threshold and fires on its own. That drift is the heartbeat.

Every part of the conduction system can do this, at different speeds. The SA node drifts fastest — around 100 a minute on its own, 60 to 100 with vagal braking — so it sets the rhythm; the AV node manages 40 to 60, and the bundle of His, bundle branches and Purkinje fibers only 20 to 40.

The fastest pacemaker wins. Which is why, if the SA node fails, a slower site takes over — the patient goes bradycardic, but stays alive.

Why your resting pulse is not 100

If the SA node fires at around 100 on its own, why is your resting pulse nearer 70? Because the vagus nerve brakes it continuously. That standing parasympathetic traffic — vagal tone — releases acetylcholine onto the node, slows the drift to threshold and holds you below your own intrinsic rate.

Sympathetic fibers release norepinephrine, and the adrenal medulla adds circulating epinephrine — both doing the opposite, through three effects: chronotropy (rate at the SA node), dromotropy (conduction speed at the AV node) and inotropy (force of contraction). Parasympathetic supply reaches the nodes but barely touches ventricular muscle, so the vagus changes rate far more than force.

When you stand and your pulse jumps, the first thing that happened was vagal withdrawal — releasing the brake is quicker than pressing the accelerator.

The heart feeds itself during diastole

The heart sits in a chamber full of blood and cannot use a drop of it. Myocardium is fed by the coronary arteries, which branch off the aorta just above the aortic valve — the first vessels off the pump.

Here is the catch. During systole the contracting left ventricle squeezes its own vessels almost shut, so the coronaries fill during diastole, when the muscle is relaxed and the closed aortic valve holds a column of pressurized blood above them. Left-heart perfusion is a diastolic event.

Now think about tachycardia. As rate climbs, diastole shortens far more than systole — a racing heart demands more oxygen while giving itself less time to collect any. Which is why chest pain appears on exertion, and why slowing the rate is so often the treatment.
