Two immune systems, working together

Innate immunity is what you are born with: fast, non-specific, no memory. Skin and mucous membranes, neutrophils and macrophages, NK cells, complement, inflammation and fever. It responds in minutes but treats every intruder the same.

Adaptive immunity is specific and remembers. It takes days on first exposure, but the second time it is dramatically faster — which is the entire principle behind vaccination.

NK cells are the confusing case: they are lymphocytes, which feels adaptive, but they attack without prior exposure, so they are innate.

Who does what in the adaptive response

Helper T cells (CD4) coordinate. They activate B cells and cytotoxic T cells and release cytokines — which is precisely why HIV, by destroying them, disables both arms at once.

B cells recognize antigen and become plasma cells, which mass-produce antibody. Cytotoxic T cells (CD8) kill infected cells directly. Memory cells of both kinds persist afterwards, and are the reason the second exposure is so much easier.

The lymphatic system does two jobs

First, immunity: lymph nodes filter lymph and hold lymphocytes and macrophages, the spleen filters blood and clears old red cells, the thymus matures T cells, and MALT guards mucosal surfaces.

Second, and easy to forget: fluid balance. Capillaries leak fluid into tissue continuously. The lymphatics collect that excess and return it to the bloodstream at the subclavian veins. Block the drainage and the fluid has nowhere to go — that is lymphedema.

Antibodies, and which one is where

All five immunoglobulins do the same basic thing — bind antigen — but each is deployed differently.

IgM is produced first in a new infection, so finding it suggests a current or recent one. IgG is the most abundant, arrives later, and is the only one that crosses the placenta — which is how a newborn borrows its mother's immunity for a few months. IgA guards mucosal surfaces and is in breast milk. IgE binds mast cells and drives allergy and parasite defense. IgD sits on B cells as a receptor.

If a question mentions a newborn, think IgG. Mucosa or milk, think IgA. Allergy, think IgE.

Active and passive immunity

Active immunity means you made the response yourself — through infection or vaccination. It is slow to establish but leaves memory, so it lasts.

Passive immunity means someone else's antibodies were handed to you — across the placenta, in breast milk, or as an injection. Protection is immediate, which is exactly why it is used in emergencies, but it fades within weeks to months and leaves no memory, because your own B cells were never involved.

Immediate but temporary, or slow but lasting. That trade-off is the whole distinction.

The complement system, all three pathways

Complement is a set of plasma proteins that circulate inactive until one of three pathways switches them on. The classical pathway starts when C1 binds an antibody already stuck to an antigen. The lectin pathway starts when mannose-binding lectin recognizes sugars on microbes but not on yours. The alternative pathway starts by itself, when activated C3 lands on a foreign surface lacking your regulatory proteins.

All three converge on one cascade with three outcomes. C3b coats the microbe so phagocytes can grip it — opsonization. C3a and C5a drive inflammation, and C5a especially pulls neutrophils in. C5b through C9 assemble the membrane attack complex, a pore that lyses the cell. Which is why people missing late complement proteins get repeated Neisseria infections — antibody alone cannot drill that hole.

The innate arsenal, layer by layer

Innate defense is layered, and a microbe meets those layers in order. The barriers come first — intact skin, mucus and cilia sweeping the airway, stomach acid, lysozyme in tears and saliva, and normal flora holding the space.

Break through those and cells respond. Neutrophils arrive first and phagocytose. Macrophages engulf and present what they find. Dendritic cells carry antigen to the lymph nodes, where adaptive immunity begins. Eosinophils attack parasites, while basophils and mast cells release histamine. Natural killer cells are lymphocytes, but innate ones — they kill any cell that has stopped displaying normal self markers, without ever having met it before. Interferons, complement, inflammation and fever run alongside. None of it requires prior exposure, which is why it works in minutes rather than days.

MHC, and why CD4 and CD8 see different things

A T cell cannot see free antigen — only fragments displayed on a major histocompatibility complex molecule — and the two classes explain the division of labor. MHC class I sits on every nucleated cell, displaying proteins made inside it. If a virus replicates there, its fragments appear on the surface, and CD8 cytotoxic T cells read class I and kill the cell outright. MHC class II sits only on antigen-presenting cells — dendritic cells, macrophages, B cells — displaying material swallowed from outside. CD4 helper T cells read class II and release cytokines that activate B cells, cytotoxic T cells and macrophages.

Which is why HIV is so devastating: it destroys CD4 cells, and without the coordinator, otherwise healthy B cells and CD8 cells never get switched on.

Why the second exposure is different

The first time you meet an antigen the response is slow — several days while the few lymphocytes that happen to fit divide into a clone. Antibody appears late, at a low level, and it is mostly IgM. That lag is why a first infection makes you ill.

Some of that clone becomes memory B and T cells, which persist for years. On re-exposure the secondary response starts within hours, climbs far higher, lasts longer and is mostly IgG. You often clear the organism before symptoms begin. That is exactly what a vaccine buys, and why boosters exist — each repeat exposure enlarges the memory pool. It is also why a laboratory finding of IgM says recent infection while IgG says past infection or vaccination.

When tolerance fails

Lymphocytes are built with random receptors, so some inevitably recognize you. Tolerance is the screening that removes them. T cells are tested in the thymus: those that cannot read your MHC die, and those that bind your own proteins too strongly are deleted. B cells are screened separately in bone marrow, where those binding your tissue are deleted or made to rebuild their receptor.

When that screening fails you get autoimmunity — Hashimoto thyroiditis, type 1 diabetes, rheumatoid arthritis, multiple sclerosis, lupus. None of these is weakness. The system is working at full strength and simply aiming at the wrong target, which is why the treatment is immunosuppression rather than immune stimulation — the opposite of what you give in immunodeficiency, where the response is too small rather than misdirected.

Breathing is a pressure problem

Air moves down a pressure gradient, and your diaphragm creates that gradient. On inhalation the diaphragm contracts and flattens, the thoracic cavity gets bigger, and by Boyle's law a bigger space means lower pressure — so pressure inside the lungs falls below atmospheric and air flows in. On quiet exhalation the diaphragm relaxes, the cavity shrinks, pressure rises above atmospheric, and air flows out. Quiet exhalation is passive; it costs nothing but elastic recoil.

Surfactant, made by Type II alveolar cells, lowers surface tension so alveoli do not collapse on themselves. A premature infant without it has to fight to reinflate the lungs with every breath.

Ventilation, perfusion, diffusion

Three separate requirements, and all three must work. Ventilation is air reaching the alveoli. Perfusion is blood reaching the capillaries around them. Diffusion is gas actually crossing the membrane, driven by the partial pressure gradient.

A pulmonary embolism ventilates fine but does not perfuse. Pulmonary edema perfuses fine but the membrane is too thick to diffuse across. Naming which of the three has failed is usually the whole question.

How the gases travel

Oxygen is barely soluble, so about 98% rides on hemoglobin and only a couple of percent dissolves. Carbon dioxide is different: roughly 70% travels as bicarbonate in the plasma, about 23% on hemoglobin, and the rest dissolved.

That bicarbonate route is why respiration and pH are inseparable. CO₂ plus water makes carbonic acid, which releases H⁺. Retain CO₂ and you become acidotic; blow it off and you become alkalotic. The lungs are a pH organ as much as a gas organ.

Lung volumes, and why some air never leaves

A normal quiet breath is tidal volume, roughly 500 mL. You can inhale a lot more on top of it and exhale a lot more beyond it, and together with tidal volume that maximum sweep is vital capacity.

But no matter how hard you blow out, some air stays — residual volume. That is not a flaw. It keeps alveoli open between breaths so they do not have to be reinflated from collapsed each time, and it means gas exchange continues even while you are exhaling.

Compliance: how easily lungs inflate

Compliance is how readily the lungs expand for a given effort. Surfactant raises it by cutting surface tension. Scarring lowers it — in pulmonary fibrosis the lungs are stiff, and every breath costs more work.

Compliance and airway obstruction are different problems and it is worth keeping them apart. Fibrosis makes lungs hard to inflate. COPD makes air hard to get out, because narrowed airways and lost elastic recoil trap it on exhalation. Restrictive versus obstructive, in one sentence each.

Which muscles do the work of breathing

Quiet inspiration is the only part of normal breathing that costs muscular energy. The diaphragm flattens and the external intercostals lift the ribs outward, the thorax enlarges, and air follows the pressure drop inward. The diaphragm alone does about 70 percent of that work.

Quiet expiration is passive. You simply stop contracting and the stretched elastic tissue of the lungs and chest wall recoils — no muscle needed. Only forced expiration recruits the internal intercostals and abdominal muscles, and only forced inspiration calls in the scalenes, sternocleidomastoid and pectoralis minor.

Which is why a patient using neck muscles to pull air in, or belly muscles to push it out, is telling you the effortless version of breathing has already failed.

The three cells lining an alveolus

An alveolus has three cell types, and only one of them exchanges gas.

Type I alveolar cells are flat squamous cells covering about 95 percent of the surface. Being thin is the entire point — oxygen and carbon dioxide cross them almost instantly. Type II cells are cuboidal, occupy the rest, and secrete surfactant, the detergent-like film that lowers surface tension so an alveolus does not collapse on itself. They also serve as the repair stem cell after lung injury. Alveolar macrophages, or dust cells, patrol the surface and engulf whatever was inhaled past the airways.

Surfactant does not reach adequate levels until roughly week 34 of gestation, which is why a premature infant's alveoli collapse at every breath out and must be reopened from nothing — neonatal respiratory distress syndrome.

How hemoglobin decides when to let go

Each hemoglobin molecule carries four oxygen molecules, and binding the first makes the next easier — cooperative binding. That is why the oxygen-hemoglobin dissociation curve is S-shaped: in the lungs saturation holds near 98 percent even as the partial pressure dips, while in the tissues the curve falls steeply, so a small pressure drop unloads a lot of oxygen.

Four things shift the curve right, so hemoglobin releases oxygen more readily: rising carbon dioxide, falling pH, rising temperature and rising 2,3-BPG. The carbon dioxide and pH half of that is the Bohr effect. The opposite conditions shift it left and hold oxygen on.

Which is why working muscle — hot, acidic and loaded with carbon dioxide — strips far more oxygen from the same arterial blood than resting tissue does.

Carbon dioxide travels as bicarbonate

Oxygen and carbon dioxide do not travel the same way. About 70 percent of carbon dioxide is carried as bicarbonate, 20 percent rides on the globin part of hemoglobin as carbaminohemoglobin, and only 7 to 10 percent dissolves in plasma.

That bicarbonate is made inside the red cell: carbonic anhydrase joins carbon dioxide to water, forming carbonic acid, which splits into a hydrogen ion and bicarbonate. The bicarbonate leaves for the plasma and a chloride ion moves in to balance the charge — the chloride shift. In the lung the reaction reverses and the gas is blown off.

Because that reaction makes acid, ventilation sets pH — hypoventilating traps carbon dioxide and drops the pH, while hyperventilating blows it off, which is why a panicking patient who over-breathes goes alkalotic and tingles.

When ventilation and perfusion do not match

Air and blood must arrive at the same alveolus for exchange to happen, and the ventilation to perfusion ratio is about 0.8. There are two ways to break it.

A shunt is perfusion without ventilation — blood flows past an alveolus with no air, as in pneumonia, atelectasis or pulmonary edema. That blood reaches the left heart still deoxygenated, and extra oxygen helps little because air cannot reach it. Dead space is the mirror image: ventilation without perfusion, classically a pulmonary embolism blocking flow to an alveolus that is perfectly well ventilated.

The lung defends itself with hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction: vessels serving poorly ventilated regions narrow, pushing blood toward alveoli that actually have air. Which is why a small mismatch stays invisible while a large one drops saturation fast.

Two kinds of digestion, all the way down

Mechanical digestion breaks food into smaller pieces without changing it chemically — chewing, churning in the stomach, segmentation in the small intestine. Its purpose is surface area, because enzymes can only work on surfaces. Chemical digestion breaks the actual bonds.

Movement matters too. Peristalsis propels food forward; segmentation mixes it without moving it along. When you meet a GI term, ask which of those two it is.

Where each nutrient gets dismantled

Carbohydrate starts in the mouth with salivary amylase — the only chemical digestion that happens there. Protein starts in the stomach, where HCl denatures it and activates pepsinogen into pepsin. Fat is barely touched until the small intestine.

In the duodenum everything arrives at once: pancreatic amylase for carbohydrate, proteases for protein, pancreatic lipase for fat, and bicarbonate to neutralize the acid so those enzymes can work at all.

Bile is not an enzyme — and this is the exam's favorite trap

Fat does not dissolve in water, so it arrives as large globules that enzymes can barely reach. Bile, made by the liver and stored and concentrated by the gallbladder, breaks those globules into tiny droplets. That is emulsification — a mechanical act. It changes surface area, not chemistry.

Pancreatic lipase then does the actual chemical digestion of those droplets. Bile prepares; lipase digests. Keep the two verbs separate and this section becomes easy.

Absorption has addresses

The duodenum takes iron, calcium and magnesium and does most of the chemical work. The jejunum is the main absorptive site — carbohydrate, protein, fat, and water-soluble vitamins — thanks to folds, villi and microvilli multiplying its surface area. The ileum is the specialist: vitamin B12 (with intrinsic factor from the stomach), bile salts for recycling, and the fat-soluble vitamins A, D, E and K. The colon reclaims water and electrolytes and secretes no enzymes of its own — its bacteria do any remaining work and make vitamin K.

Notice that intrinsic factor is made in the stomach but used in the ileum. That is why a stomach problem shows up as a blood problem.

How a meal actually moves

You can break a meal down perfectly and still get nowhere if it does not move. Mastication breaks food and mixes it with saliva into a bolus. Deglutition follows in three phases — a voluntary buccal phase, then involuntary pharyngeal and esophageal phases in which the larynx lifts and the epiglottis folds over the airway.

Peristalsis then propels: circular muscle contracts behind the bolus and relaxes ahead of it. In the small intestine segmentation takes over — it does not push forward, it mixes chyme against the absorptive wall. The colon adds slow haustral churning and a few daily mass movements. Sphincters gate every handoff — upper esophageal, lower esophageal, pyloric, ileocaecal and both anal — which is why a weak lower esophageal sphincter lets acid into a tube with no acid defenses.

The four organs that never touch food

Four accessory organs do the chemical heavy lifting without food ever touching them — secretions travel down ducts.

The salivary glands — parotid, submandibular, sublingual — supply water, mucus, lysozyme and amylase to start starch. The liver makes bile continuously, but that is its smallest job: nutrient-rich blood from the gut reaches it through the hepatic portal vein, so the liver stores glycogen, builds albumin and clotting factors, converts ammonia to urea, and clears the drugs you give before they reach the systemic circulation. The gallbladder stores and concentrates bile until CCK squeezes it out. The pancreas is two organs — acinar cells send enzymes and duct cells bicarbonate to the duodenum, islets send insulin and glucagon to blood. Which is why one stone at the shared duct outlet causes jaundice and pancreatitis together.

The three phases, and the four hormones

Secretion is timed, not constant — three phases run the clock.

The cephalic phase starts before food arrives: the sight, smell or thought of food drives vagal output and your stomach begins secreting. The gastric phase begins with stretch and peptides, which release gastrin from G cells and drive acid and motility hard. The intestinal phase mostly switches the stomach off. Acid entering the duodenum releases secretin from S cells, which calls pancreatic bicarbonate to neutralize it. Fat and protein release CCK from I cells, which orders pancreatic enzymes, contracts the gallbladder and slows gastric emptying. GIP from the duodenum starts insulin release early, before absorbed glucose fully reaches the blood. Which is why a fatty meal sits heavy — CCK is deliberately holding it back until the duodenum can keep up.

Why the stomach does not digest itself

Stomach contents sit near pH 2 — acid strong enough to digest the tissue producing it. Four defenses stop that.

Surface cells secrete an alkaline mucus layer that traps bicarbonate against the epithelium, so the cell surface itself stays near neutral. Tight junctions stop acid leaking between cells. The lining is replaced every three to six days. And the dangerous enzymes are secreted inactive: chief cells release pepsinogen, a zymogen that only becomes pepsin once HCl meets it out in the lumen — never inside the cell that made it. The pancreas uses the same trick with trypsinogen. Prostaglandins maintain that mucus and its blood supply, which is why the NSAIDs you hand out, by blocking prostaglandins, cause ulcers, and why H. pylori, which burrows beneath the mucus, does too.

What the colon is actually for

By the time chyme reaches the colon the digesting is finished — the colon secretes mucus and no enzymes at all.

Its work is reclamation and storage. About a liter and a half of liquid chyme arrives each day, and the colon reclaims nearly all of that water along with sodium and chloride, leaving formed stool. Its microbiota ferment the fiber your own enzymes cannot touch, producing short-chain fatty acids, gas, and usable vitamin K and several B vitamins. Haustral churning holds contents against the wall, and two or three mass movements a day drive stool into the rectum, where stretch triggers the defecation reflex — internal sphincter involuntary, external sphincter yours. Which is why diarrhea dehydrates so quickly: transit outruns reabsorption, and potassium leaves with the water.

What kidneys are actually doing

Filtering is only the first job. The kidneys also regulate blood volume and pressure, balance electrolytes, control acid–base status by excreting H⁺ and reclaiming bicarbonate, excrete urea and creatinine, and secrete two hormones — erythropoietin to drive red cell production and renin to raise blood pressure.

One sentence explains most of it: water follows sodium. Retain sodium and water comes with it, so blood volume and pressure rise. Excrete sodium and water leaves too. That is how the kidney controls pressure, and how diuretics work.

Walking the nephron

Blood arrives through the afferent arteriole and enters the glomerulus, where high pressure forces water, ions, glucose and small wastes into Bowman's capsule. Proteins and blood cells are too large to pass — finding protein in urine means the filter is damaged. The efferent arteriole leaving is narrower than the one arriving, and that constriction is what sustains the pressure filtration needs.

The proximal convoluted tubule does most of the reabsorption, reclaiming essentially all glucose and amino acids. Then the loop of Henle does something clever: the descending limb lets water out but not ions, while the ascending limb pumps ions out but is impermeable to water. Removing salt without water builds a salty gradient in the medulla — and that gradient is what later lets the collecting duct pull water back and produce concentrated urine.

The distal convoluted tubule fine-tunes under aldosterone, and the collecting duct makes the final call on concentration under ADH.

Reabsorption and secretion point opposite ways

Reabsorption moves things filtrate → blood: keeping what is useful. Secretion moves things blood → filtrate: dumping extra H⁺, K⁺ and drugs. Filtration is indiscriminate; these two are where the precision happens. Mixing up the direction is the single most common error on this material.

Making gametes: two very different processes

Spermatogenesis is continuous from puberty. One primary spermatocyte goes through both meiotic divisions and yields four sperm, which then mature in the epididymis where they gain motility.

Oogenesis is almost the opposite. Every primary oocyte is formed before birth and then arrests. Each cycle after puberty, one completes the first division and is ovulated — but what is released is a secondary oocyte, arrested again in meiosis II. It only finishes, and only truly becomes an ovum, if a sperm penetrates it. One precursor yields one ovum plus discarded polar bodies.

The cycle, and what ends it

Days 1–5 are menstrual: the lining sheds, and it sheds because progesterone fell, not because anything rose. Days 1–13 are follicular: FSH matures follicles and the dominant one pours out estrogen, which rebuilds the endometrium. Around day 14 that estrogen peak flips to positive feedback and triggers an LH surge, which ruptures the follicle — ovulation. Days 15–28 are luteal: the leftover follicle becomes the corpus luteum and secretes progesterone to hold the lining ready. No pregnancy, corpus luteum dies, progesterone falls, and the cycle restarts.

Menopause is when the ovaries stop responding. Estrogen and progesterone fall — but FSH and LH rise, because the pituitary has lost its negative feedback and keeps shouting at ovaries that no longer answer. That counterintuitive pair is examined constantly.

Filtration is a pressure balance

Filtration happens because blood in the glomerulus sits under more pressure than the fluid in the capsule around it. Three pressures settle the argument: glomerular hydrostatic pressure (about 55 mmHg) pushes fluid out, capsular hydrostatic pressure (about 15) pushes back, and blood colloid osmotic pressure from plasma proteins (about 30) pulls it in. That leaves roughly 10 mmHg outward and a GFR near 125 mL/min.

The filtration membrane — fenestrated endothelium, basement membrane, podocyte slits — passes water and small solutes but holds back cells and proteins. The kidney defends that pressure, constricting the afferent arteriole when systemic pressure rises and the efferent when it falls — which is why a patient in shock stops making urine, and why protein in the urine tells you the membrane itself has been damaged.

Why the medulla stays salty

Concentrated urine is only possible because the kidney keeps its deep tissue salty, and the loop of Henle builds that gradient. The descending limb passes water but not salt, so filtrate loses water and grows concentrated. The ascending limb does the opposite — it pumps sodium and chloride out while staying waterproof, so the fluid leaving is dilute and the surrounding medulla turns increasingly salty. Urea recycling from the deep collecting duct deepens it, and the hairpin vasa recta pick that salt up going down and hand it back coming up, rather than washing the gradient out.

That gradient is what ADH exploits: it inserts aquaporins into the collecting duct and water follows the gradient out — which is why someone who cannot make ADH passes liters of dilute urine.

Storing urine, and letting it go

Once fluid leaves the collecting duct it is urine, and nothing downstream changes its composition. The ureters carry it down by peristalsis and enter the bladder at an oblique angle, so a filling bladder pinches them shut and urine cannot reflux toward the kidney. The bladder wall, the detrusor muscle, stretches to hold roughly 400-600 mL. Two sphincters guard the exit: the internal urethral sphincter is smooth muscle and involuntary, the external is skeletal and under your control.

At about 200-300 mL, stretch receptors fire and a parasympathetic reflex from S2-S4 contracts the detrusor and relaxes the internal sphincter. Voiding still waits on the external sphincter — which is why a spinal injury above the sacrum leaves a bladder that, once spinal shock passes, empties on reflex alone, without warning.

What actually gets ovulated

Follicles are not made on demand — you are born with every one you will ever have, each holding a primary oocyte frozen in prophase I since before birth. A primordial follicle becomes a primary follicle, then a secondary follicle as the granulosa layers thicken, then an antral follicle once a fluid-filled cavity opens, and finally one mature (Graafian) follicle outgrows the rest.

The LH surge finishes meiosis I, so what leaves the ovary is a secondary oocyte arrested in metaphase II with a polar body beside it — not an ovum. It becomes an ovum only if a sperm penetrates it. The emptied follicle becomes the corpus luteum and then scars into a corpus albicans — unless an embryo's hCG rescues it, which is exactly what a pregnancy test detects.

Sperm are made in one place, finished in another

Sperm production runs continuously in the seminiferous tubules, and two cell types make it possible. Sertoli cells nurse the sperm and form the blood-testis barrier that hides them from your immune system; Leydig cells make testosterone when LH tells them to, while FSH drives the Sertoli cells. The process takes about 70 days, ending in spermiogenesis, where a round cell sheds cytoplasm and becomes a head with an enzyme-filled acrosome, a mitochondria-packed midpiece and tail.

Those sperm still cannot swim; motility comes over about two weeks in the epididymis. The seminal vesicles add alkaline, fructose-rich fluid, the prostate adds slightly acidic fluid with enzymes that thin it, and the bulbourethral glands neutralize the urethra — which is why most of semen is gland secretion, and a vasectomy barely changes the volume.

Every misconception on this exam

  • "Higher pH means more hydrogen ions." Inverted. pH is inversely related to H⁺ — pH 2 has ten million times more H⁺ than pH 9.
  • "Positive feedback is harmful." No. Labor and clotting both depend on it. It amplifies rather than opposes; that is the only difference.
  • "Homeostasis means values never change." Values oscillate around a set point. Homeostasis is dynamic, not static.
  • "Osmosis moves solute." Osmosis moves water. Diffusion moves solute.
  • "Facilitated diffusion uses ATP." It uses a protein but no ATP. Only active transport costs energy.
  • "Water moves toward low solute." Backwards. Water follows solute, moving toward the more concentrated side.
  • "The epidermis has its own blood supply." It is avascular — fed by diffusion from the dermis. That is why its outer cells die.
  • "Vitamin D is fully made in the skin." Skin only starts it. The liver then the kidney complete activation to calcitriol.
  • "Melanin causes skin cancer." Melanin protects against it by absorbing UV.
  • "Osteoclasts build bone." Osteoblasts build; osteoclasts carve.
  • "PTH lowers blood calcium." PTH Pulls calcium into the blood. Calcitonin tones it down.
  • "Bone is inert." It remodels continuously and is the body's main calcium reservoir.
  • "Rigor mortis is muscle contracting." It is failure to relax — without ATP, myosin cannot release actin.
  • "Anaerobic metabolism makes more ATP because it's faster." Faster, but ~2 ATP vs ~30–32 aerobic.
  • "Schwann cells myelinate the CNS." Schwann = PNS. Oligodendrocytes = CNS.
  • "Depolarization is potassium moving in." Depolarization is sodium in. Repolarization is potassium out.
  • "Sympathetic is bad, parasympathetic is good." Both are essential and balanced.
  • "All senses relay through the thalamus." Olfaction is the exception — it bypasses the thalamus entirely.
  • "The lens flattens for near vision." Opposite. It rounds up for near, flattens for far.
  • "20/200 is better than 20/20." Far worse — the larger the second number, the poorer the acuity.
  • "The semicircular canals detect gravity." They detect rotation. The utricle and saccule detect gravity and linear acceleration.
  • "Low TSH means hypothyroid." In primary hyperthyroidism TSH is low because T4 is high — negative feedback.
  • "Steroid hormones bind surface receptors." Lipid-soluble hormones cross the membrane and bind intracellular receptors.
  • "FSH and LH do the same thing." FSH grows the follicle; LH triggers ovulation.
  • "Vascular spasm is the second step of hemostasis." It is first. Platelet plug is second, coagulation third.
  • "Arteries always carry oxygenated blood." The pulmonary artery carries deoxygenated blood.
  • "Plasma proteins don't matter." Albumin holds fluid in the vessel. Lose it and you get edema.
  • "S1 is the semilunar valves." S1 = AV valves closing. S2 = semilunar valves closing.
  • "Cardiac output is just heart rate." CO = HR × stroke volume. Both matter.
  • "The QRS shows atrial repolarization." It shows ventricular depolarization; atrial repolarization is hidden inside it.
  • "NK cells are adaptive." They are lymphocytes but act without prior exposure — innate.
  • "Passive immunity gives lasting protection." It fades in weeks to months and leaves no memory.
  • "Autoimmunity means a weak immune system." It is misdirected, not weak.
  • "Exhalation is active." Quiet exhalation is passive elastic recoil.
  • "CO₂ travels mostly on hemoglobin." ~70% travels as bicarbonate in plasma.
  • "Type II cells perform gas exchange." Type I exchange gas; Type II make surfactant.
  • "Bile is an enzyme." Bile emulsifies — a mechanical action. Pancreatic lipase does the chemical digestion.
  • "Most absorption happens in the stomach." Very little. The jejunum is the main absorptive site.
  • "The colon secretes digestive enzymes." None. Its bacteria do any remaining breakdown.
  • "Reabsorption and secretion are the same." Reabsorption: filtrate → blood. Secretion: blood → filtrate.
  • "An ovum is what gets ovulated." A secondary oocyte is. It becomes an ovum only if fertilized.
  • "FSH and LH fall in menopause." They rise — the ovaries stop responding, so negative feedback is lost.

Q1. A researcher measures how quickly a single hepatocyte takes up glucose after insulin exposure. Which subdivision of physiology is this?
  1. Cell physiology
  2. Organ physiology
  3. Systemic physiology
  4. Pathophysiology
  5. Comparative anatomy
Answer: A. The unit of study is one cell and its internal handling of glucose, so this is cell physiology. Organ physiology would study the liver as a whole.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 2
Q2. A student explains how the respiratory and cardiovascular systems cooperate to raise oxygen delivery during exercise. This is best described as:
  1. Cell physiology
  2. Systemic physiology
  3. Organ physiology
  4. Regional gross anatomy
  5. Microscopic anatomy
Answer: B. Systemic physiology examines how multiple organ systems interact to accomplish a shared function.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 2
Q3. Which statement best captures the relationship between anatomy and physiology?
  1. They examine identical questions and differ only in the laboratory tools each one uses
  2. Physiology is a narrow subdivision of gross anatomy concerned with cadaver dissection
  3. Anatomy studies structure; physiology studies function
  4. Anatomy studies disease; physiology studies health
  5. Physiology examines only microscopic structures
Answer: C. Anatomy asks what a structure IS; physiology asks what it DOES. Structure and function are tightly linked but are distinct questions.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 2
Q4. A clinician studies how glomerular filtration changes in a patient with hypertension. This is:
  1. Cell physiology only
  2. Comparative physiology
  3. Gross anatomy
  4. Organ physiology
  5. Renal histology
Answer: D. The kidney is the unit of study, so this is organ physiology.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 2
Q5. Pathophysiology is best defined as the study of:
  1. How the healthy body maintains its normal function under everyday conditions
  2. The structural changes disease produces in tissue as seen under the microscope
  3. How drugs act on their target receptors
  4. Microbial causes of infectious disease
  5. How normal function is disrupted by disease
Answer: E. Pathophysiology examines the altered function that results from disease — normal physiology gone wrong.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 2
Q6. Which pairing of subdivision and example is INCORRECT?
  1. Cell physiology — how the cardiovascular and renal systems jointly regulate blood pressure
  2. Cell physiology — how a single neuron generates and propagates an action potential along its axon membrane
  3. Organ physiology — how the stomach as a whole secretes hydrochloric acid and mixes food into chyme
  4. Systemic physiology — how nerves and hormones together regulate body temperature
  5. Pathophysiology — how insulin resistance alters glucose handling in diabetes
Answer: A. Two organ systems working together is systemic physiology, not cell physiology.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 2
Q7. Two atoms share a pair of electrons unequally, producing partial charges. This bond is:
  1. Ionic attraction
  2. Polar covalent
  3. Nonpolar covalent
  4. Hydrogen bonding
  5. Van der Waals
Answer: B. Unequal sharing creates partial positive and partial negative regions — the definition of a polar covalent bond.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 5
Q8. Sodium transfers its single valence electron to chlorine. What has formed?
  1. A nonpolar covalent bond
  2. A polar covalent bond
  3. An ionic bond
  4. A hydrogen bond
  5. A peptide bond
Answer: C. Complete transfer of an electron creates oppositely charged ions that attract — an ionic bond.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 4
Q9. An atom's chemical bonding behavior is determined primarily by:
  1. The number of neutrons packed into the nucleus alongside the protons
  2. The number of protons in the nucleus
  3. Its total atomic mass in daltons, summing protons and neutrons
  4. Electrons in the outermost (valence) shell
  5. The physical size of the atom's nucleus
Answer: D. Valence electrons are the ones available to be shared or transferred, so they dictate bonding.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 4
Q10. Which particle is gained or lost when an ion forms?
  1. Proton
  2. Neutron
  3. Atomic nucleus
  4. Alpha particle
  5. Electron
Answer: E. Ions form by gaining or losing electrons. Changing proton number would change the element itself.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q11. An atom that has GAINED an electron is best described as:
  1. An anion with a negative charge
  2. A cation with a positive charge, formed when an electron is lost
  3. A cation with a negative charge, since protons are unchanged in number
  4. An anion with a positive charge
  5. An isotope with extra neutrons
Answer: A. Gaining a negatively charged electron makes the atom net negative — an anion.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 4
Q12. Which bond type holds the two hydrogens to oxygen WITHIN a single water molecule?
  1. Hydrogen bonds
  2. Polar covalent bonds
  3. Ionic bonds
  4. Nonpolar covalent bonds
  5. Van der Waals forces
Answer: B. Within one water molecule the atoms share electrons unequally — polar covalent. Hydrogen bonds act BETWEEN separate water molecules.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 6
Q13. Water molecules cling to one another, giving water a high surface tension. This is due to:
  1. Ionic bonds between molecules
  2. Covalent bonds between molecules
  3. Hydrogen bonds between molecules
  4. Electron transfer between molecules
  5. Neutron sharing between atoms
Answer: C. Hydrogen bonding between the partial-positive H of one molecule and partial-negative O of another produces cohesion.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 7
Q14. Two identical atoms share electrons equally. The resulting bond is:
  1. Ionic attraction
  2. Polar covalent
  3. Hydrogen bonding
  4. Nonpolar covalent
  5. Ionic-covalent hybrid
Answer: D. Identical atoms pull equally, so there are no partial charges — nonpolar covalent.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 5
Q15. Which of the following is the WEAKEST interaction?
  1. Ionic bond
  2. Polar covalent bond
  3. Nonpolar covalent bond
  4. Double covalent bond
  5. Hydrogen bond
Answer: E. Hydrogen bonds are weak individually — which is what lets them form and break rapidly in biological systems.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 7
Q16. Why is hydrogen bonding physiologically important?
  1. It allows DNA strands and protein folds to be held together yet separated when needed
  2. It permanently locks folded proteins into a rigid shape that can never be altered by heat or pH
  3. It is the strongest chemical bond in the body, far stronger than either a covalent or an ionic bond
  4. It transfers electrons from one molecule to another to form ions
  5. It creates the sodium and potassium ions used in nerve conduction
Answer: A. Because hydrogen bonds are weak and reversible, DNA can be unzipped for transcription and proteins can change conformation.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 7
Q17. Homeostasis is best defined as:
  1. A completely unchanging internal environment in which no variable is ever allowed to fluctuate
  2. Maintenance of a stable internal environment despite external change
  3. The body's inflammatory response to tissue injury, infection, and foreign material
  4. A resting state that is reached only during deep sleep
  5. The complete absence of disease or injury
Answer: B. Homeostasis is dynamic — variables fluctuate around a set point rather than remaining fixed.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 8
Q18. In a feedback loop, the structure that detects the change is the:
  1. Effector
  2. Control center
  3. Receptor
  4. Set point
  5. Stimulus
Answer: C. Order is stimulus → receptor (detects) → control center (decides) → effector (acts).Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q19. Which component of a feedback loop carries out the corrective response?
  1. Receptor
  2. Control center
  3. Stimulus
  4. Effector
  5. Set point
Answer: D. The effector performs the action that moves the variable back toward the set point.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 10
Q20. Body temperature rises, sweating begins, temperature falls, and sweating stops. This illustrates:
  1. Positive feedback
  2. Feedforward control
  3. Acclimatization
  4. Long-term adaptation
  5. Negative feedback
Answer: E. The response opposes and shuts off the original stimulus — the defining feature of negative feedback.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 11
Q21. Which is a true POSITIVE feedback loop?
  1. Oxytocin release intensifying uterine contractions during labor
  2. Insulin release lowering blood glucose after a carbohydrate-rich meal
  3. Sweating and skin vasodilation lowering core body temperature on a hot day
  4. Baroreceptor reflexes slowing the heart when pressure rises
  5. Renal excretion of hydrogen ions to correct blood pH
Answer: A. Contractions trigger oxytocin, which strengthens contractions — an escalating loop that ends only with delivery.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q22. The key difference between positive and negative feedback is that positive feedback:
  1. Is always harmful to the body and never occurs outside of disease states
  2. Amplifies the original stimulus rather than opposing it
  3. Involves no receptor, no control center, and no integrating pathway
  4. Acts far more slowly than negative feedback
  5. Requires no effector to carry out a response
Answer: B. Positive feedback drives the variable further from baseline. It is not inherently harmful — labor and clotting both rely on it.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 11
Q23. Blood clotting is considered positive feedback because:
  1. Clotting shuts itself off the moment the first platelet is activated
  2. It restores circulating blood volume back toward its normal set point
  3. Each activated platelet recruits more platelets to the site
  4. It is controlled by centers in the brainstem
  5. It opposes and reverses the initial injury
Answer: C. Platelet activation recruits further platelets, escalating until the vessel is sealed.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q24. Which body systems are the two principal regulators of homeostasis?
  1. Digestive and urinary
  2. Skeletal and muscular
  3. Integumentary and lymphatic
  4. Nervous and endocrine
  5. Respiratory and reproductive
Answer: D. The nervous system gives fast electrical control; the endocrine system gives slower, longer-lasting hormonal control.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 8
Q25. Compared with endocrine regulation, nervous regulation is:
  1. Slower in onset but far longer lasting in its effects
  2. Slower in onset and shorter lasting than hormonal signaling
  3. Faster in onset and longer lasting
  4. Identical in onset and duration
  5. Faster in onset but shorter lasting
Answer: E. Nerve signals act in milliseconds but stop quickly; hormones take longer to act but persist.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 9
Q26. In negative feedback, the response ultimately acts on the receptor by:
  1. Removing the stimulus so the receptor stops signaling
  2. Sensitizing it so that it signals even more strongly than before
  3. Destroying the receptor permanently so it can never fire again
  4. Converting the receptor into an effector
  5. Raising the set point to a new value
Answer: A. By correcting the variable, the response eliminates the stimulus the receptor was detecting, switching the loop off.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 10
Q27. A solution's pH falls from 7 to 5. The hydrogen ion concentration has:
  1. Decreased 10-fold
  2. Increased 100-fold
  3. Decreased by a factor of one hundred
  4. Increased by a factor of ten
  5. Remained the same
Answer: B. The pH scale is logarithmic — each unit is a 10-fold change, so two units is 100-fold. Lower pH means MORE H+.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q28. Which solution contains the fewest hydrogen ions?
  1. pH 1.5
  2. pH 4
  3. pH 12
  4. pH 6.5
  5. pH 10
Answer: C. pH is inversely related to H+ concentration, so the highest pH has the fewest hydrogen ions.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q29. The normal pH range of arterial blood is:
  1. 6.85-7.05
  2. 7.15-7.25
  3. 7.55-7.65
  4. 7.35-7.45
  5. 7.75-7.85
Answer: D. 7.35-7.45 is a narrow window because enzyme function and protein structure depend on it.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 13
Q30. A patient's arterial blood pH is 7.28. This is termed:
  1. Alkalosis
  2. Normal
  3. Neutral
  4. Compensated alkalosis
  5. Acidosis
Answer: E. Below 7.35 is acidosis. Above 7.45 is alkalosis.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q31. What does a buffer do?
  1. Resists pH change by accepting or releasing hydrogen ions
  2. Permanently prevents any change in pH from ever occurring in the blood
  3. Raises pH by adding hydroxide ions
  4. Lowers pH only, by releasing hydrogen ions and never accepting them
  5. Removes water molecules from the solution
Answer: A. A buffer works in both directions, soaking up excess H+ or releasing H+ when levels fall.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q32. The principal buffer system of the blood is:
  1. Phosphate
  2. Bicarbonate
  3. Ammonium ion
  4. Protein-only
  5. Sulfate ion
Answer: B. The bicarbonate system is the main extracellular buffer and is tied to both respiratory and renal control.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q33. If pH rises above the normal range, the patient is in alkalosis, which means H+ concentration has:
  1. Increased
  2. Stayed the same
  3. Decreased
  4. Doubled
  5. Become zero
Answer: C. Higher pH = fewer hydrogen ions, by definition of the inverse relationship.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q34. Why must blood pH be tightly regulated?
  1. Because the hypothalamus uses blood pH, not blood temperature, to set the body's thermostat
  2. Because the kidneys decide how much circulating blood volume to keep based directly on plasma pH
  3. Because clotting time is determined entirely by blood pH
  4. Because enzymes and proteins denature and lose function outside a narrow pH
  5. Because hair and nail growth rates depend on blood pH
Answer: D. Protein shape — and therefore enzyme activity — is pH dependent. Small deviations impair many reactions at once.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q35. A pH of 7.0 is considered:
  1. Strongly acidic
  2. Weakly acidic
  3. Weakly basic
  4. Strongly basic
  5. Neutral
Answer: E. At pH 7 the H+ and OH- concentrations are equal.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q36. Which change would most directly lower blood pH?
  1. Retention of carbon dioxide
  2. Excretion of hydrogen ions by the renal tubules
  3. Reabsorption of filtered bicarbonate in the proximal tubule
  4. Rapid hyperventilation
  5. Vomiting gastric acid
Answer: A. Retained CO2 forms carbonic acid, releasing H+ and lowering pH — respiratory acidosis.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 13
Q37. A marathon runner becomes dehydrated. Receptors detect rising blood osmolarity, the hypothalamus signals ADH release, and the kidneys conserve water. This sequence is an example of:
  1. Positive feedback
  2. Negative feedback
  3. A pathological loop
  4. Feedforward regulation
  5. Passive diffusion
Answer: B. The response (water conservation) corrects the stimulus (high osmolarity) and switches the loop off — negative feedback.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 15
Q38. Which of the following would NOT be part of a homeostatic feedback loop?
  1. A receptor detecting a change in a variable
  2. A control center integrating information
  3. A permanent structural change to an organ
  4. An effector producing a response
  5. A stimulus that alters a variable
Answer: C. Feedback loops produce functional, reversible corrections — not permanent structural remodeling.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 10
Q39. A patient hyperventilates during a panic attack, blowing off large amounts of CO2. The expected acid-base result is:
  1. Respiratory acidosis
  2. Metabolic acidosis
  3. No acid-base change
  4. Respiratory alkalosis
  5. Complete buffer failure
Answer: D. Losing CO2 removes carbonic acid, so H+ falls and pH rises — respiratory alkalosis.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 15
Q40. Which subdivision studies how the cardiovascular and respiratory systems cooperate during exercise?
  1. Cell physiology
  2. Organ physiology
  3. Microscopic anatomy
  4. Comparative physiology
  5. Systemic physiology
Answer: E. Two or more organ systems working together is systemic physiology.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 2
Q41. A hydrogen bond forms between:
  1. A partial positive hydrogen and a partial negative atom on another molecule
  2. Two atomic nuclei that are pulled together by their opposite electrical charges
  3. Two electrons that are paired within the same valence shell
  4. A proton and a neutron held together inside the atomic nucleus
  5. Two nonpolar molecules that carry no partial charges at all
Answer: A. Hydrogen bonds are weak electrostatic attractions between opposite partial charges.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 4
Q42. Which best explains why water is an excellent solvent for ions?
  1. It is a nonpolar molecule whose atoms share electrons perfectly evenly
  2. Its polarity lets it surround and separate charged particles
  3. It carries a full negative charge distributed over the whole molecule
  4. It forms ionic bonds with dissolved solutes
  5. It has unusually low surface tension
Answer: B. Polar water molecules orient around ions, pulling them apart and keeping them in solution.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 5
Q43. An atom with 6 valence electrons will most likely:
  1. Lose all 6 valence electrons to another atom
  2. Gain 6 more electrons to reach a total of twelve
  3. Gain 2 electrons to complete its outer shell
  4. Lose 2 protons from the nucleus to balance out
  5. Remain chemically inert and form no bonds at all
Answer: C. Atoms bond so as to fill the valence shell, usually to eight electrons.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 4
Q44. Which is an example of a chemical buffer at work?
  1. Sweating to cool the body when core temperature climbs too high
  2. Shivering to generate heat when the body cools below its set point
  3. Vasoconstriction of skin vessels in cold
  4. Bicarbonate accepting a hydrogen ion when acid rises
  5. Increased urine output after drinking
Answer: D. A buffer binds free H+ when acid rises and releases it when acid falls.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 14
Q45. In a feedback loop, the set point is best described as:
  1. The stimulus that starts the loop
  2. The effector that acts on the variable
  3. The receptor that detects the change
  4. The final response of the effector
  5. The normal value the body defends
Answer: E. The set point is the target value the loop works to maintain.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 11
Q46. Which is NOT an example of negative feedback?
  1. Oxytocin intensifying labor contractions
  2. Blood glucose regulation by insulin
  3. Body temperature regulation by sweating
  4. Blood pressure regulation by baroreceptors
  5. Calcium regulation by parathyroid hormone
Answer: A. Oxytocin in labor amplifies the stimulus, making it positive feedback.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q47. Blood pH of 7.52 would be classified as:
  1. Normal
  2. Alkalosis
  3. Acidosis
  4. Neutral
  5. Compensated acidosis
Answer: B. Above 7.45 is alkalosis; the hydrogen ion concentration has fallen.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q48. Which statement about pH is correct?
  1. A higher pH value means more free hydrogen ions are present in solution
  2. pH is a direct measure of how much oxygen the blood is carrying
  3. pH and hydrogen ion concentration are inversely related
  4. Each pH unit is a twofold change in H+
  5. A pH of exactly 7 is strongly acidic
Answer: C. More H+ gives a LOWER pH. Each unit is a tenfold change.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q49. The nervous system contributes to homeostasis mainly by:
  1. Slow hormonal signaling that produces long-lasting effects on target cells
  2. Producing antibodies directed against foreign antigens in the blood
  3. Storing calcium and phosphate in bone
  4. Rapid electrical signaling with short-lived effects
  5. Filtering blood and excreting waste
Answer: D. Nerve signals act in milliseconds but stop quickly, unlike hormones.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 9
Q50. Which pair correctly matches bond type to electron behavior?
  1. Ionic — electrons shared equally
  2. Covalent — electrons transferred
  3. Hydrogen — electrons transferred
  4. Nonpolar covalent — electrons transferred
  5. Ionic — electrons transferred
Answer: E. Ionic bonds transfer electrons; covalent bonds share them.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 5
Q51. A solution at pH 3 compared with pH 6 has:
  1. 1000 times more H+
  2. 3 times more H+
  3. 100 times fewer H+
  4. Exactly the same H+
  5. 10 times fewer H+
Answer: A. Three pH units equals 10x10x10 = 1000-fold more hydrogen ions.Lecture Outline, Module 1 — p.4
Q52. Which is the correct order of a feedback loop?
  1. Receptor, stimulus, integrating center, effector, corrective response
  2. Stimulus, receptor, control center, effector, response
  3. Effector, sensor, stimulus, control center, response
  4. Control center, stimulus, receptor, effector, corrective response
  5. Stimulus, effector, sensor, control center, response
Answer: B. A change is detected, integrated, then acted upon.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 10
Q53. Why can homeostasis be described as dynamic rather than static?
  1. Regulated values never change at all from one moment to the next
  2. The body has no set points for any variable at all
  3. Values fluctuate around a set point rather than staying fixed
  4. Only positive feedback loops operate in the body
  5. It only operates during sleep and periods of rest
Answer: C. Variables oscillate within a normal range; they are not held perfectly constant.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 11
Q54. Pathophysiology differs from physiology because it studies:
  1. Structure rather than function
  2. Only microscopic anatomy
  3. Chemical bonding in molecules
  4. Function altered by disease
  5. Population-level health trends
Answer: D. Pathophysiology examines how disease disrupts normal function.Module 1 live-class deck, slide 2
Q55. The defining functional property of the plasma membrane is that it is:
  1. Freely permeable
  2. Completely impermeable
  3. Permeable only to water
  4. Permeable only to lipids
  5. Selectively permeable
Answer: E. Selective permeability lets the cell admit what it needs and exclude what it doesn't — the basis of cellular homeostasis.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 2
Q56. Which membrane protein anchors the cytoskeleton to the inner membrane surface?
  1. Anchoring protein
  2. Transport protein
  3. Membrane enzyme protein
  4. Antigen marker protein
  5. Receptor protein
Answer: A. Anchoring proteins physically tether the cytoskeleton, maintaining cell shape and mechanical strength.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 13
Q57. A membrane protein that identifies the cell as belonging to 'self' is a:
  1. Transport carrier protein
  2. Antigen (identifier) protein
  3. Membrane-bound enzyme protein
  4. Cytoskeletal anchoring protein
  5. Ion channel (pore) protein
Answer: B. Antigen proteins are the molecular ID badge the immune system reads to distinguish self from non-self.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 17
Q58. Which membrane protein type catalyzes chemical reactions at the membrane surface?
  1. Anchoring
  2. Antigen
  3. Enzyme
  4. Cell adhesion
  5. Channel
Answer: C. Membrane-bound enzymes speed reactions occurring at or near the membrane.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 19
Q59. If a cell membrane lost selective permeability, the most immediate consequence would be:
  1. Increased ATP production by the mitochondria
  2. Faster protein synthesis at the ribosomes
  3. More efficient DNA repair inside the nucleus
  4. Loss of control over intracellular composition
  5. Faster mitosis and more frequent cell division
Answer: D. Without selectivity, ions and solutes equilibrate freely with the exterior and the internal environment collapses.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 3
Q60. Cell adhesion molecules primarily function to:
  1. Move ions across the membrane against gradients
  2. Digest worn-out organelles and cellular debris
  3. Synthesize lipids and steroid hormones
  4. Store genetic material inside the nucleus
  5. Bind cells to neighboring cells and matrix
Answer: E. Adhesion molecules hold tissues together and support communication between adjacent cells.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 4
Q61. Simple diffusion is best characterized as movement of solute:
  1. Down its gradient, no ATP required
  2. Against its gradient, ATP required
  3. Down its gradient, ATP required
  4. Against its gradient, no ATP required
  5. Only through protein channels
Answer: A. Diffusion is passive: solute moves from high to low concentration and no energy is consumed.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 7
Q62. Facilitated diffusion differs from simple diffusion in that it:
  1. Requires ATP hydrolysis to drive the solute uphill
  2. Uses a transport protein but still requires no ATP
  3. Moves solute against its concentration gradient
  4. Moves only water molecules and never any solutes
  5. Occurs only in excitable nerve and muscle cells
Answer: B. Facilitated diffusion uses a carrier or channel for molecules that cannot cross the lipid bilayer, but it is still passive.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 6
Q63. Osmosis specifically describes the movement of:
  1. Ions
  2. Glucose
  3. Water
  4. Proteins
  5. Lipids
Answer: C. Osmosis is the diffusion of WATER across a selectively permeable membrane.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 11
Q64. In osmosis, water moves toward the compartment with:
  1. Lower solute concentration
  2. Equal solute concentration
  3. Lower temperature and viscosity
  4. Higher solute concentration
  5. Higher pH and thicker membrane
Answer: D. Water follows solute. It moves from dilute to concentrated until osmotic equilibrium is reached.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 11
Q65. Does osmosis require ATP?
  1. Yes, always
  2. Only in animal cells
  3. Only across the nuclear membrane
  4. Only when solute is glucose
  5. No, it is passive
Answer: E. Osmosis is driven by the osmotic gradient, not by cellular energy.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 11
Q66. Which is an example of ACTIVE transport?
  1. The sodium-potassium pump
  2. Oxygen entering a capillary
  3. Water crossing a membrane
  4. Carbon dioxide leaving a cell
  5. Glucose moving down its gradient via GLUT
Answer: A. The Na+/K+ pump moves both ions against their gradients and consumes ATP.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 9
Q67. A red blood cell placed in a hypotonic solution will:
  1. Shrink and crenate
  2. Swell and possibly lyse
  3. Remain unchanged in volume
  4. Actively pump out water
  5. Increase its ATP output
Answer: B. Hypotonic means the outside is more dilute, so water moves INTO the cell, swelling it.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 12
Q68. A cell placed in a hypertonic solution will:
  1. Swell with water
  2. Burst open (lyse)
  3. Shrink (crenate)
  4. Stay the same size
  5. Divide by mitosis
Answer: C. Hypertonic means the outside is more concentrated, so water leaves the cell and it shrivels.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 12
Q69. Endocytosis and exocytosis are classified as:
  1. Simple diffusion (passive)
  2. Carrier-mediated diffusion
  3. Osmosis of water across a membrane
  4. Vesicular (active) transport
  5. Passive hydrostatic filtration
Answer: D. Both move bulk material in membrane-bound vesicles and require ATP.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 9
Q70. The sodium-potassium pump moves:
  1. 3 Na+ in and 2 K+ out
  2. 2 Na+ out and 3 K+ in
  3. Equal amounts of both
  4. Sodium only, no potassium
  5. 3 Na+ out and 2 K+ in
Answer: E. Three sodium out, two potassium in, per ATP — a net export of positive charge that helps set the resting potential.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 8
Q71. Which substance crosses the lipid bilayer most readily by simple diffusion?
  1. Oxygen
  2. Sodium ion
  3. Glucose
  4. Protein
  5. Potassium ion
Answer: A. Small nonpolar gases pass freely. Charged ions and large polar molecules need transport proteins.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 7
Q72. Which organelle is the primary site of ATP production?
  1. Nucleus
  2. Mitochondrion
  3. Golgi apparatus
  4. Lysosome
  5. Smooth ER
Answer: B. The mitochondrion runs the Krebs cycle and electron transport chain, generating the bulk of cellular ATP.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 14
Q73. The organelle containing hydrolytic enzymes that digest worn-out organelles is the:
  1. Ribosome
  2. Golgi apparatus
  3. Lysosome
  4. Nucleolus
  5. Peroxisome
Answer: C. Lysosomes are the cell's recycling and disposal compartment.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 14
Q74. Rough endoplasmic reticulum is distinguished by the presence of:
  1. Stored lipid droplets
  2. Digestive hydrolytic enzymes
  3. Green chlorophyll pigment
  4. Ribosomes on its surface
  5. Motile cilia and flagella
Answer: D. The ribosomes studding rough ER give it its texture and its role in protein processing.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 14
Q75. Smooth endoplasmic reticulum is primarily responsible for:
  1. Protein synthesis on attached ribosomes
  2. DNA replication and repair
  3. ATP production by oxidation
  4. Waste digestion and recycling
  5. Lipid synthesis and detoxification
Answer: E. Smooth ER lacks ribosomes and instead makes lipids and steroids, and detoxifies drugs.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 14
Q76. Which organelle modifies, sorts and packages proteins for shipment?
  1. Golgi apparatus
  2. Cell nucleus
  3. Lysosomal vesicle
  4. Mitochondrion
  5. Free ribosome
Answer: A. The Golgi is the finishing and dispatch department of the cell.Lecture Outline, Module 2 — p.11
Q77. The main function of the nucleus is to:
  1. Produce ATP by aerobic respiration
  2. Store and transmit genetic information
  3. Digest waste with hydrolytic enzymes
  4. Synthesize lipids and steroid hormones
  5. Package and ship proteins in vesicles
Answer: B. The nucleus houses DNA and is the site of transcription.Lecture Outline, Module 2 — p.11
Q78. A cell that secretes large amounts of protein hormone would be expected to have abundant:
  1. Smooth ER and lysosomes
  2. Lysosomes and vacuoles
  3. Rough ER and Golgi
  4. Peroxisomes only
  5. Microvilli and cilia
Answer: C. Protein secretion requires ribosome-studded rough ER for synthesis and Golgi for packaging.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 14
Q79. A muscle cell that fatigues rapidly is most likely deficient in:
  1. Lysosomes
  2. Golgi stacks
  3. Peroxisomes
  4. Mitochondria
  5. Smooth ER sacs
Answer: D. Fewer mitochondria means less aerobic ATP, so the cell tires quickly.Lecture Outline, Module 2 — p.11
Q80. Where does glycolysis occur?
  1. Mitochondrial matrix
  2. Nucleus
  3. Golgi apparatus
  4. Lysosome
  5. Cytoplasm
Answer: E. Glycolysis is cytoplasmic and anaerobic. Only the later aerobic stages occur in the mitochondrion.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 19
Q81. Which sequence correctly orders ATP production?
  1. Glycolysis → Krebs cycle → electron transport chain
  2. Krebs cycle → glycolysis → electron transport chain
  3. Electron transport chain → glycolysis → Krebs cycle
  4. Glycolysis → electron transport → Krebs cycle
  5. Krebs cycle → electron transport → glycolysis
Answer: A. Glucose is split in the cytoplasm, then pyruvate enters the mitochondrion for the Krebs cycle and finally the electron transport chain.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 19
Q82. Which stage of cellular respiration yields the MOST ATP?
  1. Anaerobic glycolysis
  2. Electron transport chain
  3. The Krebs (citric acid) cycle
  4. Lactic acid fermentation
  5. Substrate phosphorylation
Answer: B. The electron transport chain produces the large majority of ATP and requires oxygen as the final electron acceptor.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 19
Q83. Transcription occurs in the ____ and produces ____.
  1. cytoplasm; protein
  2. nucleus; protein
  3. nucleus; mRNA
  4. ribosome; mRNA
  5. mitochondrion; ATP
Answer: C. DNA is copied into messenger RNA inside the nucleus.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 15
Q84. Translation occurs at the ____ and produces ____.
  1. nucleus; new mRNA
  2. Golgi; packaged lipid
  3. lysosome; enzyme
  4. ribosome; protein
  5. nucleolus; new DNA
Answer: D. The ribosome reads mRNA codons and assembles the corresponding amino acid chain.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 15
Q85. Which RNA carries amino acids to the ribosome?
  1. mRNA
  2. rRNA
  3. siRNA
  4. DNA
  5. tRNA
Answer: E. Transfer RNA matches its anticodon to the mRNA codon and delivers the correct amino acid.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 16
Q86. Which RNA forms a structural and catalytic part of the ribosome itself?

  5. snRNA

Q87. Which RNA carries the genetic message from DNA to the cytoplasm?

Q88. The end product of mitosis is:
  1. Four genetically different haploid cells
  2. A single haploid daughter cell
  3. Two genetically identical diploid cells
  4. Four haploid gametes with half the DNA
  5. Two haploid cells with half the chromosomes
Answer: C. Mitosis produces two identical diploid daughter cells — the basis of growth and repair.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 21
Q89. Why is mitosis essential to an adult organism?
  1. It creates genetic diversity through crossing over
  2. It produces sperm and egg cells for reproduction
  3. It reduces the chromosome number by half in daughter cells
  4. It replaces damaged and dying cells with identical copies
  5. It generates the ATP that cells need for energy
Answer: D. Tissue maintenance depends on faithful replacement of lost cells.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 21
Q90. A drug that blocks tRNA function would most directly impair:

  3. Mitosis

  5. Translation

Q91. If a cell's ribosomes were destroyed, which process would halt first?

  2. ATP production
  3. DNA replication
  4. Osmosis of water
  5. Lipid droplet storage
Answer: A. Ribosomes are the machinery of translation, so protein synthesis stops immediately.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 16
Q92. A mutation that changes a single mRNA codon would most directly alter:

  2. The amino acid inserted at that position
  3. The number of mitochondria in the cell
  4. Membrane permeability to sodium and chloride
  5. The rate of osmosis across the membrane

Q93. Which structure gives the plasma membrane its basic framework?

Q94. A cell placed in an isotonic solution will:

  4. Show no net water movement

Q95. Which process would STOP if a cell ran out of ATP?

  5. The sodium-potassium pump

Q97. The main role of the cytoskeleton is to:

  4. Digest cellular waste and destroy invading bacteria

Q101. Which describes a hypertonic solution relative to the cell?

  2. Lower solute outside, so water enters the cell
  3. Equal solute inside and out, so no net water shift
  4. No solute present, so water cannot move
  5. Water cannot cross the membrane at all
Answer: A. Water follows solute, so it exits toward the more concentrated exterior.Module 2 live-class deck, slide 12

Q105. Facilitated diffusion requires:

  2. A concentration gradient against the flow

Q106. Which best explains why the sodium-potassium pump is electrogenic?
  1. It exports 3 Na+ for every 2 K+ imported, leaving the interior more negative
  2. It exchanges equal numbers of sodium and potassium ions in each direction
  3. It transports only potassium ions inward and leaves sodium completely untouched
  4. It works without using any ATP, so it adds no net charge to the membrane
  5. It pumps water molecules rather than charged ions across the cell membrane
Answer: A. The unequal exchange contributes to the negative resting membrane potential.Lecture Outline, Module 2 — p.11
Q107. Apoptosis is best described as:
  1. Accidental cell death from injury
  2. Programd, controlled cell death
  3. Uncontrolled cell division and growth
  4. Failure of protein synthesis
  5. Loss of membrane potential
Answer: B. Apoptosis is an orderly, regulated dismantling of the cell.Lecture Outline, Module 2 — p.11
Q108. Alveoli are lined by a single layer of extremely flat cells. This arrangement exists because:

  4. It allows stretch the way transitional epithelium does

Answer: C. Simple squamous epithelium is one flat cell thick, which is ideal wherever rapid diffusion or filtration is needed.Module 3 live-class deck, slide 13
Q109. Which epithelium lines the urinary bladder and permits it to distend?

  2. Stratified squamous
  3. Simple columnar
  4. Transitional
  5. Pseudostratified columnar
Answer: D. Transitional epithelium changes cell shape as the organ fills, allowing stretch without tearing.Lecture Outline, Module 3 — p.20
Q110. The esophagus and skin surface are lined by which epithelium, and why?
  1. Simple squamous, for rapid diffusion and filtration
  2. Simple cuboidal, for secretion and absorption in ducts
  3. Transitional, for stretch as the bladder fills
  4. Simple columnar, for absorption in the small intestine
  5. Stratified squamous, for protection against abrasion
Answer: E. Multiple layers of cells absorb mechanical wear, which is why stratified squamous lines high-friction surfaces.Module 3 live-class deck, slide 16
Q111. Kidney tubules and many glands are lined by:
  1. Simple cuboidal
  2. Simple squamous
  3. Transitional
  4. Stratified squamous
  5. Pseudostratified columnar

Q112. The lining of the small intestine is simple columnar epithelium because this tissue:
  1. Resists abrasion better than any other epithelium

  3. Permits stretch as the organ fills with urine
  4. Allows rapid gas exchange across a thin barrier
  5. Filters blood plasma at the glomerular capsule
Answer: B. Tall columnar cells have room for the organelles and microvilli that absorption requires.Module 3 live-class deck, slide 6
Q113. Which epithelium lines the trachea and helps clear debris?

Q119. Bone's role as the body's principal mineral reservoir means it primarily stores:
  1. Iron and copper reserves

Q120. Which connective tissue would you expect at a site requiring great tensile strength in one direction, such as a tendon?

Q126. List the epidermal layers from DEEP to SUPERFICIAL:
  1. Basale, spinosum, granulosum, lucidum, corneum
  2. Corneum, lucidum, granulosum, spinosum, basale
  3. Basale, granulosum, spinosum, lucidum, corneum
  4. Spinosum, basale, corneum, granulosum, lucidum
  5. Granulosum, basale, spinosum, corneum, lucidum
Answer: A. Cells are born in the basale and migrate outward, dying and flattening as they go, ending as the corneum.Module 3 live-class deck, slide 13
Q127. Cell division in the epidermis occurs chiefly in the:
  1. Stratum corneum
  2. Stratum basale
  3. Stratum lucidum
  4. Stratum granulosum
  5. Stratum spinosum
Answer: B. The basale sits on the blood-supplied dermis and is the only layer with substantial mitotic activity.Module 3 live-class deck, slide 13
Q128. As keratinocytes migrate from the basale to the corneum, they:
  1. Enlarge and become more active
  2. Gain organelles and increase in volume
  3. Flatten, fill with keratin and die
  4. Begin dividing rapidly by mitosis
  5. Become melanocytes and make pigment
Answer: C. The outer layer is a sheet of dead, keratin-packed flattened cells forming a waterproof barrier.Module 3 live-class deck, slide 13
Q129. Why is the epidermis avascular?
  1. It has no metabolic need for either nutrients or oxygen
  2. Blood vessels would interfere with keratin formation
  3. It is dead tissue at every level, so needs no supply
  4. It receives nutrients by diffusion from the dermis below
  5. Its cells manufacture their own glucose and oxygen
Answer: D. Vessels live in the dermis; the epidermis is fed by diffusion, which is one reason its outer cells die.Module 3 live-class deck, slide 14
Q130. Which skin layer contains blood vessels, nerve endings, hair follicles and glands?
  1. Epidermis
  2. Stratum corneum

  4. Stratum lucidum

Answer: E. The dermis is the vascular, innervated connective tissue layer beneath the epidermis.Module 3 live-class deck, slide 14
Q131. The role of dermal blood vessels in temperature regulation is to:

  4. Endocrine

Q134. Merocrine (eccrine) sweat glands release secretions by:

Q137. The protective function of melanin is to:

  2. Absorb ultraviolet radiation and shield DNA

  4. Reduce water loss across the skin surface

Q139. Place the vitamin D pathway in correct order:

  2. Kidney → liver → skin
  3. Skin → kidney → liver
  4. Skin → liver → kidney
  5. Liver → kidney → skin

Q141. The principal physiological action of activated vitamin D is to:

Q142. A patient with chronic kidney disease develops bone weakness. The most likely mechanism is:

Q160. Long bones are built around a hollow medullary cavity primarily because this design:

Q162. The diploe of a flat bone refers to:

Q167. Mature bone cells that sit in lacunae and monitor the matrix are:

Q168. Osteoprogenitor cells are important because they:

Q169. Canaliculi serve to:

Q170. Approximately what fraction of bone weight is mineral, and what is the rest?
  1. One-third calcium phosphate, two-thirds collagen
  2. All mineral, with no organic collagen framework
  3. All collagen, with no mineral component
  4. Half calcium phosphate and half stored marrow fat
  5. Two-thirds calcium phosphate, one-third collagen
Answer: E. The mineral gives compressive hardness; collagen gives tensile flexibility. Lose either and bone fails.Module 4 bones deck, slide 2
Q171. A defect in collagen production would most likely make bone:
  1. Brittle and prone to fracture despite normal mineral content
  2. Softer and more flexible than normal, bending under load
  3. Unable to store calcium, causing a fall in blood calcium levels
  4. Unable to produce red marrow, halting blood cell formation

Answer: A. Without collagen's tensile framework, mineralized bone shatters rather than bending — as in osteogenesis imperfecta.Module 4 bones deck, slide 2
Q172. Endochondral ossification is defined by:

Q174. The first step of intramembranous ossification is:

Q176. Closure of the epiphyseal plates results in:

Q177. Which bones form by endochondral ossification?

Q178. Blood calcium falls. Which hormone is released and what is its effect?

  2. Insulin; lowers blood calcium and glucose
  3. Parathyroid hormone; raises blood calcium
  4. Calcitriol; lowers blood calcium sharply
  5. Aldosterone; raises blood calcium and sodium
Answer: C. PTH stimulates osteoclasts, boosts intestinal absorption via vitamin D, and reduces renal calcium loss.Module 4 bones deck, slide 12
Q179. Blood calcium rises above the set point. The expected response is:
  1. PTH release and osteoclast activation in bone
  2. No change, since blood calcium is not regulated
  3. Increased renal calcium reabsorption by the kidney tubules
  4. Calcitonin release and calcium deposition into bone
  5. Increased vitamin D activation and gut absorption
Answer: D. Calcitonin TONES DOWN blood calcium by inhibiting osteoclasts and favoring deposition.Module 4 bones deck, slide 12
Q180. Parathyroid hormone raises blood calcium by acting on which three targets?
  1. Bone, liver, lung
  2. Kidney, pancreas, spleen
  3. Intestine, thyroid, skin
  4. Bone, heart, muscle
  5. Bone, intestine, kidney
Answer: E. PTH mobilizes bone calcium, increases gut absorption indirectly via calcitriol, and reduces urinary loss.Module 4 bones deck, slide 12
Q181. Which cell does parathyroid hormone ultimately stimulate to release calcium from bone?
