  2. Osteoblast
  3. Osteocyte
  4. Osteoprogenitor
  5. Chondrocyte
Answer: A. Osteoclasts perform resorption, freeing stored calcium into the circulation.Module 4 bones deck, slide 13
Q182. Bone remodeling differs from bone growth in that remodeling:
  1. Adds new length to the bone at the epiphyseal plate
  2. Continuously replaces existing bone without changing overall size
  3. Occurs only during childhood and stops at skeletal maturity
  4. Requires osteoblasts alone, with no osteoclast resorption involved
  5. Happens only after a fracture, as part of callus formation
Answer: B. Remodeling is lifelong maintenance — osteoclasts resorb and osteoblasts rebuild in balance.Module 4 bones deck, slide 14
Q183. Weight-bearing exercise increases bone density because mechanical loading:
  1. Inhibits osteoblasts and slows matrix deposition
  2. Destroys osteocytes trapped within the lacunae
  3. Stimulates osteoblast activity and matrix deposition
  4. Reduces intestinal calcium absorption from food in the gut
  5. Closes the epiphyseal plate and halts remodeling
Answer: C. Strain sensed by osteocytes signals osteoblasts to strengthen the loaded region.Module 4 bones deck, slide 14
Q184. Prolonged bed rest or spaceflight causes bone loss because:
  1. Dietary calcium intake falls below the daily requirement
  2. Osteoblasts are permanently destroyed and cannot be replaced from stem cells
  3. Vitamin D can no longer be activated by the kidney and skin
  4. Absent mechanical loading shifts the remodeling balance toward resorption
  5. Parathyroid hormone secretion stops and stored calcium is not mobilized
Answer: D. Without loading, osteoclast activity outpaces osteoblast activity and net bone is lost.Module 4 bones deck, slide 12
Q185. A postmenopausal patient develops osteoporosis. The underlying imbalance is:
  1. Osteoblast activity exceeding osteoclast activity
  2. Excess calcitonin from the thyroid gland
  3. Excess vitamin D driving heavy over-mineralization
  4. Reopening of the epiphyseal growth plates
  5. Osteoclast activity exceeding osteoblast activity
Answer: E. Falling estrogen removes a brake on osteoclasts, so resorption outstrips formation.Lecture Outline, Module 4 — p.36
Q186. Vitamin D contributes to bone health mainly by:
  1. Increasing intestinal calcium absorption so calcium is available for mineralization
  2. Directly hardening the bone matrix by binding calcium tightly onto the collagen fibers
  3. Blocking osteoclasts entirely so that bone resorption cannot occur
  4. Producing the collagen framework on which bone mineral is laid down
  5. Closing the growth plates once full skeletal maturity is reached
Answer: A. Without adequate calcium uptake, bone cannot mineralize properly — producing rickets or osteomalacia.Module 4 bones deck, slide 12
Q187. Which pairing is correct?
  1. PTH lowers calcium; calcitonin raises it
  2. PTH raises calcium; calcitonin lowers it
  3. Both hormones raise blood calcium
  4. Both hormones lower blood calcium
  5. Neither hormone affects blood calcium levels
Answer: B. They are opposing arms of a negative feedback system holding blood calcium in range.Module 4 bones deck, slide 12
Q188. The periosteum functions to:
  1. Line the medullary cavity and cover all internal bone surfaces
  2. Produce synovial fluid to lubricate the joint cavity
  3. Cover the outer bone surface and supply nerves and vessels
  4. Store yellow marrow within the central shaft cavity
  5. Form the hyaline cartilage that covers joint surfaces
Answer: C. It is the vascular, innervated outer covering essential for repair.Module 4 bones deck, slide 7
Q189. Yellow bone marrow primarily stores:
  1. Red blood cells
  2. Calcium
  3. Collagen
  4. Fat
  5. Platelets
Answer: D. Yellow marrow is largely adipose; red marrow makes blood cells.Module 4 bones deck, slide 3
Q190. Which cell arises from a monocyte lineage?
  1. Osteoblast
  2. Osteocyte
  3. Osteoprogenitor cell
  4. Chondrocyte
  5. Osteoclast
Answer: E. Osteoclasts are multinucleated cells of monocyte-macrophage origin.Lecture Outline, Module 4 — p.36
Q191. Osteons (Haversian systems) are characteristic of:
  1. Compact bone
  2. Spongy bone
  3. Hyaline cartilage
  4. Periosteal layer
  5. Red bone marrow
Answer: A. Compact bone is organized into concentric lamellae around a central canal.Lecture Outline, Module 4 — p.36
Q192. Spongy bone differs from compact bone in that it:
  1. Is organized into osteons around central canals
  2. Is arranged in trabeculae along lines of stress
  3. Contains no living cells within its matrix
  4. Is completely avascular and lacks a blood supply
  5. Cannot remodel once it has been fully laid down
Answer: B. Trabecular struts follow stress lines, giving strength with low weight.Module 4 bones deck, slide 3
Q193. Which vitamin is essential for the synthesis of bone matrix proteins and clotting factors?
  1. Vitamin A
  2. Vitamin C
  3. Vitamin K
  4. Vitamin E
  5. Vitamin B12
Answer: C. Vitamin K is required for carboxylation of osteocalcin and clotting factors.Lecture Outline, Module 4 — p.36
Q194. Vitamin C deficiency weakens bone because it impairs:
  1. Calcium absorption
  2. Osteoclast function
  3. Marrow production
  4. Collagen synthesis
  5. Vitamin D activation
Answer: D. Vitamin C is a cofactor for collagen cross-linking.Lecture Outline, Module 4 — p.36
Q195. The epiphyseal line indicates:
  1. Active longitudinal growth at the plate
  2. A healing fracture through the epiphysis
  3. Osteoporosis with severely reduced bone density
  4. Marrow cavity within the diaphysis
  5. Growth plate closure after skeletal maturity
Answer: E. When the cartilage plate ossifies it leaves a bony line.Module 4 bones deck, slide 8
Q196. Growth hormone affects bone chiefly by:
  1. Stimulating cartilage proliferation at the growth plate
  2. Stimulating osteoclasts to resorb the bone matrix
  3. Lowering blood calcium by depositing it into bone
  4. Blocking ossification of the cartilage template
  5. Reducing overall bone density by inhibiting osteoblasts
Answer: A. GH drives longitudinal growth before the plates close.Lecture Outline, Module 4 — p.36
Q197. A drop in blood calcium triggers which sequence?
  1. Calcitonin release, calcium deposition into bone
  2. PTH release, osteoclast activation, calcium release
  3. Insulin release, glucose uptake into body cells
  4. ADH release, water retention by the kidney tubules
  5. Aldosterone release, sodium retention and potassium loss
Answer: B. PTH mobilizes calcium from bone to restore blood levels.Module 4 bones deck, slide 12
Q198. Which best explains why immobilized limbs lose bone mass?
  1. Dietary calcium intake falls well below the daily requirement
  2. Osteoblasts in the immobilized limb die off and are not replaced
  3. Absent mechanical loading shifts remodeling toward resorption
  4. Vitamin D stops activating calcium absorption in the intestine
  5. Red marrow is gradually lost from the immobilized bone
Answer: C. Osteocytes sense strain; without it, formation lags behind resorption.Lecture Outline, Module 4 — p.36
Q199. Fracture repair begins with formation of a:
  1. Bony callus
  2. Cartilage model
  3. Growth plate
  4. Hematoma
  5. Osteon
Answer: D. Bleeding forms a hematoma, followed by soft then bony callus.Lecture Outline, Module 4 — p.36
Q200. Which hormone pair are direct antagonists in calcium regulation?
  1. Insulin and glucagon
  2. ADH and aldosterone
  3. FSH and LH
  4. GH and cortisol
  5. PTH and calcitonin
Answer: E. PTH raises blood calcium; calcitonin lowers it.Module 4 bones deck, slide 13
Q201. Osteomalacia in adults results from:
  1. Inadequate mineralization, usually from vitamin D deficiency
  2. Excess calcitonin from the parafollicular cells
  3. Too many osteoblasts laying down excess bone matrix
  4. Excess growth hormone acting on the closed adult growth plates
  5. Premature closure of the epiphyseal plates in childhood
Answer: A. Without adequate calcium uptake the matrix cannot mineralize properly.Lecture Outline, Module 4 — p.36
Q202. Which describes intramembranous ossification correctly?
  1. A cartilage model is gradually replaced by bone
  2. Bone forms directly within a fibrous membrane
  3. It forms the long bones of the limbs
  4. It requires an epiphyseal plate to proceed
  5. It occurs only after birth, never in the fetus
Answer: B. Mesenchymal cells become osteoblasts with no cartilage precursor.Module 4 bones deck, slide 10
Q203. Which muscle type is striated, multinucleated and under voluntary control?
  1. Smooth
  2. Cardiac
  3. Skeletal
  4. Visceral
  5. Myoepithelial
Answer: C. Skeletal muscle fibers fuse during development, giving many nuclei per cell, and are consciously controlled.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 2
Q204. Which muscle type is non-striated, has a single nucleus, and can regenerate?
  1. Skeletal
  2. Cardiac
  3. Striated voluntary
  4. Smooth
  5. Sarcomeric
Answer: D. Smooth muscle is the only one of the three with meaningful regenerative capacity.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q205. Cardiac muscle resists fatigue extremely well because it:
  1. Contracts only in brief bursts and then stops completely for minutes at a time
  2. Has no nuclei and therefore expends no energy on protein synthesis
  3. Uses only anaerobic glycolysis, which never depletes its glycogen stores
  4. Never fully contracts, so its fibers are only partially activated each beat
  5. Contains a very high density of mitochondria and relies on aerobic metabolism
Answer: E. Continuous aerobic ATP production allows the heart to work without rest for a lifetime.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 2
Q206. Automaticity — the ability to depolarize without external stimulation — is a property of:
  1. Cardiac muscle
  2. Skeletal muscle
  3. All three types equally
  4. Neither smooth nor cardiac
  5. Tendons and ligaments
Answer: A. Cardiac pacemaker tissue depolarizes spontaneously, setting the heart's intrinsic rhythm.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q207. Smooth muscle in the walls of arterioles primarily functions to:
  1. Move the skeleton by pulling on tendons attached to bone
  2. Regulate vessel diameter and therefore blood flow and pressure
  3. Generate the heartbeat through spontaneous pacemaker depolarization
  4. Maintain upright posture through sustained tonic contraction
  5. Produce heat by shivering to raise core body temperature
Answer: B. Contraction narrows the lumen (vasoconstriction); relaxation widens it (vasodilation).Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 2
Q208. Which is NOT a function of skeletal muscle?
  1. Maintaining posture through tonic contraction
  2. Generating heat through shivering
  3. Generating the intrinsic heartbeat
  4. Guarding openings via sphincters
  5. Stabilizing joints during movement
Answer: C. The heartbeat originates in cardiac pacemaker tissue, not skeletal muscle.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q209. Cardiac and skeletal muscle heal from serious injury largely by:
  1. Complete regeneration back to normal contractile tissue
  2. Converting the damaged fibers into smooth muscle
  3. Rapid mitotic division of mature muscle cells
  4. Scar formation, since neither regenerates well
  5. Producing new nuclei to replace the damaged ones
Answer: D. This is why myocardial infarction leaves permanent non-contractile scar.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 2
Q210. Which neurotransmitter is released at the neuromuscular junction?
  1. Norepinephrine
  2. Dopamine
  3. GABA
  4. Serotonin
  5. Acetylcholine
Answer: E. The motor neuron releases ACh, which binds receptors on the motor end plate.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 1
Q211. Which ion must enter the presynaptic terminal to trigger neurotransmitter release?
  1. Calcium
  2. Sodium
  3. Potassium
  4. Chloride
  5. Magnesium
Answer: A. Calcium influx causes synaptic vesicles to fuse with the membrane and release their contents.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 9
Q212. Acetylcholinesterase is essential because it:
  1. Releases ACh from the presynaptic vesicles
  2. Breaks down ACh to terminate the signal
  3. Synthesizes ACh from choline and acetyl-CoA
  4. Transports ACh back into the presynaptic neuron
  5. Blocks calcium entry at the nerve terminal
Answer: B. Without AChE the muscle would remain stimulated — the mechanism behind some nerve agents.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 16
Q213. The role of T-tubules in contraction is to:
  1. Store the calcium reserve and release it around the myofibrils the moment the fiber is depolarized
  2. Break down ATP to supply the energy needed for every cross-bridge cycle and the recovery stroke
  3. Carry the action potential deep into the fiber so the whole cell contracts together
  4. Manufacture acetylcholine and release it into the cleft at the motor end plate
  5. Attach the muscle to bone so that force generated is transmitted to the skeleton
Answer: C. T-tubules are membrane invaginations that ensure a synchronous contraction throughout the fiber.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 16
Q214. Calcium released during contraction is stored in the:
  1. Mitochondrial cristae and matrix
  2. Transverse tubule network
  3. Cell nucleus
  4. Sarcoplasmic reticulum
  5. Golgi apparatus
Answer: D. The sarcoplasmic reticulum sequesters calcium and releases it on depolarization.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 9
Q215. ATP is required in the cross-bridge cycle chiefly to:
  1. Form the cross-bridge by binding myosin to actin
  2. Release acetylcholine from the motor nerve terminal
  3. Depolarize the sarcolemma and open sodium channels
  4. Bind calcium to troponin so that tropomyosin shifts aside
  5. Detach myosin from actin and re-cock the myosin head
Answer: E. This is why rigor mortis occurs — without ATP, myosin cannot release actin.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q216. Place these in correct order: (1) ACh binds receptor (2) Ca2+ released from SR (3) action potential travels down motor neuron (4) cross-bridges form
  1. 3,1,2,4
  2. 1,2,3,4
  3. 2,3,1,4
  4. 3,2,1,4
  5. 4,3,2,1
Answer: A. Nerve impulse → ACh release and binding → depolarization → calcium release → cross-bridge formation.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 16
Q217. Muscle relaxation requires ATP because calcium must be:
  1. Released from the sarcoplasmic reticulum into the cytosol
  2. Actively pumped back into the sarcoplasmic reticulum
  3. Bound to acetylcholine released at the end plate
  4. Excreted from the cell and lost as metabolic waste
  5. Converted into sodium ions inside the fiber
Answer: B. Lowering cytoplasmic calcium is an active process, so relaxation also costs energy.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 9
Q218. Glycolysis occurs in the ____, requires ____, and yields ____ ATP.
  1. mitochondria; oxygen; ~32
  2. cytoplasm; oxygen; ~32
  3. cytoplasm; no oxygen; ~2
  4. mitochondria; no oxygen; ~2
  5. nucleus; oxygen; ~4
Answer: C. Glycolysis is fast and anaerobic but produces very little ATP per glucose.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 10
Q219. Aerobic respiration occurs in the ____ and yields approximately ____ ATP per glucose.
  1. cytoplasm; 2-4
  2. nucleus; 10-12
  3. sarcoplasmic reticulum; 8
  4. mitochondria; 30-32
  5. Golgi apparatus; 4
Answer: D. Complete oxidation in the mitochondrion is far more efficient than glycolysis alone.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 10
Q220. In the absence of adequate oxygen, pyruvate is converted to:
  1. Glucose
  2. Acetyl-CoA
  3. Glycogen
  4. Creatine phosphate
  5. Lactic acid
Answer: E. Converting pyruvate to lactate regenerates NAD+ so glycolysis can continue briefly.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 10
Q221. The immediate energy source for a muscle contraction is:
  1. ATP
  2. Glucose
  3. Glycogen
  4. Fatty acid
  5. Creatine
Answer: A. ATP is the only molecule the contractile apparatus can use directly; everything else must be converted to it.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 9
Q222. A sprinter running 100 meters relies predominantly on:
  1. Aerobic respiration
  2. Anaerobic glycolysis
  3. Beta-oxidation
  4. Protein catabolism
  5. Oxidative phosphorylation only
Answer: B. Short maximal efforts outrun oxygen delivery, so anaerobic glycolysis dominates and lactate accumulates.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q223. The burning sensation during intense exercise is most associated with:
  1. Excess oxygen in the muscle
  2. Calcium depletion in the fiber
  3. Lactic acid accumulation
  4. Excess acetylcholine release
  5. Glycogen storage in the fiber
Answer: C. Anaerobic metabolism produces lactate and hydrogen ions, lowering local pH.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q224. The primary function of a neuron is to:
  1. Produce the myelin sheath
  2. Phagocytose cellular debris
  3. Form the blood-brain barrier
  4. Transmit electrical signals
  5. Produce cerebrospinal fluid
Answer: D. Neurons are the signaling cells; glia are the support cells.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 12
Q225. Which glial cell maintains the blood-brain barrier and provides structural support?
  1. Microglia
  2. Oligodendrocyte
  3. Schwann cell
  4. Ependymal cell
  5. Astrocyte
Answer: E. Astrocytes wrap capillaries and regulate what enters the CNS.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 19
Q226. Which glial cell acts as the resident immune cell of the CNS?
  1. Microglia
  2. Astrocyte
  3. Oligodendrocyte
  4. Schwann cell
  5. Satellite cell
Answer: A. Microglia phagocytose pathogens and cellular debris in the central nervous system.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 22
Q227. Which glial cell produces and circulates cerebrospinal fluid?
  1. Astrocyte
  2. Ependymal cell
  3. Microglia
  4. Oligodendrocyte
  5. Schwann cell
Answer: B. Ependymal cells line the ventricles and their cilia help circulate CSF.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 21
Q228. Myelin in the PERIPHERAL nervous system is produced by:
  1. Oligodendrocytes
  2. Astrocytes
  3. Schwann cells
  4. Microglia
  5. Ependymal cells
Answer: C. Schwann cells myelinate PNS axons, one cell per internodal segment.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 23
Q229. Myelin in the CENTRAL nervous system is produced by:
  1. Schwann cells
  2. Fibrous astrocytes
  3. Microglial cells
  4. Oligodendrocytes
  5. Satellite glial cells
Answer: D. One oligodendrocyte can myelinate segments of several different CNS axons.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 20
Q230. The physiological benefit of myelin is that it:
  1. Slows conduction along the axon so that arriving signals are spaced out more accurately
  2. Produces the neurotransmitter that is packaged into vesicles at the synaptic terminal
  3. Generates the ATP that powers the sodium-potassium pumps along the axon
  4. Prevents action potentials from ever reaching the axon terminal button
  5. Insulates the axon so the impulse jumps node to node, greatly increasing speed
Answer: E. Saltatory conduction is far faster than continuous conduction along an unmyelinated axon.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 20
Q231. Demyelination, as in multiple sclerosis, would most directly cause:
  1. Slowed or blocked nerve conduction
  2. Faster than normal nerve conduction
  3. Increased neurotransmitter release
  4. Increased ATP production in the axon
  5. Loss of peripheral Schwann cells only
Answer: A. Without insulation, current leaks and saltatory conduction fails.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 20
Q232. Which neuron type carries impulses from the CNS to muscles and glands?
  1. Sensory (afferent)
  2. Motor (efferent)
  3. Interneuron
  4. Bipolar
  5. Unipolar sensory
Answer: B. Efferent means 'exits' — motor neurons carry commands outward.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 12
Q233. Which neuron type integrates information entirely within the CNS?
  1. Sensory
  2. Motor
  3. Interneuron
  4. Bipolar
  5. Multipolar motor
Answer: C. Interneurons connect sensory input to motor output and perform CNS processing.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 19
Q234. Bipolar neurons are characteristically found in:
  1. Skeletal muscle fibers, clustered at the motor end plate of the neuromuscular junction
  2. The anterior horn of the spinal cord, where the large alpha motor neuron bodies sit
  3. Cardiac conduction tissue such as the SA node and the AV node
  4. Special sense organs such as the retina and olfactory epithelium
  5. Red bone marrow and the periosteum that covers long bones
Answer: D. Their one-dendrite, one-axon structure suits direct sensory transduction.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 14
Q235. An inhibitory neurotransmitter makes the postsynaptic neuron:
  1. More likely to fire by depolarizing it toward threshold
  2. Fire immediately, regardless of its threshold voltage
  3. Release calcium from its internal store
  4. Produce extra myelin around its own axon
  5. Less likely to fire by hyperpolarizing it
Answer: E. Hyperpolarization moves membrane potential further from threshold.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 17
Q236. Which neurotransmitter is most associated with reward, motivation and motor control?
  1. Dopamine
  2. Acetylcholine
  3. GABA
  4. Glycine
  5. Histamine
Answer: A. Dopamine deficiency in the substantia nigra produces the motor signs of Parkinson disease.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 17
Q237. Epinephrine and norepinephrine are chiefly associated with:
  1. Parasympathetic rest-and-digest activity
  2. Sympathetic fight-or-flight activity
  3. Neuromuscular transmission at the end plate
  4. Myelin production by oligodendrocytes
  5. Cerebrospinal fluid secretion by ependymal cells
Answer: B. They are the principal transmitters and hormones of the sympathetic response.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q238. An electrical synapse differs from a chemical synapse in that it:
  1. Releases a far larger quantity of neurotransmitter into the cleft with each impulse
  2. Is slower to transmit because the signal must diffuse across a wide synaptic cleft
  3. Passes current directly through gap junctions with essentially no delay
  4. Occurs only in the brain and nowhere else in the body's tissues
  5. Requires far more ATP to reset presynaptic terminals after every impulse
Answer: C. Gap junctions allow near-instantaneous coupling — essential in cardiac muscle.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 17
Q239. Where in the body would you most expect to find electrical synapses?
  1. Skeletal neuromuscular junctions
  2. Sensory receptors in the skin and joints
  3. The retina and optic nerve only
  4. Cardiac muscle and some smooth muscle
  5. Bone and dense connective tissue
Answer: D. Cardiac myocytes must depolarize as a unit, which gap junctions make possible.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 2
Q240. A toxin that blocks calcium channels at the presynaptic terminal would:
  1. Increase neurotransmitter release and cause sustained muscle spasm
  2. Speed action potential conduction along the motor neuron axon
  3. Increase myelination of the peripheral nerve fibers
  4. Have no effect, since sodium alone triggers release
  5. Prevent neurotransmitter release and cause paralysis
Answer: E. Calcium entry is the trigger for vesicle fusion, so blocking it silences the synapse.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 9
Q241. The functional contractile unit of skeletal muscle is the:
  1. Sarcomere
  2. Myofibril
  3. Sarcolemma
  4. Motor unit
  5. Fascicle
Answer: A. A sarcomere spans Z-disc to Z-disc and shortens during contraction.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q242. During contraction, which band shortens?
  1. A band
  2. I band
  3. The thick filaments themselves
  4. The Z-disc itself
  5. The M line
Answer: B. Thin filaments slide inward, narrowing the I band; the A band length is unchanged.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q243. Calcium initiates contraction by binding:
  1. Myosin heads directly on the thick filament
  2. Actin directly, exposing the myosin-binding sites
  3. Troponin, moving tropomyosin off the binding sites
  4. ATP already bound to the head of the myosin molecule
  5. Acetylcholine on the motor end plate receptors
Answer: C. Troponin-tropomyosin shift exposes actin so cross-bridges can form.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 9
Q244. A motor unit consists of:
  1. One muscle fiber and the many neurons supplying it
  2. All of the fibers contained within one whole muscle
  3. One sarcomere spanning Z-disc to Z-disc
  4. One motor neuron and all fibers it innervates
  5. The neuromuscular junction and its receptors only
Answer: D. Fine control comes from small motor units with few fibers each.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 12
Q245. Rigor mortis occurs because:
  1. Calcium is actively pumped back into the reticulum
  2. Acetylcholine floods the neuromuscular junction
  3. Tropomyosin permanently covers the actin binding sites
  4. Motor neurons keep firing after death
  5. Without ATP, myosin cannot detach from actin
Answer: E. ATP is required to break the cross-bridge, so muscles stay locked.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q246. Creatine phosphate serves to:
  1. Rapidly regenerate ATP at the start of exercise
  2. Store oxygen for use during aerobic metabolism
  3. Buffer the lactic acid produced by glycolysis
  4. Bind calcium released from the sarcoplasmic reticulum
  5. Produce myoglobin for oxygen storage in the fiber
Answer: A. It donates a phosphate to ADP for an immediate but brief ATP supply.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q247. Myoglobin functions in muscle to:
  1. Contract
  2. Store oxygen
  3. Break down glucose
  4. Bind calcium
  5. Conduct impulses
Answer: B. Myoglobin holds an oxygen reserve for aerobic metabolism.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q248. Which fiber type is best suited to endurance activity?
  1. Fast glycolytic, with large glycogen stores
  2. Smooth muscle of the walls of hollow organs
  3. Slow oxidative, rich in mitochondria and myoglobin
  4. Cardiac muscle fibers of the ventricular wall
  5. Fast oxidative, used only for brief maximal sprints
Answer: C. Slow oxidative fibers resist fatigue through aerobic metabolism.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q249. Nodes of Ranvier are:
  1. Segments of myelin wrapped tightly around the length of the axon
  2. Synaptic vesicles that store neurotransmitter before release
  3. Branch points where the dendrites receive input
  4. Gaps between myelin where the impulse regenerates
  5. Nuclei of the glial cells that wrap the axon
Answer: D. Voltage-gated channels cluster here, enabling saltatory conduction.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 20
Q250. The axon hillock is important because it:
  1. Receives neurotransmitter released from presynaptic neurons
  2. Manufactures the myelin sheath that insulates the whole axon
  3. Is where calcium is stored ready for vesicle fusion
  4. Releases neurotransmitter into the synaptic cleft
  5. Is the trigger zone where the action potential begins
Answer: E. It has the lowest threshold and integrates incoming graded potentials.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 16
Q251. Which structure receives incoming signals in a typical neuron?
  1. Dendrites
  2. Axon terminal
  3. Myelin sheath
  4. Node of Ranvier
  5. Synaptic cleft
Answer: A. Dendrites collect input and carry graded potentials toward the cell body.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 12
Q252. A neurotransmitter is removed from the synapse by all EXCEPT:
  1. Enzymatic breakdown in the cleft
  2. Permanent binding to the receptor
  3. Reuptake into the presynaptic neuron
  4. Diffusion away from the synapse
  5. Uptake by surrounding glial cells
Answer: B. Signals must terminate; permanent binding would prevent that.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 17
Q253. Multiple sclerosis primarily damages:
  1. Muscle fibers at the neuromuscular junction
  2. Acetylcholine receptors at the end plate
  3. Myelin in the central nervous system
  4. Bone and the surrounding joint tissue
  5. Blood vessels supplying the brain
Answer: C. Demyelination slows or blocks conduction, producing varied deficits.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 23
Q254. Which best describes summation at a synapse?
  1. A single presynaptic input is always enough on its own to trigger firing
  2. Action potentials vary in size according to how strong the stimulus is
  3. Calcium entry blocks transmitter release at nerve terminals
  4. Multiple graded potentials add together to reach threshold
  5. Myelin around the axon prevents synaptic signaling
Answer: D. Temporal and spatial summation combine subthreshold inputs.Lecture Outline, Module 5 — p.49
Q255. Smooth muscle contraction differs from skeletal in that it:
  1. Uses no calcium in the contraction process
  2. Has sarcomeres arranged in neat rows
  3. Requires no ATP to shorten the fiber
  4. Cannot regenerate after injury
  5. Is slower, sustained, and involuntary
Answer: E. Smooth muscle contracts slowly and can maintain tone for long periods.Module 5 muscles & nerves deck, slide 13
Q256. List the meninges from OUTERMOST to innermost:
  1. Dura, arachnoid, pia
  2. Pia, arachnoid, dura
  3. Arachnoid, dura, pia
  4. Dura, pia, arachnoid
  5. Pia, dura, arachnoid
Answer: A. Dura mater (tough outer), arachnoid mater (web-like middle), pia mater (delicate, hugs the brain surface).Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 6
Q257. Cerebrospinal fluid circulates within which space?
  1. Epidural space
  2. Subarachnoid space
  3. Subdural space
  4. Between dura and skull
  5. Within the pia only
Answer: B. CSF fills the subarachnoid space, cushioning the brain within the cranium.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 6
Q258. A principal function of CSF is to:
  1. Deliver oxygen and glucose directly to neurons in place of the cerebral blood supply
  2. Produce the myelin sheaths that insulate axons in the white matter tracts
  3. Cushion the brain and provide buoyancy so it does not compress under its own weight
  4. Generate the action potentials that carry signals along cortical axons
  5. Store calcium and magnesium that muscles draw on during contraction
Answer: C. Buoyancy reduces the brain's effective weight enormously, protecting deep structures.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 5
Q259. White matter appears white because it consists mainly of:
  1. Neuron cell bodies
  2. Glial nuclei
  3. Blood vessels
  4. Myelinated axons
  5. Cerebrospinal fluid
Answer: D. Lipid-rich myelin is white; its function is rapid transmission between regions.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 7
Q260. Gray matter is composed chiefly of ____ and functions in ____.
  1. myelinated axons and fiber tracts; long-distance transmission
  2. glial cells and their processes; structural support
  3. blood vessels and capillaries; oxygen and nutrient delivery
  4. cerebrospinal fluid; cushioning and buoyancy of the brain
  5. neuron cell bodies and dendrites; information processing
Answer: E. Cell bodies do the computing; axons do the transmitting.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 7
Q261. The resting membrane potential of a typical neuron is approximately:
  1. -70 mV
  2. 0 mV
  3. -40 mV
  4. -55 mV
  5. +30 mV
Answer: A. About -70 mV, maintained largely by the Na+/K+ pump and potassium leak channels.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 3
Q262. Threshold potential is approximately:
  1. -90 mV
  2. -55 mV
  3. -70 mV
  4. 0 mV
  5. +30 mV
Answer: B. At roughly -55 mV, voltage-gated sodium channels open and the all-or-none action potential fires.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 13
Q263. Depolarization of the axon membrane is caused by:
  1. K+ moving out
  2. Cl- moving in
  3. Na+ moving in
  4. Ca2+ moving out
  5. Na+ moving out
Answer: C. Sodium rushes down its electrochemical gradient into the cell, making the interior more positive.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 2
Q264. Repolarization occurs when:
  1. Na+ enters the cell
  2. Cl- exits the cell
  3. Ca2+ enters the cell
  4. K+ exits the cell
  5. The Na+/K+ pump reverses
Answer: D. Potassium efflux restores the negative interior after the sodium spike.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q265. Hyperpolarization occurs because:
  1. Voltage-gated sodium channels reopen and admit a second wave of sodium
  2. Calcium floods into the axon through voltage-gated calcium channels
  3. The sodium-potassium pump stops and the gradients collapse entirely
  4. Chloride leaves the cell and carries negative charge outward
  5. Potassium continues to leave, driving the potential below resting
Answer: E. Slow-closing potassium channels overshoot, taking the membrane to about -90 mV.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q266. The sodium-potassium pump restores resting potential by moving:
  1. Na+ out and K+ in
  2. Na+ in and K+ out
  3. Both ions outward
  4. Both ions inward
  5. Only potassium inward
Answer: A. Three sodium out and two potassium in per ATP, re-establishing the ionic gradients.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 3
Q267. An action potential is described as all-or-none, meaning:
  1. It varies in size in direct proportion to the strength of the stimulus
  2. Once threshold is reached the response is complete and of fixed amplitude
  3. It can be partially generated when the stimulus is below threshold
  4. It occurs only in myelinated axons that have nodes of Ranvier
  5. It requires no ATP because the ion gradients never need restoring
Answer: B. Subthreshold stimuli produce only graded potentials; at threshold the full spike fires.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 13
Q268. A graded potential differs from an action potential because a graded potential:
  1. Is always larger in amplitude than an action potential
  2. Cannot be summed with other graded potentials at the axon hillock
  3. Varies in amplitude and may fail to reach threshold
  4. Occurs only in axons and never in dendrites or cell bodies
  5. Requires no ion movement across the plasma membrane
Answer: C. Graded potentials decay with distance and can summate to reach threshold.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 3
Q269. Saltatory conduction refers to impulses:
  1. Traveling continuously along an unmyelinated axon
  2. Moving backward along the axon toward the cell body
  3. Crossing a chemical synapse by way of neurotransmitter release
  4. Jumping between nodes of Ranvier on a myelinated axon
  5. Passing directly through gap junctions at an electrical synapse
Answer: D. Jumping node to node is dramatically faster and more energy-efficient than continuous conduction.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 2
Q270. During the absolute refractory period, the neuron:
  1. Fires more easily than usual because the membrane is already depolarized
  2. Fires spontaneously without any incoming synaptic stimulation
  3. Hyperpolarizes indefinitely and never returns to resting potential
  4. Loses its myelin sheath and must rebuild it before firing again
  5. Cannot fire another action potential regardless of stimulus strength
Answer: E. Sodium channels are inactivated, which also ensures the impulse travels in one direction only.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 3
Q271. Sympathetic activation produces which set of effects?
  1. Increased heart rate, decreased digestion, dilated pupils
  2. Decreased heart rate, increased digestion, constricted pupils
  3. Decreased heart rate and blood pressure
  4. Increased digestion and salivation
  5. Bronchoconstriction and bradycardia
Answer: A. Fight-or-flight prioritizes muscle and brain perfusion and suspends non-urgent functions like digestion.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 9
Q272. Parasympathetic activation is best summarized as:
  1. Fight or flight
  2. Rest and digest
  3. Thermoregulation
  4. Fever response
  5. Inflammatory response
Answer: B. It conserves energy, slows the heart, and promotes digestion and elimination.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q273. Sympathetic stimulation affects the pupils by causing:
  1. Constriction (miosis)
  2. No change
  3. Dilation (mydriasis)
  4. Lens thickening
  5. Tear production
Answer: C. Pupil dilation admits more light, improving visual detection during threat.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q274. The effect of parasympathetic stimulation on the digestive tract is to:
  1. Inhibit peristalsis and secretion
  2. Cause vasoconstriction of gut vessels
  3. Halt salivation
  4. Stimulate peristalsis and secretion
  5. Close all sphincters
Answer: D. Rest-and-digest actively promotes gastrointestinal activity.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q275. Sympathetic activation typically raises blood pressure by:
  1. Causing vasodilation of arterioles together with bradycardia
  2. Reducing stroke volume and lowering total peripheral resistance
  3. Increasing urine output and reducing circulating blood volume
  4. Decreasing cardiac output and slowing conduction at the SA node
  5. Increasing heart rate and causing widespread vasoconstriction
Answer: E. Higher cardiac output plus higher peripheral resistance raises arterial pressure.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 9
Q276. An alternative name for the sympathetic division reflecting its anatomy is:
  1. Thoracolumbar
  2. Craniosacral
  3. Enteric division
  4. Somatic efferent
  5. Craniocervical
Answer: A. Sympathetic fibers emerge from thoracic and lumbar spinal segments; parasympathetic are craniosacral.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q277. Which response would you NOT expect during sympathetic activation?
  1. Airway bronchodilation
  2. Increased peristalsis
  3. Increased sweating
  4. Glycogen breakdown
  5. Increased alertness
Answer: B. Digestion is suppressed, not enhanced, during fight-or-flight.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q278. The occipital lobe is primarily responsible for:
  1. Hearing
  2. Voluntary movement
  3. Vision
  4. Balance
  5. Language production
Answer: C. The primary visual cortex occupies the occipital lobe.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 8
Q279. Decision-making, planning and personality are functions of the:
  1. Occipital lobe
  2. Parietal lobe
  3. Temporal lobe
  4. Frontal lobe
  5. Cerebellum
Answer: D. The prefrontal region of the frontal lobe governs executive function.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q280. Processing of touch, pressure, pain and temperature occurs in the:
  1. Frontal lobe
  2. Occipital lobe
  3. Brainstem
  4. Cerebellum
  5. Parietal lobe
Answer: E. The primary somatosensory cortex lies in the parietal lobe.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q281. Hearing and language comprehension are associated with the:
  1. Temporal lobe
  2. Occipital lobe
  3. Parietal lobe
  4. Cerebellum
  5. Medulla
Answer: A. The auditory cortex and Wernicke's area are temporal.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 8
Q282. The cerebellum's principal role is to:
  1. Regulate the rate and depth of breathing
  2. Coordinate movement, balance and posture
  3. Process visual input from the retinas
  4. Secrete hormones into the bloodstream
  5. Control blood pressure and vessel diameter
Answer: B. It fine-tunes motor output; damage causes ataxia and incoordination.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 8
Q283. Which structure controls vital autonomic functions such as breathing and heart rate?
  1. Cerebrum
  2. Cerebellum
  3. Brainstem
  4. Thalamus
  5. Corpus callosum
Answer: C. The medulla within the brainstem houses the cardiovascular and respiratory centers — damage here is rapidly fatal.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 9
Q284. The thalamus is best described as the:
  1. Master endocrine gland controlling the other glands
  2. Motor coordination center for balance and posture
  3. Site of cerebrospinal fluid production and secretion
  4. Sensory relay station routing input to the cortex
  5. Emotional memory center storing fear and reward
Answer: D. Nearly all sensory pathways synapse in the thalamus before reaching the cortex.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 9
Q285. The hypothalamus regulates all of the following EXCEPT:
  1. Hunger and satiety signals
  2. Thirst and water balance
  3. Body temperature set point
  4. Endocrine output via the pituitary
  5. Fine motor coordination
Answer: E. Fine motor coordination is cerebellar. The hypothalamus is the homeostatic control center.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q286. A patient with a lesion causing loss of balance and clumsy, uncoordinated movement most likely has damage to the:
  1. Cerebellum
  2. Frontal lobe
  3. Occipital lobe
  4. Temporal lobe
  5. Thalamus
Answer: A. Ataxia is the classic sign of cerebellar dysfunction.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 8
Q287. Which sensory pathway is unique in that it bypasses the thalamus?
  1. Color vision
  2. Olfaction
  3. Hearing
  4. Gustation
  5. Fine touch
Answer: B. Olfactory signals travel from the olfactory bulb directly to the cortex and limbic system — which is why smell so strongly evokes memory and emotion.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 9
Q288. Odorant molecules must first ____ before they can be detected.
  1. Be broken down into single atoms by enzymes in the mucus
  2. Be converted into light energy by receptor pigments
  3. Dissolve in the mucus of the olfactory epithelium
  4. Bind to hemoglobin and travel in the bloodstream to the bulb
  5. Cross the blood-brain barrier into the cerebral circulation
Answer: C. Receptors detect dissolved chemicals, so the odorant must go into solution first.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 12
Q289. Which neuron type serves olfactory transduction?
  1. Multipolar motor
  2. Unipolar interneuron
  3. Pyramidal
  4. Bipolar
  5. Purkinje
Answer: D. Olfactory receptor neurons are bipolar, sending one process to the epithelium and one to the bulb.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 13
Q290. The five recognized primary tastes are:
  1. Sweet, sour, salty, bitter, spicy
  2. Sweet, sour, salty, umami, metallic
  3. Sweet, salty, bitter, spicy, fatty
  4. Sour, salty, bitter, cold, umami
  5. Sweet, sour, salty, bitter, umami
Answer: E. Umami is the savoury/glutamate taste. Spicy is a pain and temperature sensation, not a taste.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 15
Q291. Why is 'spicy' NOT classified as a taste?
  1. Capsaicin activates pain and heat receptors rather than gustatory receptors
  2. It is detected only in the throat and not on the surface of the tongue
  3. Capsaicin is neutralized by saliva long before it reaches receptors
  4. It is detected by olfactory receptors in the nasal epithelium instead
  5. It is too weak a stimulus to depolarize any taste receptor cell
Answer: A. Capsaicin stimulates TRPV1 nociceptors — the brain interprets it as burning, not flavor.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 14
Q292. Gustatory information reaches the cortex after relaying through the:
  1. Olfactory bulb
  2. Thalamus
  3. Cerebellum
  4. Pineal gland
  5. Hypothalamus only
Answer: B. Unlike smell, taste follows the standard route through the thalamus.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 9
Q293. A patient with a severe head cold reports that food is tasteless. The best explanation is:
  1. The taste buds have been destroyed by the viral infection
  2. The thalamus is inflamed and can no longer relay taste signals
  3. Loss of olfaction removes most of the perceived flavor
  4. Saliva production has stopped so tastants cannot dissolve
  5. The gustatory nerves have been severed by the congestion
Answer: C. Most of what we call flavor is retronasal smell, so nasal congestion blunts it dramatically.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q294. The blood-brain barrier is formed chiefly by:
  1. Microglia acting as the resident immune cells of the brain
  2. Oligodendrocytes wrapping myelin around central nervous system axons
  3. Ependymal cells lining the ventricles and secreting spinal fluid
  4. Tight junctions of capillary endothelium supported by astrocytes
  5. The three meningeal layers wrapping the outside of the brain
Answer: D. Tight junctions restrict passage; astrocyte end-feet help regulate it.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q295. Cerebrospinal fluid is produced by the:
  1. Arachnoid villi
  2. The pia mater
  3. Corpus callosum
  4. Pituitary gland
  5. Choroid plexus
Answer: E. Ependymal cells of the choroid plexus secrete CSF into the ventricles.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 5
Q296. The corpus callosum functions to:
  1. Connect the two cerebral hemispheres
  2. Produce cerebrospinal fluid for the ventricles
  3. Control the rhythm and depth of breathing
  4. Relay sensory input on its way to the cortex
  5. Regulate hormone release from the pituitary
Answer: A. It is the largest white-matter commissure linking left and right.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q297. Broca's area is associated with:
  1. Language comprehension
  2. Speech production
  3. Visual processing
  4. Balance and posture
  5. Sound perception
Answer: B. Damage causes expressive aphasia: understanding is intact but speech is labored.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 8
Q298. The medulla oblongata regulates:
  1. Voluntary movement of the skeletal muscles
  2. Vision and visual interpretation
  3. Heart rate, breathing and blood pressure
  4. Hormone release from the anterior pituitary

Answer: C. It houses the cardiovascular and respiratory control centers.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 14
Q299. Which structure links the nervous and endocrine systems?
  1. The thalamus
  2. Cerebellum
  3. Pons and midbrain
  4. Hypothalamus
  5. Medulla oblongata
Answer: D. The hypothalamus controls the pituitary and thus much of the endocrine system.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 1
Q300. The limbic system is most associated with:
  1. Visual interpretation
  2. Balance and posture
  3. Breathing and heart rate
  4. Spinal reflex arcs
  5. Emotion and memory
Answer: E. The amygdala and hippocampus mediate emotion and memory formation.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 13
Q301. Absolute refractory period exists to ensure:
  1. One-way propagation and a maximum firing rate
  2. Faster conduction velocity along the axon
  3. Stronger signals with greater amplitude
  4. Neurotransmitter release at every synapse
  5. Myelin formation around the growing axon
Answer: A. Inactivated sodium channels prevent the impulse from traveling backward.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 3
Q302. During repolarization the membrane potential moves:
  1. From -70 toward +30 mV
  2. From +30 back toward -70 mV
  3. From -55 to -70 mV only
  4. It does not change at all
  5. From -90 up to +30 mV
Answer: B. Potassium efflux restores negativity after the sodium spike.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 3
Q303. Sympathetic effects on the bladder and gut are to:
  1. Stimulate emptying of the bladder and bowel
  2. Increase peristalsis and glandular secretion
  3. Inhibit motility and contract sphincters
  4. Relax the internal sphincters to allow emptying
  5. Increase salivation and gastric acid output
Answer: C. Fight-or-flight defers elimination and digestion.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q304. Which neurotransmitter dominates at parasympathetic target organs?
  1. Norepinephrine
  2. Dopamine
  3. Serotonin
  4. Acetylcholine
  5. Histamine
Answer: D. Parasympathetic postganglionic fibers are cholinergic.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q305. The basal ganglia are chiefly involved in:
  1. Hearing and the localization of sound
  2. Processing visual input from the retina
  3. Digestion and absorption of nutrients
  4. Regulating body temperature and shivering
  5. Initiating and regulating voluntary movement
Answer: E. Dysfunction produces movement disorders such as Parkinson disease.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 10
Q306. Taste receptor cells are found within:
  1. Taste buds
  2. Papillae only
  3. The olfactory bulb
  4. The thalamus
  5. The tongue muscle
Answer: A. Taste buds sit within papillae and house the gustatory receptor cells.Module 6 brain & ANS deck, slide 14
Q307. Which cranial nerve carries most taste sensation from the anterior tongue?
  1. CN V (trigeminal)
  2. CN VII (facial)
  3. CN IX (glossopharyngeal)
  4. CN X (vagus)
  5. CN XII (hypoglossal)
Answer: B. The facial nerve serves the anterior two-thirds of the tongue.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q308. Olfactory adaptation explains why:
  1. Smells intensify the longer they are present
  2. Taste sensation grows stronger over the meal
  3. You stop noticing a persistent odor
  4. Vision blurs when an odor is strong
  5. Hearing sharpens when the nose is blocked
Answer: C. Receptors desensitize rapidly to a constant stimulus.Lecture Outline, Module 6 — p.60
Q309. When focusing on a nearby object, the lens:
  1. Flattens as the ciliary muscle relaxes
  2. Moves forward within the vitreous chamber
  3. Becomes opaque and scatters incoming light
  4. Becomes rounder and more refractive
  5. Stops changing shape and stays fully flat
Answer: D. Near vision requires more refraction, so the ciliary muscle contracts and the lens rounds up — accommodation.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q310. When viewing a distant object, the lens:
  1. Rounds up
  2. Thickens
  3. Detaches
  4. Darkens
  5. Flattens
Answer: E. Distant light rays are nearly parallel and need less bending, so the lens flattens.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q311. Rods differ from cones in that rods:
  1. Function in dim light and detect black and white
  2. Detect color and fine detail at high light levels
  3. Are concentrated at the fovea centralis for acuity
  4. Require bright daylight to respond at all
  5. Are absent from the peripheral retina
Answer: A. Rods are highly light-sensitive but give no color and poor acuity.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 6
Q312. Cones are concentrated at the ____ and are responsible for ____.
  1. peripheral retina; dim-light night vision
  2. fovea centralis; color and fine detail
  3. optic disc; peripheral motion detection
  4. lens; accommodation and near focusing
  5. sclera; structural protection of the globe
Answer: B. The fovea is packed with cones, which is why we move our eyes to place images there.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q313. Opsin functions in vision by:
  1. Bending incoming light rays through the cornea and lens onto the retinal surface
  2. Producing aqueous humor within the ciliary epithelium
  3. Changing shape when struck by light, triggering a signaling cascade
  4. Draining spent tears through the nasolacrimal duct into the nose
  5. Pumping fluid out of the corneal stroma to keep it transparent
Answer: C. Photoisomerization of retinal alters opsin, initiating the transduction cascade.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 7
Q314. Trace the correct retinal signal pathway:
  1. Ganglion → bipolar → photoreceptor → optic nerve → visual cortex
  2. Bipolar → photoreceptor → ganglion → optic chiasm → optic nerve
  3. Amacrine → horizontal → bipolar → ganglion → optic nerve
  4. Photoreceptor → bipolar → ganglion → optic nerve
  5. Optic nerve → ganglion → bipolar → rod cell
Answer: D. Light is transduced by rods and cones, relayed by bipolar cells, and carried out by ganglion cell axons.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 5
Q315. The axons of which cells form the optic nerve?
  1. Rod photoreceptors
  2. Cone photoreceptors
  3. Bipolar cells
  4. Amacrine cells
  5. Ganglion cells
Answer: E. Ganglion cell axons converge at the optic disc and exit as the optic nerve.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 5
Q316. Aqueous humor differs from vitreous humor in that aqueous humor is:
  1. Continuously produced and drained, maintaining intraocular pressure
  2. Renewed only once every few years by slow diffusion
  3. Located behind the lens, filling the large posterior cavity of the eyeball
  4. Responsible for focusing light rays sharply onto the retina
  5. Secreted by the pigmented epithelium that lines the retina
Answer: A. Blocked drainage of aqueous humor raises intraocular pressure — the mechanism of glaucoma.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 4
Q317. Vitreous humor is best described as:
  1. A watery fluid that circulates in the space in front of the lens
  2. A permanent gel that maintains the eye's shape
  3. A thin tear film spread across the front of the cornea
  4. A clear layer covering the front surface of the lens
  5. A photoreceptor layer lining the back wall of the eye
Answer: B. It is formed before birth and never replaced.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 4
Q318. Tears are produced by the ____ and drain via the ____.

  2. conjunctiva; eustachian tube
  3. lacrimal glands; nasolacrimal duct
  4. retina; optic nerve and optic chiasm
  5. corneal epithelium; the pupil
Answer: C. Drainage into the nasal cavity is why crying produces a runny nose.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 3
Q319. Tears contribute to eye health by:
  1. Forming the clear vitreous gel that fills the posterior cavity behind the lens
  2. Producing the melanin pigment that darkens the choroid and shades the eye's interior
  3. Regulating pupil diameter by contracting the iris to control how much light enters the eye
  4. Lubricating, washing away debris and providing lysozyme for antibacterial defense
  5. Generating the action potentials that travel along the optic nerve to the brain
Answer: D. Lysozyme in tears digests bacterial cell walls.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 3
Q320. A visual acuity of 20/200 means the patient:
  1. Has sharper than average vision at every distance
  2. Can see at 200 feet what a normal eye must be at 20 feet to see
  3. Has perfect uncorrected distance vision in both eyes
  4. Has no light perception at all in the affected eye
  5. Must be at 20 feet to see what a normal eye sees at 200 feet
Answer: E. The larger the second number, the worse the acuity. 20/200 is the threshold for legal blindness in many jurisdictions.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 5
Q321. Which acuity indicates BETTER than average vision?
  1. 20/15
  2. 20/20
  3. 20/30
  4. 20/40
  5. 20/200
Answer: A. 20/15 means you see at 20 feet what a normal eye must be at 15 feet to see.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q322. The structure that regulates how much light enters the eye is the:
  1. Ciliary body
  2. Iris/pupil
  3. Lens capsule
  4. Fovea centralis
  5. Vitreous humor
Answer: B. The iris muscles adjust pupil diameter in response to light and autonomic tone.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 9
Q323. Order the pathway of sound: (1) ossicles (2) tympanic membrane (3) pinna (4) cochlea
  1. 1,2,3,4
  2. 2,3,4,1
  3. 3,2,1,4
  4. 3,1,2,4
  5. 4,3,2,1
Answer: C. The pinna collects, the eardrum vibrates, the ossicles amplify, and the cochlea transduces.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 13
Q324. The tympanic membrane functions to:
  1. Secrete the waxy cerumen that coats and protects the ear canal lining
  2. Transduce sound energy into nerve impulses that reach the brain
  3. Equalize middle ear pressure with the outside air
  4. Convert air pressure waves into mechanical vibration
  5. Detect head rotation in each of the three separate planes
Answer: D. It is the interface between airborne sound and the mechanical ossicular chain.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 10
Q325. The three ossicles function primarily to:
  1. Produce the endolymph that fills the cochlear duct
  2. Detect the pull of gravity on the head when it is tilted to one side
  3. Drain accumulated fluid from the middle ear cavity
  4. Generate nerve impulses along the auditory nerve
  5. Amplify vibrations from the eardrum to the oval window
Answer: E. Malleus, incus and stapes lever the signal up to overcome the impedance of cochlear fluid.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 13
Q326. The cochlea's essential role is to:
  1. Convert fluid vibration into electrical nerve signals via hair cells
  2. Amplify sound waves mechanically as they cross the air-filled middle ear cavity
  3. Equalize pressure between the middle ear cavity and the back of the throat
  4. Collect sound waves and funnel them down the external ear canal
  5. Maintain balance by sensing head tilt and rotation of the body
Answer: A. This is where mechanical energy becomes neural signal.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 14
Q327. The eustachian tube functions to:
  1. Drain tears from the eye into the nasal cavity
  2. Equalize pressure between the middle ear and the atmosphere
  3. Amplify sound waves mechanically on their way through to the cochlea
  4. Produce the cerumen that protects the outer ear canal
  5. Detect rotation of the head in any plane of movement
Answer: B. It opens on swallowing or yawning — why ears pop during altitude change.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 10
Q328. Which cranial nerve carries both hearing and equilibrium information?
  1. CN V (trigeminal nerve)
  2. CN VII (facial nerve)
  3. CN VIII (vestibulocochlear)
  4. CN IX (glossopharyngeal nerve)
  5. CN X (vagus nerve)
Answer: C. CN VIII has a cochlear branch for hearing and a vestibular branch for balance.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 5
Q329. Damage to the ossicles would produce which type of hearing loss?
  1. Sensorineural
  2. Central
  3. Mixed cortical
  4. Conductive
  5. No hearing loss
Answer: D. Conductive loss arises from failure to mechanically transmit sound to the cochlea.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q330. Otoliths in the utricle and saccule detect:
  1. Rotational acceleration of the head
  2. Sound frequency and loudness
  3. Light intensity and color
  4. Temperature of the endolymph
  5. Gravity and linear acceleration
Answer: E. Calcium carbonate crystals lag behind during linear movement, bending the hair cells beneath.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 12
Q331. The semicircular canals detect:
  1. Rotational (angular) acceleration of the head
  2. Linear acceleration and head tilt from gravity
  3. The static pull of gravity on the otoliths
  4. Sound pitch and loudness of incoming waves
  5. Pressure changes in the middle ear cavity
Answer: A. Three canals in three planes detect rotation in any direction.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 11
Q332. Hair cells in the semicircular canals are stimulated when:
  1. Otolith crystals shift across the gelatinous membrane and press on the hair bundles
  2. Endolymph lags behind head rotation and bends the cupula in the ampulla
  3. Sound waves traveling through the fluid of the cochlear duct strike them directly
  4. Light energy strikes the visual pigment molecules stored inside them
  5. Pressure equalizes across the eardrum during swallowing or yawning
Answer: B. Fluid inertia is the key — the endolymph lags, deflecting the cupula.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 11
Q333. The structure within the semicircular canal that houses the sensory hair cells is the:
  1. Cochlea
  2. Utricle
  3. Ampulla
  4. Oval window
  5. Tympanic cavity
Answer: C. The ampulla is the dilated base of each canal containing the crista and cupula.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q334. Which brain region integrates vestibular input with vision and proprioception to maintain balance?
  1. Frontal lobe
  2. Occipital lobe
  3. Medulla only
  4. Cerebellum
  5. Thalamus only
Answer: D. The cerebellum combines these three streams to produce coordinated postural control.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 12
Q335. Vertigo following an inner ear infection most likely results from disruption of the:
  1. Organ of Corti in the cochlea
  2. Optic nerve fibers in the retina
  3. Tympanic membrane
  4. Eustachian tube
  5. Vestibular apparatus
Answer: E. Damage to the vestibular system produces a false sense of motion.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q336. Spinning rapidly then stopping produces a lingering sense of rotation because:
  1. Endolymph continues to move briefly after the head stops
  2. Otolith crystals are dislodged from the gelatinous surface of the macula
  3. The cochlear hair cells are overstimulated by the spinning motion
  4. The optic nerve keeps firing after the eyes stop
  5. Middle ear pressure fails to equalize after the spinning stops
Answer: A. Fluid momentum keeps deflecting the cupula for several seconds after motion ceases.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q337. The cornea contributes to vision mainly by:
  1. Detecting light and starting transduction
  2. Providing most of the eye's refraction
  3. Producing the watery layer of tears
  4. Controlling pupil size in bright light
  5. Draining aqueous humor from the eye
Answer: B. The cornea performs the bulk of light bending; the lens fine-tunes focus.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q338. Accommodation is achieved by:
  1. Moving the retina closer to the lens
  2. Dilating the pupil with the iris muscles
  3. Changing lens shape via the ciliary muscle
  4. Rotating the eye with the extraocular muscles
  5. Thickening the cornea to bend light further
Answer: C. Ciliary contraction rounds the lens for near vision.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 9
Q339. Myopia (nearsightedness) occurs when images focus:
  1. Behind the surface of the retina
  2. Exactly on the fovea centralis
  3. On the optic disc
  4. In front of the retina
  5. On the outer surface of the cornea
Answer: D. An overly long eye or overly strong refraction focuses light too early.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q340. The blind spot corresponds to the:
  1. Fovea centralis, the tightly cone-packed pit that provides the sharpest vision
  2. Macula lutea, the yellowish region of retina that surrounds the central fovea
  3. Ciliary body, the ring of muscle that changes the shape of the lens
  4. Iris, the pigmented muscular ring that sets the size of the pupil
  5. Optic disc, where the optic nerve exits and there are no photoreceptors
Answer: E. No rods or cones are present where axons leave the eye.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 5
Q341. Night vision depends primarily on:
  1. Rods
  2. Cones
  3. The lens
  4. The cornea
  5. Aqueous humor
Answer: A. Rods are far more light-sensitive but give no color.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 6
Q342. Which vitamin deficiency most directly impairs night vision?
  1. Vitamin C
  2. Vitamin A
  3. Vitamin D
  4. Vitamin K
  5. Vitamin B12
Answer: B. Retinal, derived from vitamin A, is essential to the visual pigment.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q343. Glaucoma results from:
  1. Clouding of the lens that scatters and blocks incoming light
  2. Detachment of the retina from the choroid
  3. Raised intraocular pressure damaging the optic nerve
  4. Scarring of the cornea after repeated injury
  5. Gradual loss of the vitreous gel from the eyeball
Answer: C. Impaired aqueous drainage raises pressure and damages nerve fibers.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 4
Q344. A cataract is best described as:
  1. Retinal tear
  2. Optic nerve inflammation
  3. Corneal ulcer
  4. Clouding of the lens
  5. Excess tears
Answer: D. Lens opacity scatters light and blurs vision.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q345. Sound is converted to a neural signal by:
  1. The tympanic membrane at the end of the ear canal
  2. The three ossicles of the middle ear cavity
  3. The pinna and the external ear canal
  4. The eustachian tube draining to the throat
  5. Hair cells of the organ of Corti in the cochlea
Answer: E. Bending of stereocilia opens ion channels and generates receptor potentials.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 14
Q346. High-frequency sound is detected at the:
  1. Base of the cochlea near the oval window
  2. Apex of the cochlea, farthest from the oval window
  3. Semicircular canals of the vestibular system
  4. Utricle and saccule of the vestibule
  5. Eardrum at the end of the ear canal
Answer: A. The stiff basal membrane responds to high frequencies; the apex to low.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 10
Q347. Conductive hearing loss would result from:
  1. Damage to the hair cells of the cochlea
  2. Middle ear or ossicular dysfunction
  3. Damage to the auditory branch of CN VIII
  4. A stroke in the auditory cortex
  5. Disease of the vestibular apparatus
Answer: B. Anything blocking mechanical transmission produces conductive loss.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 10
Q348. The round window functions to:
  1. Collect sound waves from the outside air and funnel them inward
  2. Detect rotation of the head as it turns from side to side
  3. Relieve pressure as fluid moves within the cochlea
  4. Equalize middle ear pressure with the air in the throat
  5. Produce the endolymph inside the cochlear duct
Answer: C. It bulges outward, allowing the incompressible cochlear fluid to move.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 10
Q349. Motion sickness arises largely from:
  1. Excess cerumen blocking the ear canal
  2. Loss of hair cells from the spiral organ of the cochlear duct
  3. Blockage of the eustachian tube on one side
  4. Conflict between vestibular and visual input
  5. Cataract clouding of the lens in one eye
Answer: D. Mismatched sensory signals produce nausea and disorientation.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q350. Which structure detects head tilt relative to gravity?
  1. The three semicircular canals and their cupulae
  2. The cochlea and its organ of Corti
  3. The tympanic membrane and the ossicles
  4. The eustachian tube and its lining
  5. Utricle and saccule with their otoliths
Answer: E. Otolith organs respond to gravity and linear acceleration.Module 7 eye & ear deck, slide 12
Q351. Presbyopia results from:
  1. Loss of lens elasticity with age, impairing near focus
  2. Flattening of the corneal curvature, reducing its refractive power
  3. Degeneration of the retina at the central macula
  4. Increased production of aqueous humor by the ciliary body epithelium
  5. Damage to optic nerve fibers at the optic disc
Answer: A. The ageing lens stiffens and cannot round up for near vision.Lecture Outline, Module 7 — p.73
Q352. A lipid-soluble steroid hormone most likely binds which receptor type?
  1. Ion channel-linked
  2. Intracellular
  3. G-protein coupled
  4. Enzyme-linked
  5. Membrane antigen
Answer: B. Lipid-soluble hormones cross the membrane freely and bind receptors inside the cell.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 4
Q353. Receptors that alter gene transcription and produce slow but long-lasting effects are:
  1. Ion channel-linked
  2. G-protein coupled
  3. Intracellular
  4. Enzyme-linked
  5. Mechanoreceptors
Answer: C. The hormone-receptor complex acts as a transcription factor, so the effect takes hours but persists.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 5
Q354. Which receptor type produces the FASTEST response?
  1. Intracellular (nuclear)
  2. G-protein coupled
  3. Enzyme-linked (kinase)
  4. Ion channel-linked
  5. Membrane antigen receptor
Answer: D. The receptor IS the channel — ions flow the instant the ligand binds, as at the neuromuscular junction.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q355. The acetylcholine receptor at the neuromuscular junction is an example of a:
  1. G-protein coupled receptor
  2. Intracellular steroid receptor
  3. Enzyme-linked kinase receptor
  4. Pressure-sensing baroreceptor
  5. Ion channel-linked receptor
Answer: E. ACh binding opens the channel directly, allowing sodium influx and immediate depolarization.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q356. G-protein coupled receptors amplify signals by:
  1. Activating G-proteins that generate second messengers inside the cell
  2. Opening a membrane ion channel directly so that ions flow the instant the ligand binds
  3. Binding directly to hormone response elements in the DNA and switching on gene transcription
  4. Carrying the hormone across the membrane into the nucleus
  5. Degrading the ligand enzymatically to end the signal
Answer: A. One receptor can activate many G-proteins, each generating many second messengers — enormous amplification.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 4
Q357. A water-soluble hormone cannot cross the plasma membrane, so it must:
  1. Bind an intracellular receptor and alter gene transcription
  2. Bind a surface receptor and act through second messengers
  3. Be converted into a lipid-soluble steroid before it acts
  4. Enter the cell through a nuclear pore to reach its receptor
  5. Be taken into the cell by phagocytosis before binding
Answer: B. This is the fundamental division: lipid-soluble acts inside, water-soluble acts at the surface.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 4
Q358. Baroreceptors detect:
  1. Chemical concentration of oxygen and pH
  2. Tissue damage and noxious chemicals
  3. Pressure and stretch in vessel walls
  4. Light striking photoreceptor pigments
  5. Temperature change at the skin surface
Answer: C. They are central to moment-to-moment blood pressure regulation.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 3
Q359. Chemoreceptors detect changes in:
  1. Pressure and stretch in vessel walls
  2. Physical deformation of the membrane
  3. Light intensity striking the retina
  4. Oxygen, carbon dioxide and pH
  5. Sound vibration in the cochlea
Answer: D. Peripheral and central chemoreceptors drive respiratory adjustment.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q360. Nociceptors are activated by:
  1. Light touch and vibration on the skin
  2. Sound waves bending cochlear hair cells
  3. Blood pressure changes in the aorta
  4. Rising osmolarity of the blood plasma
  5. Tissue damage and noxious stimuli
Answer: E. Nociceptors are the pain receptors; their activation warns of actual or impending injury.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q361. Mechanoreceptors are activated when:
  1. The receptor membrane is physically deformed
  2. The oxygen concentration in the surrounding blood plasma falls
  3. The pH of the surrounding fluid falls
  4. The temperature of the surrounding tissue rises above its set point
  5. The retinal pigment absorbs light
Answer: A. Physical distortion opens stretch-sensitive channels — the basis of touch, hearing and proprioception.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 3
Q362. A patient with damaged carotid baroreceptors would have most difficulty:
  1. Detecting pain from injured tissue
  2. Regulating blood pressure on standing
  3. Sensing temperature change at the skin
  4. Hearing high-frequency tones clearly
  5. Focusing the lens for near vision
Answer: B. Loss of baroreflex causes orthostatic hypotension because pressure changes go undetected.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 3
Q363. Aldosterone is released in response to ____ and causes ____.
  1. high blood pressure or low potassium; loss of sodium and water in the urine with retention of potassium
  2. high blood glucose; movement of glucose into muscle cells
  3. low blood pressure or high potassium; sodium and water reabsorption with potassium excretion
  4. darkness at night; drowsiness and sleep
  5. low blood calcium; release of calcium from bone with increased absorption from the small intestine
Answer: C. Aldosterone is the salt-retaining hormone, raising blood volume and pressure.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 11
Q364. Antidiuretic hormone (ADH) causes the kidney to:
  1. Excrete more water into the filtrate, producing a large volume of dilute urine
  2. Excrete more sodium into the urine while holding on to potassium ions
  3. Release renin from the juxtaglomerular cells
  4. Reabsorb more water, producing concentrated urine
  5. Reabsorb potassium in exchange for sodium
Answer: D. ADH inserts aquaporins in the collecting duct. Anti-diuretic literally means against urine production.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 11
Q365. A surge of which hormone causes the mature ovarian follicle to rupture?
  1. FSH
  2. Estrogen
  3. Progesterone
  4. Prolactin
  5. LH
Answer: E. The LH surge is the trigger for ovulation. In males the same hormone drives Leydig cells to make testosterone.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q366. Which hormone stimulates follicle growth in females and spermatogenesis in males?
  1. FSH
  2. LH
  3. Oxytocin
  4. ADH
  5. Prolactin
Answer: A. FSH — Follicle Stimulating Hormone — grows the follicle and supports sperm production.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 12
Q367. Glucagon is released when blood glucose is ____ and acts by ____.
  1. high; moving glucose out of the blood into muscle cells
  2. low; stimulating the liver to break down glycogen into glucose
  3. high; storing surplus glucose as glycogen in the liver while suppressing fat breakdown
  4. low; moving glucose from the blood into adipose tissue for storage as fat
  5. high; increasing storage of fat in adipose tissue
Answer: B. Glucagon comes from pancreatic alpha cells and raises blood glucose between meals.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 14
Q368. Insulin is released when blood glucose is ____ and acts by ____.
  1. low; releasing stored glucose from the liver into blood
  2. low; breaking down liver glycogen to raise blood glucose
  3. high; promoting glucose uptake into cells and storage as glycogen
  4. high; blocking glucose uptake by skeletal muscle and fat cells so glucose stays in the blood
  5. low; stimulating gluconeogenesis in the liver from amino acids and lactate
Answer: C. Insulin comes from pancreatic beta cells and is the only hormone that lowers blood glucose.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 14
Q369. Oxytocin during childbirth acts to:
  1. Relax the uterine smooth muscle and delay labor
  2. Thicken the endometrium to prepare for implantation
  3. Promote sleep and reset the circadian rhythm
  4. Stimulate uterine contractions and milk ejection
  5. Increase blood glucose by breaking down glycogen
Answer: D. Oxytocin drives a positive feedback loop: contractions release more oxytocin, intensifying contractions.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q370. Growth hormone promotes:
  1. Storage of fat in adipose tissue with reduced synthesis of new protein
  2. Sodium retention with potassium excretion
  3. Excretion of calcium in the urine with reduced breakdown of bone matrix
  4. Sleep onset and control of the circadian rhythm
  5. Tissue growth, protein synthesis and fat breakdown
Answer: E. GH builds tissue while mobilizing fat as an alternative fuel.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 12
Q371. Melatonin secretion is stimulated by ____ and functions to ____.
  1. darkness; promote sleep and regulate circadian rhythm
  2. bright morning light; increase daytime alertness and sharpen concentration
  3. prolonged stress; raise blood pressure and glucose
  4. eating a large meal; stimulate digestive secretion and speed nutrient absorption
  5. exercise; build muscle and mobilize stored fat
Answer: A. The pineal gland releases melatonin in darkness, which is why screen light at night disrupts sleep.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 7
Q372. Thyroxine (T4) primarily regulates:
  1. Blood calcium and bone deposition
  2. Metabolic rate and energy consumption
  3. Blood clotting and platelet activity
  4. Urine concentration and water reabsorption
  5. Immune memory and lymphocyte activation
Answer: B. T4 sets basal metabolic rate, influencing heat production, heart rate and growth.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 8
Q373. A patient with untreated hypothyroidism would most likely present with:
  1. Weight loss, heat intolerance and tachycardia
  2. Increased appetite with weight loss
  3. Weight gain, cold intolerance and fatigue
  4. Hypertension and tremor
  5. Excessive sweating
Answer: C. Low thyroid hormone lowers metabolic rate, producing the classic sluggish picture.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 15
Q374. Which pairing of hormone and gland is INCORRECT?
  1. Insulin — pancreas
  2. Aldosterone — adrenal cortex
  3. Melatonin — pineal gland
  4. Oxytocin — adrenal medulla
  5. Thyroxine — thyroid gland
Answer: D. Oxytocin is made in the hypothalamus and released from the posterior pituitary, not the adrenal medulla.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 15
Q375. Cortisol, released during prolonged stress, acts to:
  1. Lower blood glucose and improve the sensitivity of skeletal muscle to insulin
  2. Promote sleep and reset the circadian rhythm at nightfall
  3. Increase calcium deposition in bone and lower serum calcium
  4. Stimulate ovulation by triggering rupture of the mature ovarian follicle at mid-cycle
  5. Raise blood glucose, suppress inflammation and support blood pressure
Answer: E. Cortisol mobilizes fuel and dampens immune activity — useful acutely, harmful chronically.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 11
Q376. Epinephrine from the adrenal medulla produces:
  1. Increased heart rate, bronchodilation and mobilization of stored energy
  2. Slowing of the heart rate, narrowing of the airways and stronger contractions of the gut
  3. Increased secretion of digestive enzymes with greater blood flow to the intestines
  4. Pupil constriction with increased watery salivary secretion
  5. Decreased blood glucose with increased storage of fat and glycogen
Answer: A. It reinforces and prolongs the sympathetic fight-or-flight response.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 10
Q377. Parathyroid hormone and calcitonin act on blood calcium as:
  1. Both raise blood calcium levels
  2. PTH raises, calcitonin lowers
  3. Both lower blood calcium levels
  4. PTH lowers, calcitonin raises
  5. Neither changes blood calcium levels
Answer: B. They form the opposing arms of calcium homeostasis.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q378. A patient produces too little ADH. The expected finding is:
  1. Highly concentrated urine and low volume
  2. Sodium retention with rising blood volume
  3. Large volumes of dilute urine and thirst
  4. Hypertension with an expanded blood volume
  5. Hyperglycemia with glucose in the urine
Answer: C. Without ADH the collecting duct cannot reabsorb water — the mechanism of diabetes insipidus.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 11
Q379. Which hormone would you expect to rise during a prolonged fast?
  1. Insulin
  2. Calcitonin
  3. Oxytocin
  4. Glucagon
  5. Prolactin
Answer: D. Falling blood glucose triggers glucagon to mobilize hepatic glycogen.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 17
Q380. The hypothalamus controls the anterior pituitary primarily by:
  1. Direct nerve fibers running down the stalk
  2. Ion channels opening in the gland cells
  3. Mechanical compression of the pituitary stalk
  4. Lymphatic drainage carrying signaling peptides
  5. Releasing hormones into a portal blood supply
Answer: E. The hypophyseal portal system carries releasing and inhibiting hormones the short distance to the anterior pituitary.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 15
Q381. The anterior pituitary is controlled by the hypothalamus via:
  1. A portal blood system carrying releasing hormones
  2. Direct nerve fibers descending from the hypothalamus
  3. The vagus nerve carrying parasympathetic signals
  4. Cerebrospinal fluid circulating through the ventricles
  5. Lymphatic drainage from the surrounding tissue
Answer: A. The hypophyseal portal system delivers releasing hormones over a short distance.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 15
Q382. Which hormones are stored and released by the POSTERIOR pituitary?

  2. ADH and oxytocin
  3. GH and prolactin
  4. FSH, LH and TSH
  5. T4 and calcitonin
Answer: B. They are made in the hypothalamus and stored in the posterior lobe.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 6
Q383. Tropic hormones are defined as hormones that:
  1. Act only on bone and cartilage tissue
  2. Are lipid-soluble and cross membranes
  3. Stimulate another endocrine gland
  4. Act within the cell that made them
  5. Regulate blood calcium concentration
Answer: C. TSH, ACTH, FSH and LH all act on other endocrine glands.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 6
Q384. In the HPA axis, cortisol exerts negative feedback on:
  1. The kidney and its juxtaglomerular cells
  2. The pancreatic islet beta cells
  3. The thyroid gland follicular cells
  4. The hypothalamus and anterior pituitary
  5. The ovaries and the testes only
Answer: D. Rising cortisol suppresses CRH and ACTH, closing the loop.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 15
Q385. A patient with high T3/T4 would be expected to have:
