  2. Normal TSH with a raised TRH level
  3. High TRH driving the pituitary harder
  4. No change in TSH or TRH secretion
  5. Low TSH due to negative feedback
Answer: E. Excess thyroid hormone suppresses pituitary TSH output.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 8
Q386. Calcitonin is produced by which cells?
  1. Parafollicular (C) cells of the thyroid

  4. Zona glomerulosa of the adrenal cortex

Answer: A. C cells secrete calcitonin, lowering blood calcium.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 8
Q387. Which adrenal region produces aldosterone?

Answer: B. The outermost glomerulosa makes mineralocorticoids.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q388. The adrenal medulla is functionally an extension of the:
  1. Parasympathetic nervous system
  2. Somatic nervous system
  3. Sympathetic nervous system
  4. Enteric nervous system
  5. Endocrine pancreatic islets
Answer: C. It is modified sympathetic tissue releasing epinephrine into blood.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 10
Q389. Type 1 diabetes results primarily from:
  1. Insulin resistance developing in skeletal muscle and adipose cells
  2. Excess glucagon from pancreatic alpha cells
  3. Adrenal cortex failure with low cortisol
  4. Autoimmune destruction of pancreatic beta cells
  5. Thyroid hormone deficiency that slows the whole-body metabolic rate
Answer: D. Loss of beta cells means little or no insulin is produced.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q390. Type 2 diabetes is characterized chiefly by:
  1. Absent insulin from autoimmune beta-cell destruction
  2. Excess calcitonin from the thyroid gland
  3. Low cortisol output from an adrenal cortex that gradually stops working
  4. High ADH levels causing water retention and a dilutional fall in serum sodium
  5. Target-cell insulin resistance with relative insulin deficiency
Answer: E. Cells respond poorly to insulin, so glucose remains elevated.Lecture Outline, Module 8 — p.88
Q391. Down-regulation of receptors occurs when:
  1. Persistently high hormone levels reduce receptor number
  2. Hormone levels fall for days and the cell inserts extra receptors into its membrane
  3. Circulating enzymes break the hormone down before it reaches the target cell
  4. Cellular ATP is depleted during prolonged exercise
  5. Body temperature rises above the normal set point
Answer: A. Cells reduce receptor density to blunt excessive stimulation.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 6
Q392. Which hormone raises blood calcium by acting on bone, gut and kidney?
  1. Thyroid calcitonin
  2. Parathyroid hormone
  3. Pancreatic insulin
  4. Antidiuretic hormone
  5. Adrenal aldosterone
Answer: B. PTH mobilizes bone calcium, boosts gut absorption and reduces renal loss.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 10
Q393. Prolactin's principal action is to:

Q394. Cushing syndrome reflects excess:
  1. Insulin
  2. Calcitonin

  5. Melatonin

Q395. Addison disease results from insufficient:
  1. Thyroid hormones such as thyroxine and triiodothyronine from the follicles

  3. Anterior pituitary hormones such as growth hormone and prolactin
  4. Posterior pituitary hormones such as oxytocin
  5. Adrenal cortical hormones such as cortisol and aldosterone
Answer: E. Loss of cortisol and aldosterone causes fatigue, hypotension and low sodium.Module 8 receptors & hormones deck, slide 10
Q396. The three broad functions of blood are:
  1. Transport, regulation and protection
  2. Digestion, absorption, excretion
  3. Contraction, conduction, secretion
  4. Filtration, reabsorption, secretion
  5. Support, movement and mineral storage
Answer: A. Blood transports gases and nutrients, regulates temperature and pH, and protects via immunity and clotting.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q397. Blood contributes to thermoregulation by:
  1. Generating heat directly by metabolizing glucose within the plasma itself
  2. Redistributing heat — dilating skin vessels to lose heat, constricting to conserve it
  3. Evaporating plasma water through the capillary wall to cool the blood down
  4. Storing excess heat inside the red cells until it is released in the lungs
  5. Cooling only the brain, leaving the trunk and limb temperature unchanged
Answer: B. Shifting blood toward or away from the skin surface changes heat loss.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q398. Which formed element transports oxygen?

Q404. Which cell specifically produces antibodies?

  4. Plasma cell (activated B lymphocyte)

Q405. The normal pH range of blood is critical because:
  1. Blood pH determines which A, B or Rh antigens are carried on the red cell surface
  2. Plasma pH sets the number of platelets and white cells the marrow releases
  3. Blood pH fixes the total volume of plasma that the circulation contains
  4. Blood pH determines the circulating red cell count and the hematocrit
  5. Enzymes and proteins denature outside it, and hemoglobin's oxygen binding is disrupted
Answer: E. Protein conformation is pH-dependent, so even small deviations impair many processes.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 2
Q406. A patient with a very low erythrocyte count would most likely experience:
  1. Fatigue and shortness of breath from reduced oxygen carriage
  2. Excessive clotting and deep vein thrombosis formation
  3. Frequent bacterial infections from loss of phagocytic defense
  4. Allergic reactions with itching, hives and wheezing
  5. Hypertension caused by increased blood viscosity
Answer: A. Fewer red cells means less hemoglobin and reduced oxygen delivery — anemia.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q407. A patient on chemotherapy develops a very low neutrophil count. The greatest risk is:
  1. Spontaneous bleeding and bruising
  2. Serious bacterial infection
  3. Excessive clotting in the veins
  4. Anemia from red cell destruction
  5. Severe allergic reactions
Answer: B. Neutropenia removes the first line of antibacterial defense.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q408. Which vessel layer contains smooth muscle and controls vasoconstriction and vasodilation?
  1. Tunica intima
  2. Tunica externa
  3. Tunica media
  4. Endothelium
  5. Basement membrane
Answer: C. The tunica media's smooth muscle sets vessel diameter and therefore resistance.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q409. The tunica intima consists of endothelium and functions to:
  1. Contract to narrow the vessel lumen
  2. Anchor the vessel to the surrounding connective tissue
  3. Store calcium for muscle contraction
  4. Provide a smooth, non-thrombogenic lining
  5. Produce red blood cells for circulation
Answer: D. A smooth intact endothelium prevents inappropriate platelet adhesion.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q410. Arteries differ from veins in that arteries:
  1. Carry blood toward the heart and rely on valves to prevent backflow
  2. Are a single endothelial cell thick to allow diffusion
  3. Always carry deoxygenated blood back to the right heart
  4. Have lower pressure and thinner, more distensible walls
  5. Carry blood away from the heart and have thicker muscular walls
Answer: E. Arteries handle high pulsatile pressure, hence thick walls; veins are low pressure and need valves.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 4
Q411. Veins contain valves in order to:
  1. Prevent backflow so blood returns against gravity
  2. Increase the pressure generated inside the vessel
  3. Filter clots and debris out of the bloodstream
  4. Regulate body temperature by changing vessel width
  5. Allow oxygen and carbon dioxide to be exchanged
Answer: A. Skeletal muscle pumping plus valves drives venous return from the limbs.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q412. Capillaries are ideally suited for exchange because they:
  1. Have thick walls of smooth muscle that resist a high pressure
  2. Are one endothelial cell thick with a very large total surface area
  3. Contain one-way valves that slow the blood flow for exchange
  4. Carry blood at the highest pressure found in any vessel type
  5. Carry only freshly oxygenated blood arriving from the lungs
Answer: B. Minimal diffusion distance and huge surface area maximize exchange.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 3
Q413. Which vessels are the primary regulators of peripheral resistance and blood pressure?
  1. Capillaries
  2. Venules
  3. Arterioles
  4. Large veins
  5. The aorta
Answer: C. Arterioles have proportionally the most smooth muscle relative to lumen size.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q414. Vasoconstriction affects blood pressure by:
  1. Lowering it by reducing total peripheral resistance
  2. Leaving mean arterial pressure completely unchanged
  3. Lowering cardiac output by slowing the heart rate
  4. Raising it through increased peripheral resistance
  5. Increasing vessel diameter and therefore flow
Answer: D. Narrowing the vessel increases resistance to flow, raising arterial pressure.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q415. Vasodilation of systemic arterioles will:
  1. Increase blood pressure
  2. Increase heart rate only
  3. Halt blood flow completely
  4. Increase vascular resistance
  5. Decrease blood pressure
Answer: E. Widening the vessels lowers resistance and therefore pressure.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q416. Autoregulation of blood flow refers to:
  1. Local adjustment of blood flow to match a tissue's own metabolic needs
  2. Central nervous system control of the diameter of every vessel
  3. Hormonal control of vessel width by circulating epinephrine alone
  4. The action of the one-way valves found inside the systemic veins
  5. Formation of a clot to seal a break in the wall of the vessel
Answer: A. Active tissue releases metabolites that cause local vasodilation, increasing its own supply.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q417. During exercise, blood flow to skeletal muscle increases largely because:
  1. The heart stops delivering blood to any of the other organs
  2. Local metabolites cause vasodilation in active muscle beds
  3. The venous valves close and hold blood in the muscle
  4. Capillary beds in the muscle close to raise pressure
  5. Blood viscosity falls sharply during heavy exertion
Answer: B. Local metabolic control redirects flow toward tissue that needs it.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q418. What is the FIRST step of hemostasis?
  1. Platelet plug formation
  2. Coagulation
  3. Vascular spasm
  4. Fibrinolysis
  5. Clot retraction
Answer: C. Immediate smooth muscle constriction narrows the vessel and limits blood loss.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 9
Q419. What is the SECOND step of hemostasis?
  1. Immediate vascular spasm
  2. The coagulation cascade
  3. Fibrinolysis by plasmin
  4. Platelet plug formation
  5. Activation of thrombin
Answer: D. Platelets adhere to exposed collagen and aggregate into a temporary plug.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 9
Q420. What is the THIRD step of hemostasis?
  1. Vascular spasm, narrowing the vessel
  2. Platelet plug formation at the injury
  3. Fibrinolysis, dissolving the clot
  4. Vasodilation, widening the vessel
  5. Coagulation, producing a fibrin mesh
Answer: E. The cascade converts fibrinogen to fibrin, stabilizing the platelet plug into a true clot.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 9
Q421. Von Willebrand factor is essential because it:
  1. Bridges platelets to exposed collagen, enabling adhesion
  2. Converts fibrinogen into the insoluble fibrin mesh
  3. Dissolves the fibrin clot once healing is complete
  4. Activates Factor X directly, with no other factors needed
  5. Constricts the injured vessel to slow blood loss
Answer: A. vWF deficiency impairs step two of hemostasis, producing a bleeding tendency.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 9
Q422. The intrinsic and extrinsic coagulation pathways converge at:
  1. Factor II
  2. Factor X
  3. Factor VII
  4. Fibrinogen
  5. Calcium
Answer: B. Factor X marks the start of the common pathway.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 10
Q423. Which enzyme converts fibrinogen into fibrin?
  1. Plasmin
  2. Factor VII
  3. Thrombin
  4. Renin
  5. Trypsin
Answer: C. Thrombin, produced from prothrombin, polymerizes fibrinogen into the fibrin mesh.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 11
Q424. Place in correct order: prothrombin → ? → fibrinogen → ?
  1. plasmin; clot lysis
  2. renin; angiotensin
  3. Factor VII; Factor X
  4. thrombin; fibrin
  5. collagen; platelet plug
Answer: D. Prothrombin becomes thrombin, which converts fibrinogen into fibrin.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 10
Q425. The extrinsic pathway is triggered by:
  1. Contact with collagen exposed inside the damaged vessel
  2. Aggregation of platelets into a temporary plug at the injury site
  3. A fall in the concentration of calcium in the plasma
  4. A sustained rise in systemic arterial blood pressure
  5. Tissue factor released from damaged tissue outside the vessel
Answer: E. External trauma exposes tissue factor, giving the extrinsic pathway its speed.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 10
Q426. Fibrinolysis refers to:
  1. Breakdown and removal of a clot once the vessel has healed
  2. Formation of a stable fibrin clot at the injury site
  3. Adhesion of platelets to exposed collagen fibers
  4. Constriction of the vessel immediately after injury
  5. Production of antibodies against foreign antigens on microbes
Answer: A. Plasmin digests fibrin so the clot does not persist and obstruct flow.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 5
Q427. A patient on warfarin has an elevated bleeding risk because the drug interferes with:
  1. Adhesion of platelets to the wall of the injured vessel
  2. Synthesis of vitamin K-dependent clotting factors
  3. Vascular spasm of the vessel smooth muscle layer
  4. Production of red cells in the bone marrow
  5. Fibrinolysis, the breakdown of fibrin by plasmin
Answer: B. Warfarin blocks vitamin K recycling, reducing functional clotting factors.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q428. Why does severe liver disease often cause a bleeding tendency?
  1. The liver stores the body's reserve of platelets
  2. The liver produces the circulating red blood cells
  3. Most clotting factors are synthesized in the liver
  4. The liver stores the vitamin C needed for clotting
  5. The liver constricts the vessels of the gut wall
Answer: C. Loss of hepatic synthetic function depletes the coagulation factors.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q429. Approximately what percentage of blood volume is plasma?
  1. 25%
  2. 45%
  3. 75%
  4. 55%
  5. 90%
Answer: D. Plasma is roughly 55%; formed elements about 45%.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q430. The most abundant plasma protein is:
  1. Fibrinogen
  2. Globulin
  3. Hemoglobin
  4. Thrombin
  5. Albumin
Answer: E. Albumin maintains colloid osmotic pressure and transports substances.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q431. Low plasma albumin would most likely cause:
  1. Edema from reduced colloid osmotic pressure
  2. Widespread clotting inside the small vessels
  3. Anemia with a low circulating hemoglobin
  4. Leukocytosis with a raised white cell count
  5. Polycythemia with a raised red cell count
Answer: A. Fluid leaves the capillaries when the osmotic pull of albumin falls.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 14
Q432. Erythropoietin is released in response to:
  1. A rise in the arterial oxygen content
  2. Tissue hypoxia detected by the kidney
  3. A rise in the plasma calcium level
  4. A fall in the blood glucose level
  5. Acute bacterial infection with fever
Answer: B. Low oxygen stimulates renal EPO release to boost red cell production.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q433. Mature erythrocytes lack a nucleus, which means they:
  1. Live indefinitely and never need replacing
  2. Produce antibodies directed against foreign antigens
  3. Cannot divide or repair, surviving about 120 days
  4. Can still divide by mitosis to replace losses
  5. Store calcium for release into the plasma
Answer: C. Losing the nucleus maximizes hemoglobin space but limits lifespan.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 3
Q434. Which blood type is the universal RED CELL donor?
  1. A positive
  2. B negative
  3. AB negative
  4. O negative
  5. AB positive
Answer: D. O negative lacks A, B and Rh antigens, so it provokes no reaction.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 6
Q435. Type AB individuals are universal recipients because they:
  1. Have both anti-A and anti-B antibodies
  2. Lack both the A and the B surface antigens
  3. Are Rh negative and lack the D antigen
  4. Produce no plasma antibodies of any kind
  5. Have neither anti-A nor anti-B antibodies
Answer: E. With no anti-A or anti-B antibodies, donor red cells are not attacked.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q436. Hemolytic disease of the newborn arises when:
  1. Mother is Rh negative and sensitized against an Rh positive fetus
  2. The mother is Rh positive and the fetus is Rh negative at birth
  3. Both the mother and the fetus are Rh negative at delivery
  4. Both the mother and the fetus have type O positive blood
  5. The mother is type AB and the fetus is type O negative
Answer: A. Maternal anti-D antibodies cross the placenta and attack fetal red cells.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q437. Blood pressure is highest in the:
  1. The systemic capillary beds
  2. Aorta and large arteries
  3. The large systemic veins
  4. The postcapillary venules
  5. The right atrium of the heart
Answer: B. Pressure falls progressively along the circuit, lowest on the venous side.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q438. Capillary exchange is driven outward by ____ and inward by ____.
  1. colloid osmotic pressure; hydrostatic pressure
  2. gravity; simple diffusion down a gradient
  3. hydrostatic pressure; colloid osmotic pressure
  4. active transport; osmosis across the wall
  5. lymphatic drainage; capillary filtration
Answer: C. Hydrostatic pressure pushes fluid out; albumin's osmotic pull draws it back.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 14
Q439. The skeletal muscle pump aids venous return by:
  1. Increasing the arterial pressure in the working limb
  2. Dilating the leg veins so they pool more blood in the limb
  3. Slowing the heart rate to allow a longer filling time
  4. Compressing veins so valves direct blood toward the heart
  5. Thickening the blood so that it flows more forcefully
Answer: D. Muscle contraction squeezes veins; valves prevent backflow.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 13
Q440. Which leukocyte count would you expect to rise in a parasitic infection?
  1. Neutrophils
  2. Basophils
  3. Monocytes
  4. Platelets
  5. Eosinophils
Answer: E. Eosinophilia is characteristic of parasitic disease and allergy.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q441. Thrombocytopenia most directly increases the risk of:
  1. Bleeding
  2. Clotting
  3. Infection
  4. Anemia
  5. Hypertension
Answer: A. Too few platelets impairs the platelet plug and prolongs bleeding.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q442. Aspirin reduces clot formation by:
  1. Blocking vitamin K recycling in the liver
  2. Inhibiting platelet aggregation
  3. Dissolving fibrin strands directly
  4. Increasing thrombin production
  5. Blocking Factor X activation
Answer: B. Aspirin irreversibly inhibits platelet cyclooxygenase.Module 9 blood & vessels deck, slide 11
Q443. A thrombus that breaks free and travels is called a(n):
  1. Infarct
  2. Hematoma
  3. Embolus
  4. Petechia
  5. Aneurysm
Answer: C. A mobile clot is an embolus and may lodge downstream.Lecture Outline, Module 9 — p.98
Q444. The atrioventricular valves are open during:
  1. Ventricular systole, while blood is ejected into the aorta
  2. Atrial systole only, closing before the ventricles fill
  3. The entire cardiac cycle, including both systole and diastole
  4. Ventricular diastole, allowing the ventricles to fill
  5. Isovolumetric contraction, as ventricular pressure rises
Answer: D. AV valves open when ventricular pressure is low, permitting filling; they snap shut as the ventricles contract.Module 10 heart deck, slide 13
Q445. The semilunar valves close at the start of:
  1. Ventricular systole
  2. Atrial systole
  3. Atrial diastole
  4. Isovolumetric contraction
  5. Ventricular diastole
Answer: E. As the ventricles relax and pressure falls below arterial pressure, backflow snaps the semilunar valves shut.Module 10 heart deck, slide 13
Q446. The first heart sound (S1, 'lub') is produced by:
  1. AV valves closing at the start of ventricular systole
  2. Semilunar valves closing at the end of ventricular ejection
  3. Atrial contraction pushing the last blood into the ventricles
  4. Blood entering the atria from the vena cavae and pulmonary veins
  5. Rapid ventricular filling early in diastole
Answer: A. S1 marks the beginning of ventricular contraction.Module 10 heart deck, slide 16
Q447. The second heart sound (S2, 'dub') is produced by:
  1. AV valves closing at the start of ventricular systole
  2. Semilunar valves closing at the start of ventricular diastole
  3. Atrial contraction topping off ventricular filling
  4. Coronary artery blood flow through the myocardium during systole
  5. Turbulent ventricular filling in early diastole
Answer: B. S2 marks the end of ejection and the start of relaxation.Module 10 heart deck, slide 4
Q448. Which valve prevents backflow into the LEFT atrium during ventricular contraction?
  1. Tricuspid valve
  2. Aortic semilunar
  3. Mitral (bicuspid)
  4. Pulmonary semilunar
  5. Eustachian valve
Answer: C. The mitral valve guards the left AV orifice.Module 10 heart deck, slide 12
Q449. Which valve prevents backflow into the RIGHT atrium?
  1. Bicuspid
  2. Semilunar
  3. Pulmonary
  4. Tricuspid
  5. Thebesian
Answer: D. The tricuspid valve guards the right AV orifice.Module 10 heart deck, slide 12
Q450. Systole refers to ____ and diastole refers to ____.
  1. relaxation; contraction
  2. filling; ejection
  3. depolarization; repolarization
  4. inflow; outflow
  5. contraction; relaxation
Answer: E. Systole is contraction and ejection; diastole is relaxation and filling.Module 10 heart deck, slide 8
Q451. Trace blood correctly from the body back to the body:
  1. RA → RV → lungs → LA → LV → body
  2. LA → LV → lungs → RA → RV → body
  3. RA → LA → RV → LV → lungs → body
  4. RV → RA → lungs → LV → LA → body
  5. LV → LA → lungs → RV → RA → body
Answer: A. Deoxygenated blood enters the right heart, goes to the lungs, returns to the left heart, and is pumped to the body.Module 10 heart deck, slide 3
Q452. The pulmonary circuit carries:
  1. Oxygenated blood from the left heart out to the body
  2. Deoxygenated blood from the right heart to the lungs
  3. Oxygenated blood from the lungs to the right heart
  4. Blood only to the heart muscle by way of the coronary arteries
  5. Lymph from the tissues to the thoracic duct
Answer: B. The pulmonary artery is the one artery carrying deoxygenated blood.Module 10 heart deck, slide 3
Q453. Cardiac output is defined as:
  1. Blood ejected by one ventricle with each beat
  2. Total blood volume held in the heart chambers
  3. Blood pumped by each ventricle per minute
  4. Pressure generated during systole
  5. Heart rate alone, measured in beats per minute
Answer: C. CO = heart rate x stroke volume, typically around 5 L/min at rest.Module 10 heart deck, slide 14
Q454. If heart rate is 70 bpm and stroke volume is 70 mL, cardiac output is:
  1. 1.0 L/min
  2. 14 L/min
  3. 0.49 L/min
  4. 4.9 L/min
  5. 49 L/min
Answer: D. 70 x 70 = 4900 mL/min = 4.9 L/min.Module 10 heart deck, slide 14
Q455. Which change would directly INCREASE cardiac output?
  1. Decreased heart rate with stroke volume unchanged
  2. Decreased stroke volume from poor ventricular filling
  3. Increased parasympathetic tone slowing the SA node
  4. Reduced venous return lowering end-diastolic volume
  5. Increased heart rate with stroke volume maintained
Answer: E. Since CO = HR x SV, raising either factor raises output.Module 10 heart deck, slide 15
Q456. Sympathetic stimulation of the heart produces:
  1. Increased rate and increased contractility
  2. Decreased rate and contractility
  3. Decreased rate with contractility unchanged
  4. No change in rate, contractility, or output
  5. Slowed conduction through the atrioventricular node
Answer: A. Sympathetic input raises both HR and the force of contraction, boosting cardiac output.Module 10 heart deck, slide 15
Q457. Vagal (parasympathetic) stimulation of the heart primarily:
  1. Increases heart rate
  2. Decreases heart rate
  3. Increases contractility strongly
  4. Closes the aortic valve
  5. Raises blood pressure
Answer: B. The vagus nerve slows SA node firing, lowering heart rate.Module 10 heart deck, slide 15
Q458. A patient in severe hemorrhage has reduced venous return. The effect on stroke volume is:
  1. Increased, because the ventricle fills more completely
  2. Unchanged, since stroke volume is set by heart rate
  3. Decreased, because less filling means less preload
  4. Doubled, as the ventricle compensates for blood loss
  5. Eliminated entirely, with no blood ejected at all
Answer: C. Less preload means less stretch and a weaker contraction — the Frank-Starling relationship.Module 10 heart deck, slide 15
Q459. The normal pacemaker of the heart is the:
  1. AV node
  2. Bundle of His
  3. Purkinje fibers
  4. SA node
  5. Left bundle branch
Answer: D. The SA node has the fastest intrinsic rate, so it sets the pace for the whole heart.Module 10 heart deck, slide 5
Q460. Place the conduction pathway in correct order:
  1. AV node → SA node → Purkinje fibers → Bundle of His → bundle branches
  2. Purkinje fibers → SA node → AV node → Bundle of His → bundle branches
  3. SA node → Purkinje fibers → AV node → Bundle of His → bundle branches
  4. Bundle of His → SA node → AV node → bundle branches → Purkinje fibers
  5. SA node → AV node → Bundle of His → bundle branches → Purkinje fibers
Answer: E. Impulse originates at the SA node and spreads down to the Purkinje network.Module 10 heart deck, slide 17
Q461. The physiological purpose of the AV nodal delay is to:
  1. Allow the atria to finish emptying into the ventricles before ventricular contraction
  2. Slow the overall heart rate permanently so that diastole is always longer than systole
  3. Prevent the SA node from firing again until the ventricles have fully repolarized
  4. Increase ventricular contractility by allowing more calcium to enter the myocardium
  5. Keep the semilunar valves closed while the ventricles are ejecting blood
Answer: A. Without the delay, atria and ventricles would contract simultaneously and filling would be inadequate.Module 10 heart deck, slide 5
Q462. The Bundle of His conducts impulses from the:
  1. SA node across the atrial myocardium to the left atrium
  2. AV node into the interventricular septum and bundle branches
  3. Purkinje fibers of the ventricular walls back up to the atria
  4. Aortic root through the septum into the left ventricle
  5. Coronary sinus into the wall of the right atrium
Answer: B. It is the only normal electrical bridge between atria and ventricles.Module 10 heart deck, slide 6
Q463. Purkinje fibers function to:
  1. Delay the impulse briefly so that the atria can empty before the ventricles contract
  2. Generate the normal pacemaker rhythm that sets heart rate at sixty to one hundred beats per minute
  3. Rapidly distribute the impulse through the ventricular walls for a coordinated contraction
  4. Close the atrioventricular valves by pulling on the chordae tendineae during systole
  5. Supply oxygenated blood to the myocardium through the right and left coronary arteries
Answer: C. They allow the ventricles to contract almost simultaneously from the apex upward.Module 10 heart deck, slide 6
Q464. The P wave on an ECG represents:
  1. Ventricular depolarization
  2. Ventricular repolarization
  3. Atrial repolarization
  4. Atrial depolarization
  5. Ventricular filling
Answer: D. The P wave precedes atrial contraction.Module 10 heart deck, slide 7
Q465. The QRS complex represents:
  1. Atrial depolarization
  2. Ventricular repolarization
  3. Atrial repolarization only
  4. Coronary artery perfusion
  5. Ventricular depolarization
Answer: E. Atrial repolarization occurs at the same time but is hidden within the much larger QRS.Module 10 heart deck, slide 7
Q466. The T wave represents:
  1. Ventricular repolarization
  2. Atrial depolarization
  3. Ventricular depolarization
  4. Atrial repolarization
  5. Semilunar valve closure
Answer: A. The T wave is ventricular recovery, preparing for the next beat.Module 10 heart deck, slide 7
Q467. Why is atrial repolarization not normally visible on an ECG?
  1. It does not occur in healthy atrial tissue
  2. It is masked by the much larger QRS complex
  3. It occurs during the P wave and cancels it out
  4. It produces no electrical change
  5. It happens well after the T wave has ended
Answer: B. The ventricular mass generates a far larger signal that obscures it.Module 10 heart deck, slide 7
Q468. Cardiac muscle cells contract as a coordinated unit because they are joined by:
  1. Tight junctions that seal the cell membranes
  2. Desmosomes alone, without any gap junctions
  3. Gap junctions within intercalated discs
  4. Chemical synapses between adjacent fibers
  5. A shared basement membrane and collagen
Answer: C. Gap junctions allow current to pass directly, making the myocardium a functional syncytium.Module 10 heart deck, slide 5
Q469. If the SA node fails, the AV node can take over at a slower intrinsic rate. This property is called:
  1. Contractility
  2. Conductivity
  3. Excitability
  4. Automaticity
  5. Elasticity
Answer: D. Several cardiac tissues possess automaticity, providing backup pacemakers.Lecture Outline, Module 10 — p.113
Q470. A complete block at the AV node would result in:
  1. Immediate cardiac arrest with no ventricular activity
  2. Faster ventricular rate than atrial
  3. Loss of the P wave only
  4. Normal conduction
  5. Atria and ventricles beating independently
Answer: E. In third-degree block the ventricles escape at their own slower intrinsic rate, dissociated from the atria.Module 10 heart deck, slide 6
Q471. Damage to the left ventricle after myocardial infarction most directly reduces:
  1. Stroke volume and therefore cardiac output
  2. Venous return from the systemic circulation
  3. Heart rate set by the sinoatrial node
  4. The force of right atrial contraction
  5. Valve integrity only, with pumping preserved
Answer: A. Scar tissue is non-contractile, so ejection falls.Module 10 heart deck, slide 14
Q472. The pericardial fluid functions to:
  1. Nourish the myocardium with oxygen
  2. Reduce friction as the heart beats
  3. Conduct impulses to the ventricles
  4. Store calcium for contraction
  5. Filter waste from the blood
Answer: B. Serous fluid lets the heart move freely within the pericardial sac.Lecture Outline, Module 10 — p.113
Q473. The myocardium is thickest in the:
  1. Right atrium
  2. Right ventricle
  3. Left ventricle
  4. Left atrium
  5. Interatrial septum
Answer: C. The left ventricle must generate systemic pressure, so its wall is thickest.Lecture Outline, Module 10 — p.113
Q474. Coronary arteries fill mainly during:
  1. Ventricular systole
  2. Atrial systole
  3. Isovolumetric contraction
  4. Ventricular diastole
  5. Ejection
Answer: D. During systole the vessels are compressed; filling occurs at rest.Module 10 heart deck, slide 10
Q475. Preload is best defined as:
  1. Resistance the ventricle pumps against
  2. The number of beats the heart makes in one full minute
  3. The intrinsic force of myocardial contraction
  4. The volume of blood ejected with each beat
  5. The degree of ventricular stretch at end-diastole
Answer: E. Preload reflects venous return and end-diastolic volume.Lecture Outline, Module 10 — p.113
Q476. Afterload refers to:
  1. The resistance the ventricle must overcome to eject blood
  2. The volume of blood returning to the right atrium
  3. The volume of blood in the ventricle at the end of diastole
  4. The number of cardiac cycles completed each minute
  5. The intrinsic strength of myocardial fiber shortening
Answer: A. Increased afterload, as in hypertension, makes ejection harder.Lecture Outline, Module 10 — p.113
Q477. The Frank-Starling relationship states that:
  1. Heart rate alone determines the volume of blood ejected each beat
  2. Greater end-diastolic stretch produces a stronger contraction
  3. Rising afterload increases the volume ejected each beat
  4. Contractility is fixed and cannot be altered by nerves
  5. Preload has no effect on the force of contraction
Answer: B. More filling stretches the fibers, improving cross-bridge overlap.Lecture Outline, Module 10 — p.113
Q478. Ejection fraction is calculated as:
  1. Heart rate multiplied by stroke volume
  2. End-systolic volume over end-diastolic volume
  3. Stroke volume divided by end-diastolic volume
  4. Cardiac output divided by heart rate
  5. Preload divided by afterload pressure
Answer: C. EF expresses the proportion of the filled ventricle that is ejected.Module 10 heart deck, slide 11
Q479. An ejection fraction of 30% indicates:
  1. Normal ventricular function for an adult
  2. Excellent contractility above the normal range
  3. A normal athletic adaptation to endurance training
  4. Reduced systolic function, as in heart failure
  5. Dehydration with a low circulating volume
Answer: D. Normal is roughly 55-70%; 30% reflects significant systolic dysfunction.Module 10 heart deck, slide 11
Q480. The intrinsic rate of the SA node is approximately:
  1. 20-30 bpm
  2. 40-60 bpm
  3. 100-140 bpm
  4. 140-180 bpm
  5. 60-100 bpm
Answer: E. The SA node's faster rate lets it override slower pacemakers.Lecture Outline, Module 10 — p.113
Q481. The plateau phase of the cardiac action potential is produced by:
  1. Calcium influx balancing potassium efflux
  2. Rapid sodium influx through fast channels
  3. Potassium influx through leak channels
  4. Chloride efflux across the sarcolemma
  5. The sodium-potassium pump restoring gradients
Answer: A. Slow calcium channels sustain depolarization, prolonging contraction.Module 10 heart deck, slide 5
Q482. Why is a long cardiac refractory period important?
  1. It speeds conduction through the AV node
  2. It prevents tetany so the heart can refill
  3. It increases the force of each contraction
  4. It slows the firing rate of the SA node
  5. It closes the atrioventricular valves
Answer: B. Cardiac muscle cannot be tetanized, guaranteeing time to fill.Module 10 heart deck, slide 8
Q483. A prolonged PR interval indicates delay at the:
  1. SA node
  2. Purkinje fibers
  3. AV node
  4. Ventricles
  5. Bundle branches
Answer: C. The PR interval reflects atrial depolarization plus AV nodal delay.Module 10 heart deck, slide 5
Q484. Atrial fibrillation would most likely show:
  1. Regular P waves before every QRS complex
  2. A widened QRS complex with otherwise normal P waves
  3. A tall peaked T wave with a regular rhythm
  4. Absent organized P waves with an irregular rhythm
  5. A prolonged plateau phase in the ST segment
Answer: D. Chaotic atrial activity replaces discrete P waves.Lecture Outline, Module 10 — p.113
Q485. Which change increases cardiac output during exercise?
  1. Reduced venous return from the exercising skeletal muscle beds
  2. Increased vagal tone slowing the sinoatrial node
  3. Decreased preload and lower end-diastolic volume
  4. Increased afterload only, with no change in heart rate
  5. Increased heart rate and contractility from sympathetic drive
Answer: E. Sympathetic stimulation raises both determinants of output.Module 10 heart deck, slide 15
Q486. Blood in the pulmonary VEINS is:
  1. Oxygenated
  2. Deoxygenated
  3. Mixed venous
  4. Lymph
  5. Filtered plasma
Answer: A. Pulmonary veins are the exception carrying oxygenated blood to the heart.Module 10 heart deck, slide 3
Q487. B cells contribute to immunity by:
  1. Directly killing virus-infected cells by releasing perforin
  2. Differentiating into plasma cells that secrete antibodies
  3. Engulfing and digesting bacteria inside a phagolysosome
  4. Producing myelin sheaths around peripheral nerve axons
  5. Forming the keratinized physical barrier of the epidermis
Answer: B. B cells drive humoral (antibody-mediated) adaptive immunity.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 14
Q488. Natural killer cells are classified as ____ immunity and act by ____.
  1. adaptive; producing antigen-specific antibodies after clonal selection and class switching
  2. adaptive; forming long-lived memory cells only after a first antigen exposure
  3. innate; destroying virus-infected and tumor cells without prior exposure
  4. innate; secreting antibodies into mucosal surfaces and breast milk
  5. adaptive; engulfing opsonized pathogens and presenting their antigen
Answer: C. Despite being lymphocytes, NK cells need no prior sensitization, so they are innate.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 12
Q489. Helper T cells are essential because they:
  1. Directly lyse virus-infected cells by releasing perforin and granzyme granules
  2. Secrete antibodies themselves after differentiating into plasma cells
  3. Form the keratinized barrier layer of the skin surface
  4. Activate B cells and cytotoxic T cells and release cytokines
  5. Clot blood by forming a platelet plug at the injury site
Answer: D. They coordinate the whole adaptive response — which is why HIV, by destroying them, is so devastating.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 18
Q490. Cytotoxic T cells kill target cells by:
  1. Secreting antibodies that neutralize free virus particles
  2. Engulfing them into a phagosome and digesting them
  3. Releasing histamine to increase capillary permeability
  4. Coating the target with complement proteins for opsonization
  5. Releasing perforin and granzymes to induce apoptosis
Answer: E. They punch holes in the target membrane and trigger programd cell death.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 19
Q491. Memory cells are important because they:
  1. Enable a faster, stronger response on re-exposure to the same antigen
  2. Prevent every future infection by any pathogen encountered, permanently and completely
  3. Produce the histamine that drives vasodilation and swelling during inflammation
  4. Form the innate physical barrier at the body's outer surface
  5. Digest engulfed pathogens using lysosomal enzymes and free radicals
Answer: A. This accelerated secondary response is the principle underlying vaccination.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 17
Q492. Which cell type actually secretes antibodies?
  1. Naive B cell
  2. Plasma cell
  3. Helper T cell
  4. Cytotoxic T cell
  5. Macrophage
Answer: B. Activated B cells differentiate into plasma cells, which are dedicated antibody factories.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 7
Q493. Which cells are the hallmark of ADAPTIVE immunity?
  1. Neutrophils and tissue macrophages
  2. NK cells and circulating basophils
  3. T lymphocytes and B lymphocytes
  4. Eosinophils and tissue mast cells
  5. Platelets and mature erythrocytes
Answer: C. Only T and B cells display antigen specificity and immunological memory.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 17
Q494. The thymus functions as the site of:
  1. B cell maturation
  2. Red blood cell production
  3. Antibody storage
  4. T cell maturation
  5. Lymph filtration
Answer: D. T cells mature and are selected in the thymus, which involutes after puberty.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 6
Q495. The spleen's principal immune function is to:
  1. Filter lymph and trap antigens draining from the tissues
  2. Produce and mature naive T lymphocytes before release
  3. Store and concentrate bile produced by the liver
  4. Generate cerebrospinal fluid in the choroid plexus
  5. Filter blood and remove aged or damaged red blood cells
Answer: E. The spleen screens blood-borne pathogens and recycles worn-out erythrocytes.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 4
Q496. Lymph nodes function to:
  1. Filter lymph and house lymphocytes and macrophages
  2. Filter blood and remove worn-out red blood cells
  3. Produce hormones that regulate the metabolic rate
  4. Store platelets and release them during bleeding
  5. Absorb digested nutrients across the intestinal wall
Answer: A. Nodes trap antigens traveling in lymph and present them to immune cells.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 3
Q497. MALT refers to lymphoid tissue located:
  1. In the red bone marrow cavities where B lymphocytes first develop and mature
  2. At mucosal surfaces such as the gut, airways and tonsils
  3. In the white pulp of the spleen and in no other site in the body
  4. Within the cortex and medulla of the thymus gland
  5. In the liver alongside the bile-producing hepatocytes
Answer: B. Mucosa-Associated Lymphoid Tissue guards the body's largest points of contact with the outside world.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 5
Q498. Which are the PRIMARY lymphoid organs?
  1. Lymph nodes and spleen
  2. Tonsils and Peyer's patches
  3. Red bone marrow and thymus
  4. Spleen and MALT
  5. Liver and thymus
Answer: C. Primary organs are where lymphocytes develop; secondary organs are where they meet antigen.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 6
Q499. The lymphatic system helps maintain blood volume by:
  1. Producing the plasma albumin proteins that hold water inside the blood vessels
  2. Filtering worn-out red blood cells out of the circulation inside the thymus gland
  3. Absorbing water from the large intestine into portal blood
  4. Collecting excess interstitial fluid and returning it to the bloodstream
  5. Transporting oxygen from the lungs out to the working tissues
Answer: D. Capillaries leak fluid continuously; lymphatics recover it and return it via the subclavian veins.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 2
Q500. Blockage of lymphatic drainage would most likely produce:
  1. Whole-body dehydration
  2. Increased blood volume
  3. Anemia with low hemoglobin
  4. Sustained arterial hypertension
  5. Localized edema (swelling)
Answer: E. Fluid that cannot be collected accumulates in the tissue — lymphedema.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 2
Q501. Why do lymph nodes swell during infection?
  1. Lymphocytes proliferate rapidly within them as they respond to trapped antigen
  2. Blood diverted from the nearby arteries pools inside them and distends the capsule
  3. They fill only with pus formed from dead neutrophils and other cellular debris
  4. The fibrous capsule breaks down and lets tissue fluid swell the node
  5. They absorb water osmotically from the surrounding interstitial space
Answer: A. Swollen nodes are a sign the adaptive response is being mounted.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 17
Q502. Lymph differs from blood in that lymph:
  1. Contains just as many red blood cells per microliter as whole circulating blood
  2. Contains no red blood cells and travels in one direction toward the heart
  3. Is pumped around the body by the rhythmic contractions of the left ventricle
  4. Carries oxygen to the tissues bound to hemoglobin molecules
  5. Clots faster than blood because it holds more fibrinogen
Answer: B. Lymph is a one-way collection system driven by muscle movement and valves, not a pump.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 6
Q503. Innate immunity is best characterized as:
  1. Slow, highly specific, with memory
  2. Slow, non-specific, with memory
  3. Fast, non-specific, without memory
  4. Fast, antigen-specific, with memory
  5. Present only after vaccination
Answer: C. Innate defenses act within minutes but cannot distinguish one pathogen from another.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 15
Q504. Adaptive immunity is best characterized as:
  1. Fast, entirely non-specific, and identical in magnitude on every exposure
  2. Present from birth at full strength and completely unchanged by later exposures
  3. Non-specific but able to generate lasting memory cells
  4. Slower on first exposure, highly specific, and generates memory
  5. Effective only against parasitic worms and fungal spores
Answer: D. Specificity and memory are the two defining features of adaptive immunity.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 17
Q505. Which is the body's FIRST line of defense?
  1. Antibodies in the circulating blood plasma
  2. Activated cytotoxic T cells in the tissues
  3. Antigen-specific memory B cells
  4. The complement protein cascade
  5. Intact skin and mucous membranes
Answer: E. Physical and chemical barriers prevent entry before any immune cell is needed.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 10
Q506. The complement system functions primarily to:
  1. Enhance phagocytosis, promote inflammation and lyse pathogens
  2. Replace antibody molecules after they have been used up in a response
  3. Produce brand-new lymphocytes inside the red bone marrow of the flat bones
  4. Store captured antigens for presentation at a later date
  5. Clot blood by converting fibrinogen into fibrin strands
Answer: A. Complement 'complements' antibody and phagocyte action, and can form the membrane attack complex.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 14
Q507. The membrane attack complex kills pathogens by:
  1. Digesting them internally inside a phagolysosome full of acid hydrolases
  2. Forming pores in the pathogen membrane causing lysis
  3. Producing antibodies that agglutinate pathogens into large clumps
  4. Blocking the surface receptors they use to attach
  5. Starving them of the iron they need to multiply
Answer: B. Pore formation destroys osmotic integrity and the cell bursts.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 14
Q508. Opsonization refers to:
  1. Direct killing of virus-infected cells by cytotoxic T cells releasing perforin
  2. Production of large amounts of antibody by activated plasma cells
  3. Coating a pathogen to make it easier for phagocytes to engulf
  4. Generation of fever by pyrogens acting on the hypothalamus
  5. Sealing a damaged vessel by forming a fibrin clot from fibrinogen
Answer: C. Complement and antibody act as handles that phagocytes can grip.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 21
Q509. Which is an example of an innate, non-specific defense?
  1. Antibody production
  2. Memory T cell activation
  3. Plasma cell differentiation
  4. Fever and inflammation
  5. Vaccination response
Answer: D. Fever and inflammation occur regardless of the specific pathogen involved.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 16
Q510. Active immunity differs from passive immunity in that active immunity:
  1. Provides immediate protection that fades within weeks and leaves no memory
  2. Comes from ready-made antibodies transferred from another person's plasma
  3. Never involves B cells or any antibody production at all
  4. Occurs only in infants during the first year of life
  5. Results from your own immune response and produces lasting memory
Answer: E. Passive immunity (maternal antibodies, immunoglobulin injections) is immediate but temporary and leaves no memory.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 17
Q511. A patient receives antibodies from a donor for immediate protection. This is:
  1. Passive immunity
  2. Active natural immunity
  3. Innate immunity
  4. Adaptive cellular immunity
  5. Autoimmunity
Answer: A. Borrowed antibodies protect now but confer no memory once they degrade.Lecture Outline, Module 11 — p.122
Q512. Why is the secondary immune response faster than the primary?
  1. A much larger dose of antigen is always present on the second encounter
  2. Memory cells already exist and can proliferate immediately
  3. The complement cascade has been permanently strengthened by the first infection
  4. The skin barrier has thickened since the first exposure
  5. Antibodies made at the first exposure never break down
Answer: B. Memory cells bypass the lengthy initial activation and clonal expansion phase.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 4
Q513. An autoimmune disease occurs when:
  1. The immune system stops responding to any antigen at all, leaving the person defenseless
  2. Only the innate defenses remain because every adaptive lymphocyte has been destroyed
  3. The immune system attacks the body's own tissues, failing to recognize self
  4. The complement proteins are completely absent from the blood plasma
  5. All memory lymphocytes are lost within days of the first infection
Answer: C. Loss of self-tolerance underlies conditions such as rheumatoid arthritis and type 1 diabetes.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 2
Q514. A person lacking functional helper T cells would be most vulnerable because:
  1. All innate immunity, including neutrophil phagocytosis, would fail completely
  2. Only the skin and mucous barriers would be left, with no complement activity
  3. Complement proteins could no longer be manufactured by the liver
  4. Both antibody production and cytotoxic T cell activity would be impaired
  5. Red blood cell production in the red bone marrow would stop
Answer: D. Helper T cells are the hub coordinating both arms of adaptive immunity.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 18
Q515. Lymph ultimately returns to the bloodstream at the:
  1. Aorta
  2. Renal artery
  3. Hepatic portal vein
  4. Pulmonary artery
  5. Subclavian veins
Answer: E. The thoracic and right lymphatic ducts drain into the subclavian veins.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 2
Q516. Lacteals are specialized lymphatic vessels that absorb:
  1. Dietary fats
  2. Simple sugars
  3. Amino acids
  4. Plain water
  5. Electrolytes
Answer: A. Lacteals in intestinal villi take up chylomicrons.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 2
Q517. Which is a physical barrier of innate immunity?
  1. Antibodies in the circulating blood plasma
  2. Intact skin and mucous membranes
  3. T lymphocytes circulating in the lymph nodes
  4. Long-lived memory B lymphocytes
  5. The complement protein cascade
Answer: B. Barriers block entry before any cellular response is needed.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 10
Q518. Interferons act primarily against:
  1. Bacteria
  2. Parasites
  3. Viruses
  4. Fungi
  5. Toxins
Answer: C. Infected cells release interferon to warn neighboring cells.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 13
Q519. The cardinal signs of inflammation include all EXCEPT:
  1. Redness
  2. Heat
  3. Swelling
  4. Pallor
  5. Pain
Answer: D. Inflamed tissue is red and warm from vasodilation, not pale.Lecture Outline, Module 11 — p.122
Q520. Fever aids defense by:
  1. Cooling the deep tissues so that bacteria divide much more slowly
  2. Reducing blood flow through the infected tissue
  3. Blocking antibodies from binding their target antigen
  4. Lowering the metabolic rate of every cell in the body to save energy
  5. Inhibiting pathogen growth and enhancing immune activity
Answer: E. Moderate fever slows pathogens and speeds immune reactions.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 16
Q521. MHC class I molecules present antigen to:
  1. Cytotoxic T cells
  2. Helper T lymphocytes
  3. Naive B lymphocytes
  4. Neutrophils
  5. Mast cells
Answer: A. All nucleated cells display MHC I, allowing cytotoxic T cell surveillance.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 19
Q522. Which immunoglobulin crosses the placenta to protect the fetus?
  1. IgA
  2. IgG
  3. IgM
  4. IgE
  5. IgD
Answer: B. IgG is the only class transferred across the placenta.Lecture Outline, Module 11 — p.122
Q523. Which immunoglobulin predominates in breast milk and mucosal secretions?
  1. IgG
  2. IgM
  3. IgA
  4. IgE
  5. IgD
Answer: C. Secretory IgA protects mucosal surfaces.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 10
Q524. Which immunoglobulin is produced first in a primary response?
  1. IgG
  2. IgA
  3. IgE
  4. IgM
  5. IgD
Answer: D. IgM appears first, then class switching produces IgG.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 15
Q525. IgE is most associated with:
  1. Neutralizing bacterial toxins in the plasma
  2. Fixing complement by the classical pathway
  3. Crossing the placenta to protect the fetus
  4. Mucosal defense in saliva and breast milk
  5. Allergic reactions and parasite defense
Answer: E. IgE binds mast cells and triggers histamine release.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 11
Q526. Vaccination produces which type of immunity?
  1. Active artificial
  2. Passive natural
  3. Passive artificial
  4. Innate
  5. Active natural
Answer: A. A vaccine provokes your own response and memory without disease.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 11
Q527. Maternal antibodies crossing the placenta give the newborn:
  1. Active natural immunity
  2. Passive natural immunity
  3. Innate barrier immunity
  4. Active artificial immunity
  5. No immune protection
Answer: B. Borrowed antibodies protect temporarily and leave no memory.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 12
Q528. Anaphylaxis is best described as:
  1. A localized itchy skin rash confined to the point of contact with the allergen
  2. A chronic autoimmune disease of the synovial joints and their connective tissue
  3. A severe systemic hypersensitivity with hypotension and bronchoconstriction
  4. An immunodeficiency caused by the absence of helper T lymphocytes
  5. A delayed cell-mediated reaction peaking after forty-eight hours
Answer: C. Massive mediator release causes airway and circulatory collapse.Lecture Outline, Module 11 — p.122
Q529. HIV progressively destroys which cell, crippling immunity?
  1. Circulating B cells
  2. Blood neutrophils
  3. Mature erythrocytes
  4. Helper T (CD4) cells
  5. Circulating platelets
Answer: D. Loss of the coordinating helper T cell disables both adaptive arms.Module 11 immune & lymph deck, slide 18
Q530. During inhalation, the diaphragm ____ and intrapulmonary pressure ____.
  1. relaxes and domes upward; rises above atmospheric
  2. contracts and flattens; rises above atmospheric
  3. relaxes while the ribs drop; falls below atmospheric
  4. remains motionless; stays equal to atmospheric
  5. contracts and flattens; falls below atmospheric
Answer: E. Boyle's law: increasing thoracic volume lowers pressure, so air flows in.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 2
Q531. During quiet exhalation, the diaphragm ____ and air ____.
  1. relaxes and rises, raising pressure; leaves
  2. contracts and flattens, lowering pressure; enters
  3. contracts and pulls downward; leaves
  4. relaxes and rises, raising pressure; enters
  5. stays fixed in position; airflow stops entirely
Answer: A. Quiet exhalation is passive — elastic recoil does the work.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 2
Q532. Air and gases always move:
  1. From low to high pressure, up their gradient
  2. From high to low pressure, down their gradient
  3. Only by active transport using ATP-driven pumps
  4. Against their gradient whenever oxygen is needed
  5. Only when the diaphragm relaxes during exhalation
Answer: B. Every step of ventilation and gas exchange is gradient-driven and passive.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 6
Q533. Surfactant is produced by ____ and functions to ____.
  1. Type I alveolar cells; form the thin barrier across which oxygen diffuses into blood
  2. alveolar macrophages; engulf inhaled bacteria, dust and other debris in the alveoli
  3. Type II alveolar cells; reduce surface tension and prevent alveolar collapse
  4. goblet cells; secrete the mucus that traps debris in the conducting airways
  5. capillary endothelium; line the vessels carrying blood past the alveolar wall
Answer: C. Without surfactant, surface tension would collapse small alveoli — the problem in neonatal RDS.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 8
Q534. Lung compliance refers to:
  1. The rate of diffusion of gases
  2. Blood flow through the lungs
  3. Oxygen-carrying capacity of blood
  4. How easily the lungs expand
  5. Resistance in the airways only
Answer: D. Surfactant increases compliance; fibrosis decreases it.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 8
Q535. A patient with pulmonary fibrosis has stiff, scarred lungs. Compliance is:
  1. Increased, so the scarred lungs inflate far more easily than healthy ones
  2. Unchanged, since fibrous scar tissue does not alter how the lung expands
  3. Infinite, because stiff tissue stretches without limit
  4. Irrelevant, since compliance affects only gas exchange
  5. Decreased, so more effort is required to inflate the lungs
Answer: E. Scarred tissue resists expansion, increasing the work of breathing.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q536. Perfusion refers to:
  1. Blood flow to the pulmonary capillaries
  2. Movement of air into and out of the alveoli
  3. Diffusion of gas across the respiratory membrane
  4. Oxygen binding to hemoglobin in red cells
  5. Surfactant production by Type II cells
Answer: A. Ventilation delivers air; perfusion delivers blood. Both must be adequate and matched.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 4
Q537. A pulmonary embolism blocks blood flow to a lung region. The primary problem is:
  1. Inadequate ventilation, so the well-perfused alveoli receive no fresh air
  2. Inadequate perfusion, so ventilated alveoli cannot exchange gas
  3. Excess surfactant release, which overinflates the alveoli beyond the clot
  4. Airway obstruction that traps air in the affected lobe
  5. Reduced compliance alone, making the lung stiff to inflate
Answer: B. The alveoli receive air but no blood, creating dead space and impairing oxygenation.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 7
Q538. Gas exchange occurs at the:
  1. Nasopharynx
  2. Diaphragm
  3. Alveoli
  4. Pleura
  5. Larynx
Answer: C. Only the alveoli have the thin walls and capillary contact required for diffusion.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 7
Q539. Which three factors most influence the rate of gas exchange?
  1. Body temperature, arterial blood pH and total lung capacity
  2. Heart rate, respiratory rate and the resting tidal volume of a breath
  3. Blood color, plasma viscosity and the density of the alveolar gas mixture
  4. Surface area, membrane thickness and partial pressure gradient
  5. Airway diameter, mucus thickness and ciliary beating
Answer: D. Greater area and steeper gradient speed exchange; a thicker membrane slows it.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 9
Q540. Oxygen moves from alveoli into blood because:
  1. It is actively pumped across the alveolar wall by ATP
  2. Plasma proteins chemically attract oxygen out of the alveoli
  3. The alveoli contract and squeeze oxygen into the capillary
  4. Surfactant pushes oxygen through the respiratory membrane
  5. Alveolar oxygen partial pressure exceeds that in the blood
Answer: E. Diffusion always follows the partial pressure gradient.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 6
Q541. Carbon dioxide moves from blood into alveoli because:
  1. Blood CO2 partial pressure exceeds alveolar CO2 partial pressure
  2. Hemoglobin actively pumps it out of the plasma into the alveolar air
  3. It is lighter than oxygen and simply rises into the alveoli
  4. Alveolar CO2 partial pressure exceeds the CO2 partial pressure of blood
  5. Type II alveolar cells actively transport it out of blood
Answer: A. CO2 also follows its own gradient, in the opposite direction to oxygen.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 6
Q542. Oxygen is transported in blood primarily:
  1. Dissolved directly in the plasma as free gas
  2. Bound to hemoglobin in red blood cells
  3. As bicarbonate ions dissolved in plasma
  4. Bound to albumin carried in the plasma
  5. As carbonic acid formed inside red cells
Answer: B. About 98% of oxygen is carried on hemoglobin; only a small fraction dissolves in plasma.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 4
Q543. Carbon dioxide is transported in blood primarily:
  1. Bound directly to hemoglobin
  2. Dissolved as free gas bubbles
  3. As bicarbonate ion in plasma
  4. Bound to plasma albumin
  5. As carbon monoxide in plasma
Answer: C. Roughly 70% travels as bicarbonate, with smaller amounts on hemoglobin and dissolved.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q544. Hemoglobin's role is to:
  1. Produce the surfactant that lowers surface tension and keeps small alveoli open
  2. Digest the inhaled pathogens that reach the alveolar air spaces
  3. Clot blood by forming a fibrin mesh across an injured vessel wall
  4. Bind and transport oxygen, releasing it where tissue partial pressure is low
  5. Filter dust and airborne debris out of inhaled air long before it reaches the alveoli
Answer: D. Each hemoglobin molecule can carry up to four oxygen molecules.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 6
Q545. Carbon dioxide affects blood pH by the reaction CO2 + H2O forming:
  1. Bicarbonate only, with no accompanying change in hydrogen ion concentration
  2. Oxygen and water vapor, which the lungs then exhale together
  3. Lactic acid, which then combines with bicarbonate and raises the blood pH
  4. Ammonia, which the kidneys must then excrete in the urine
  5. Carbonic acid, which dissociates to release hydrogen ions and lower pH
Answer: E. This links respiration directly to acid-base balance.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 10
Q546. A patient who retains carbon dioxide will develop:
  1. Respiratory acidosis
  2. Respiratory alkalosis
  3. Metabolic alkalosis
  4. No acid-base change
  5. Increased blood pH
Answer: A. Retained CO2 generates H+, lowering pH.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q547. A patient hyperventilating blows off excess CO2, producing:
  1. Respiratory acidosis
  2. Respiratory alkalosis
  3. Metabolic acidosis
  4. Unchanged blood pH
  5. Hypercapnia with acidosis
Answer: B. Losing CO2 removes acid, raising pH.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q548. Carbon monoxide is dangerous chiefly because it:
  1. Physically blocks the upper airway so that inhaled air never reaches the alveoli
  2. Destroys surfactant so that the alveoli collapse on every expiration
  3. Binds hemoglobin far more tightly than oxygen, preventing oxygen transport
  4. Collapses the alveoli by raising alveolar surface tension
  5. Raises the blood pH sharply, producing a severe and rapid respiratory alkalosis
Answer: C. CO occupies the oxygen binding sites with roughly 200 times the affinity.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q549. Type I alveolar cells are:
  1. Cuboidal cells that synthesize and secrete pulmonary surfactant
  2. Phagocytic cells that engulf inhaled debris
  3. Mucus-producing cells that line the larger conducting airways
  4. Thin squamous cells forming the gas exchange surface
  5. Ciliated cells that sweep mucus toward the pharynx
Answer: D. Their extreme thinness minimizes the diffusion distance.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 7
Q550. Type II alveolar cells function to:
  1. Perform gas exchange
  2. Engulf inhaled debris
  3. Line the bronchi
  4. Produce airway mucus
  5. Produce surfactant
Answer: E. They also serve as progenitors that can replace damaged Type I cells.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q551. Alveolar macrophages ('dust cells') function to:
  1. Engulf inhaled pathogens and particulate debris
  2. Produce the surfactant that lowers alveolar surface tension
  3. Exchange gases across the alveolar membrane
  4. Secrete mucus onto the alveolar surface
  5. Constrict the smooth muscle encircling the bronchioles
Answer: A. They are the last line of defense in the alveolus, where there are no cilia.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q552. Damage to Type I alveolar cells would most directly cause:
  1. Excess surfactant production and abnormally compliant alveoli
  2. Reduced gas exchange efficiency due to a thickened or disrupted membrane
  3. Complete airway obstruction caused by thick mucus plugging the small bronchioles
  4. Increased lung compliance, making the alveoli far easier to inflate than normal
  5. Reduced perfusion through the pulmonary capillary network
Answer: B. The exchange surface itself is compromised.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 7
Q553. A premature infant lacking surfactant will have alveoli that:
  1. Overinflate steadily, because alveolar surface tension has been abolished
  2. Fill with mucus secreted by the airway goblet cells
  3. Tend to collapse, greatly increasing the work of breathing
  4. Lose their capillary blood supply and become dead space
  5. Enlarge and merge into a few oversized air sacs, as they do in emphysema
Answer: C. High surface tension collapses alveoli at end-expiration — neonatal respiratory distress syndrome.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 8
Q554. Emphysema destroys alveolar walls. The main consequence is:
  1. Increased surface area available for gas exchange
  2. Excess surfactant flooding the enlarged air sacs
  3. Increased perfusion of the remaining alveoli
  4. Decreased surface area, impairing gas exchange
  5. Improved compliance with completely normal gas exchange
Answer: D. Merging alveoli into larger sacs drastically reduces total exchange surface.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 7
Q555. Which pairing correctly matches structure to function?
  1. Type I cell — surfactant production and secretion
  2. Type II cell — gas exchange across a thin wall
  3. Type I cell — phagocytosis of inhaled dust
  4. Type II cell — ciliary clearance of mucus
  5. Alveolar macrophage — phagocytosis of debris
Answer: E. Type I exchanges gas, Type II makes surfactant, macrophages clean up.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q556. Cilia in the conducting airways function to:
  1. Sweep mucus and trapped debris upward and out of the airway
  2. Exchange oxygen and carbon dioxide between air and the pulmonary blood
  3. Produce the surfactant that keeps the alveoli open
  4. Regulate the perfusion of blood through the pulmonary capillary beds
  5. Contract the diaphragm so that air is drawn inward
Answer: A. This mucociliary escalator is why smoking, which paralyzes cilia, causes chronic cough.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q557. The conducting zone of the airway functions to:
  1. Exchange oxygen and carbon dioxide between air and blood
  2. Warm, humidify and filter air while conducting it
  3. Produce the surfactant that reduces alveolar surface tension
  4. Absorb oxygen directly into the bloodstream
  5. Regulate arterial blood pH and nothing else
Answer: B. No exchange occurs here; it is anatomical dead space.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q558. Which muscle is the primary driver of quiet inspiration?
  1. Internal intercostals
  2. Abdominals
  3. Diaphragm
  4. Sternocleidomastoid
  5. Scalenes
Answer: C. Diaphragmatic contraction accounts for most resting tidal volume.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q559. Intrapleural pressure is normally:
  1. Exactly equal to atmospheric pressure at rest
  2. Strongly positive throughout the breathing cycle
  3. Zero, the same as alveolar pressure at rest
  4. Slightly negative relative to atmospheric
  5. Higher than alveolar pressure during inspiration
Answer: D. Negative pleural pressure keeps the lungs inflated against their recoil.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q560. A pneumothorax causes lung collapse because:
  1. Surfactant is washed away and surface tension pulls every alveolus shut
  2. The alveoli fill with fluid that blocks gas diffusion
  3. The diaphragm is paralyzed and can no longer contract during inspiration
  4. Blood flow through the pulmonary capillaries stops
  5. Air enters the pleural space and destroys the negative pressure
Answer: E. Losing the pressure gradient allows elastic recoil to collapse the lung.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 8
Q561. Tidal volume is defined as:
  1. Air moved in a normal quiet breath
  2. Maximum air inhaled after a full breath in
  3. Air remaining after maximal exhalation
  4. Total lung capacity after full inflation
  5. Vital capacity, the largest breath possible
Answer: A. Roughly 500 mL in a typical adult at rest.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q562. Residual volume is important because it:
  1. Is completely exhaled from the lungs during each quiet expiration
  2. Keeps alveoli open and permits continuous gas exchange
  3. Equals the tidal volume during quiet breathing
  4. Is driven out of the lungs completely by a single forceful cough
  5. Contains only carbon dioxide and no oxygen at all
Answer: B. Air that cannot be exhaled prevents alveolar collapse between breaths.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 7
Q563. The primary chemical stimulus driving normal respiration is:
  1. Falling oxygen sensed by the medullary chemoreceptors
  2. Rising arterial oxygen sensed by the carotid bodies
  3. Rising carbon dioxide and the resulting fall in pH
  4. Falling plasma potassium sensed by the kidneys
  5. Rising blood glucose after a large carbohydrate meal
Answer: C. Central chemoreceptors respond mainly to CO2-driven pH change in CSF.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q564. Central chemoreceptors are located in the:
  1. Carotid bodies
  2. Aortic arch bodies
  3. Alveolar walls
  4. Medulla oblongata
  5. Adrenal medulla
Answer: D. They sense pH change in cerebrospinal fluid.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q565. The Bohr effect describes how:
  1. Rising oxygen levels increase carbon dioxide binding
  2. Temperature has no measurable effect on oxygen binding by hemoglobin
  3. Hemoglobin never releases oxygen once it has bound it
  4. Rising CO2 and falling blood pH raise hemoglobin's affinity for oxygen
  5. Increased CO2 and lower pH reduce hemoglobin's oxygen affinity
Answer: E. Active tissue unloads more oxygen exactly where it is needed.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 4
Q566. A rightward shift of the oxygen-hemoglobin curve means:
  1. Decreased affinity, so oxygen is released more readily
  2. Increased affinity, so oxygen is held more tightly at the tissues
  3. No change in affinity, only in the total oxygen the blood carries
  4. Total oxygen-carrying capacity falls substantially
  5. Hemoglobin denatures and can no longer bind oxygen
Answer: A. Heat, acidity and high CO2 shift it right, favoring unloading.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q567. The chloride shift occurs to:
  1. Store extra oxygen inside the red blood cell by binding it to chloride ions
  2. Maintain electrical balance as bicarbonate leaves the red cell
  3. Generate the ATP that powers the ion pumps of the red cell membrane
  4. Buffer the alveolar air against sudden changes in pH
  5. Transport glucose into the red cell to fuel glycolysis
Answer: B. Chloride enters as bicarbonate exits, preserving neutrality.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 4
Q568. Ventilation-perfusion mismatch means:
  1. Airflow and blood flow are matched perfectly in every alveolus of both lungs
  2. The diaphragm fails to contract strongly enough during inspiration
  3. Ventilated alveoli are underperfused or perfused alveoli underventilated
  4. Surfactant is absent, so the alveoli collapse at end-expiration
  5. Pleural pressure becomes positive throughout inspiration and expiration alike
Answer: C. Either component alone is insufficient for gas exchange.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q569. In COPD, the primary problem is:
  1. Restricted lung expansion from stiff, scarred tissue
  2. Absent surfactant causing widespread alveolar collapse
  3. A pulmonary embolism blocking capillary blood flow
  4. Obstructed airflow, especially on expiration
  5. Pneumothorax with air trapped in the pleural space
Answer: D. Airway narrowing and loss of recoil trap air on exhalation.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q570. Which finding suggests chronic CO2 retention?
  1. Respiratory alkalosis with a low bicarbonate
  2. Metabolic alkalosis with a normal carbon dioxide
  3. Normal pH with a low arterial carbon dioxide
  4. Hyperventilation with rapid, deep breathing
  5. Respiratory acidosis with elevated bicarbonate
Answer: E. The kidneys retain bicarbonate to compensate for chronic acidosis.Module 12 respiratory deck, slide 10
Q571. Cheyne-Stokes respiration is characterized by:
  1. Cycles of increasing then decreasing depth separated by apnea
  2. Constant deep, labored breathing at a fast but perfectly steady rate
  3. Rapid shallow breaths at an unvarying rate and depth
  4. Complete apnea with no respiratory effort at all
  5. Normal quiet breathing at a steady twelve to sixteen breaths per minute
Answer: A. A crescendo-decrescendo pattern with pauses, seen in heart failure and CNS injury.Lecture Outline, Module 12 — p.140
Q572. Mechanical digestion differs from chemical digestion in that mechanical digestion:
  1. Uses enzymes such as amylase and pepsin to break covalent bonds
  2. Physically breaks food into smaller pieces without changing its chemical structure
  3. Takes place only inside the stomach, where powerful churning contractions break food up
  4. Requires bile from the gallbladder before any breakdown can begin
  5. Happens only in the colon, after nutrient absorption is complete
Answer: B. Chewing, churning and segmentation increase surface area so enzymes can work faster.Module 13 GI deck, slide 13
Q573. Chemical digestion of carbohydrate begins in the:
  1. Stomach
  2. Duodenum
  3. Mouth
  4. Jejunum
  5. Colon
Answer: C. Salivary amylase begins splitting starch into maltose as soon as food is chewed.Module 13 GI deck, slide 3
Q574. Chemical digestion of protein begins in the:
  1. Mouth
  2. Duodenum
  3. Ileum
  4. Stomach
  5. Colon
Answer: D. HCl activates pepsinogen into pepsin, which cleaves protein into peptides.Module 13 GI deck, slide 6
Q575. The bulk of fat digestion occurs in the:
  1. Mouth and pharynx
  2. Stomach and pylorus
  3. Large intestine
  4. Lower esophagus
  5. Small intestine
Answer: E. Bile emulsifies fat and pancreatic lipase digests it, both acting in the small intestine.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q576. Peristalsis is best defined as:
  1. Wave-like contractions that propel content forward
  2. Mixing contractions that do not advance content
  3. Chemical breakdown of nutrients by digestive enzymes
  4. Absorption of nutrients across the intestinal wall
  5. Storage of undigested waste in the rectum
Answer: A. Peristalsis moves; segmentation mixes.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q577. Segmentation differs from peristalsis in that segmentation:
  1. Propels the bolus forward rapidly toward the next segment
  2. Mixes chyme with digestive juices without net forward movement
  3. Occurs only in the esophagus during the swallowing reflex
  4. Requires bile from the gallbladder in order to occur at all
  5. Only happens during fasting, between meals, when the gut is empty
Answer: B. Segmentation maximizes contact between chyme and the absorptive surface.Module 13 GI deck, slide 3
Q578. The lower esophageal sphincter functions to:
  1. Begin protein digestion by releasing pepsinogen into the lumen
  2. Release stored bile into the duodenum when fat arrives
  3. Prevent stomach acid from refluxing into the esophagus
  4. Absorb water and electrolytes from the passing bolus
  5. Produce the mucus that lubricates the swallowed bolus
Answer: C. Incompetence of this sphincter produces gastro-esophageal reflux disease.Module 13 GI deck, slide 4
Q579. Which phase of swallowing is under voluntary control?
  1. The pharyngeal phase, when the soft palate seals the nasopharynx
  2. The esophageal phase, as peristaltic waves carry the bolus down
  3. All three phases are consciously controlled throughout
  4. The initial oral phase, when the tongue pushes the bolus back
  5. None of the phases; swallowing is entirely reflexive
Answer: D. Once the pharyngeal phase begins, swallowing becomes an involuntary reflex.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q580. During swallowing, the airway is protected because:
  1. The tongue folds back and blocks the trachea
  2. The esophagus closes off until the bolus passes
  3. Peristalsis briefly reverses to clear the airway
  4. The diaphragm contracts and seals the lower airway
  5. The epiglottis covers the laryngeal opening
Answer: E. The epiglottis diverts the bolus into the esophagus rather than the trachea.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q581. Chyme is best defined as:
  1. The semi-liquid mixture of food and gastric juice leaving the stomach
  2. The rounded ball of chewed food mixed with saliva that is formed in the mouth before swallowing
  3. The alkaline enzyme-rich fluid secreted by the pancreas into the duodenum
  4. The formed solid waste that is held in the rectum before defecation
  5. The absorbed nutrients traveling in the hepatic portal blood
Answer: A. The stomach's churning converts the bolus into chyme before releasing it into the duodenum.Module 13 GI deck, slide 4
Q582. Hydrochloric acid in the stomach serves to:
  1. Emulsify dietary fats into smaller droplets so that pancreatic lipase can reach them
  2. Denature proteins and convert pepsinogen into active pepsin
  3. Neutralize the chyme as it leaves the pylorus so intestinal enzymes can work
  4. Absorb vitamin B12 directly across the gastric lining
  5. Digest starch and other carbohydrate into simple sugars
Answer: B. The strongly acidic environment is essential for pepsin activity.Module 13 GI deck, slide 6
Q583. Why does the stomach not digest itself?
  1. The stomach makes no digestive enzymes, so nothing in it can break down its wall
  2. Its surface cells are already dead and keratinized, so enzymes cannot digest them
  3. A thick mucus layer protects the lining from acid and pepsin
  4. Bile refluxes upward and neutralizes the gastric acid
  5. It has no blood supply, so digestive enzymes never reach it
Answer: C. Breakdown of this mucosal barrier is what produces peptic ulcers.Module 13 GI deck, slide 6
Q584. Intrinsic factor is produced by ____ and is required for the absorption of ____.
  1. chief cells; dietary iron
  2. goblet cells; dietary calcium
  3. G cells; folate and vitamin C
  4. parietal cells; vitamin B12
  5. Paneth cells; vitamin K
Answer: D. Loss of parietal cells causes pernicious anemia — a stomach problem presenting as a blood problem.Module 13 GI deck, slide 6
Q585. Segmentation in the duodenum serves primarily to:
  1. Push chyme rapidly onward toward the colon
  2. Absorb the bulk of the remaining water
  3. Store waste until the defecation reflex
  4. Produce insulin and glucagon for glucose control
  5. Mix chyme with pancreatic enzymes and bile
Answer: E. Thorough mixing is essential because this is where most chemical digestion occurs.Module 13 GI deck, slide 3
Q586. Which region of the small intestine is the primary site of nutrient absorption?
  1. Jejunum
  2. Duodenum
  3. Ileum
  4. Colon
  5. Stomach
Answer: A. The jejunum's folds, villi and microvilli create enormous absorptive surface area.Module 13 GI deck, slide 11
Q587. Vitamin B12 and bile salts are absorbed in the:
  1. Duodenum
  2. Ileum
  3. Jejunum
  4. Colon
  5. Stomach
Answer: B. The ileum is the specialist — B12 with intrinsic factor, plus bile salt recycling.Module 13 GI deck, slide 9
Q588. Fat-soluble vitamins A, D, E and K are absorbed primarily in the:
  1. Stomach
  2. Colon
  3. Ileum
  4. Esophagus
  5. Mouth
Answer: C. They require bile for absorption and are taken up in the ileum.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q589. The large intestine's principal absorptive function is to reclaim:
  1. Amino acids from protein
  2. Glucose and monosaccharides
  3. Fatty acids and glycerol
  4. Water and electrolytes
  5. Vitamin B12 and bile salts
Answer: D. This is what converts liquid residue into formed stool.Module 13 GI deck, slide 11
Q590. Which statement about the large intestine is TRUE?
  1. It secretes most of the body's digestive enzymes, including amylase, lipase and trypsin, into its lumen
  2. It is the main absorptive site for amino acids and other products of protein digestion
  3. It produces bile and stores it; the bile is then released into the lumen to emulsify remaining fat
  4. It is lined throughout with villi that greatly increase its area for nutrient uptake
  5. It secretes no digestive enzymes of its own; resident bacteria perform any remaining breakdown
