Answer: E. Colonic bacteria also synthesize vitamin K and several B vitamins.Module 13 GI deck, slide 11
Q591. Elimination refers to:
  1. Expulsion of undigested waste from the body
  2. Enzymatic breakdown of food into absorbable units
  3. Absorption of nutrients into blood and lymph
  4. Emulsification of fat by bile salts in the gut
  5. Storage of concentrated bile in the gallbladder
Answer: A. It is the final step of the digestive process.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q592. The defecation reflex involves:
  1. Voluntary relaxation of both the internal and the external anal sphincters under conscious cortical control
  2. Involuntary relaxation of the internal sphincter and voluntary relaxation of the external sphincter
  3. Involuntary relaxation of both anal sphincters, with no conscious control over either one
  4. Reflex contraction of both anal sphincters as the rectal wall is stretched by stool
  5. No sphincter involvement at all; peristalsis of the colon alone expels the stool
Answer: B. The internal sphincter is smooth muscle (involuntary); the external is skeletal muscle (voluntary).Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q593. Which accessory organ PRODUCES bile?
  1. Gallbladder
  2. Pancreas
  3. Liver
  4. Stomach
  5. Duodenum
Answer: C. The liver produces bile continuously; the gallbladder only stores it.Module 13 GI deck, slide 9
Q594. The gallbladder's function is to:
  1. Produce bile continuously from cholesterol and the bilirubin pigment of old red cells
  2. Produce insulin and glucagon from clusters of endocrine cells scattered in its wall
  3. Secrete pepsinogen and intrinsic factor into the stomach
  4. Store and concentrate bile, releasing it when fat arrives
  5. Absorb the products of fat digestion into the lymph
Answer: D. After cholecystectomy, bile drains continuously rather than in concentrated boluses.Module 13 GI deck, slide 10
Q595. Bile's role in digestion is to:
  1. Chemically break triglycerides into free fatty acids and monoglycerides
  2. Digest dietary protein into peptides and free amino acids
  3. Neutralize acid in the mouth and protect the tooth enamel
  4. Break dietary starch down into maltose and simple sugars
  5. Emulsify fat into smaller droplets, increasing surface area for lipase
Answer: E. Bile is NOT an enzyme — emulsification is a mechanical action.Module 13 GI deck, slide 3
Q596. The difference between bile and pancreatic lipase is that bile:
  1. Physically emulsifies fat while lipase chemically digests it
  2. Chemically digests fat while lipase emulsifies
  3. Both act identically, breaking the same bonds in triglycerides
  4. Bile acts on dietary protein while lipase acts on fat
  5. Lipase acts on carbohydrate while bile acts on triglycerides
Answer: A. This distinction is a favorite exam target.Module 13 GI deck, slide 9
Q597. Which organ produces the greatest variety of digestive enzymes?
  1. Liver
  2. Pancreas
  3. Gallbladder
  4. Stomach
  5. Salivary glands
Answer: B. Pancreatic juice contains amylase, lipase, proteases and nucleases — covering all macronutrients.Module 13 GI deck, slide 3
Q598. Pancreatic proteases are secreted in inactive form in order to:
  1. Save metabolic energy during secretion
  2. Improve absorption of amino acids in the ileum
  3. Prevent the pancreas from digesting itself
  4. Increase the acidity of chyme in the duodenum
  5. Slow gastric emptying through the pyloric sphincter
Answer: C. Premature activation within the pancreas causes acute pancreatitis.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q599. Bicarbonate in pancreatic juice functions to:
  1. Emulsify dietary fat into fine droplets so that pancreatic lipase can reach the fat
  2. Digest protein into short peptides before they reach the jejunum
  3. Absorb water and electrolytes back across the intestinal wall
  4. Neutralize acidic chyme entering the duodenum, creating optimal pH for enzymes
  5. Activate pepsinogen into pepsin so protein digestion continues
Answer: D. Pancreatic enzymes work best near neutral pH, so the acid must be buffered.Module 13 GI deck, slide 8
Q600. Cholecystokinin (CCK) is released in response to ____ and causes ____.
  1. acid in the duodenum; release of bicarbonate-rich juice from the pancreas
  2. rising blood glucose; insulin release from the pancreatic islet beta cells
  3. stretch of the stomach wall; increased secretion of gastric acid and stronger gastric motility
  4. water arriving in the colon; stronger peristalsis and faster transit of stool
  5. fat and protein in the duodenum; gallbladder contraction and pancreatic enzyme release
Answer: E. Remember C for CCK, C for Contract the gallbladder.Module 13 GI deck, slide 10
Q601. Secretin is released in response to ____ and causes ____.
  1. acidic chyme entering the duodenum; pancreatic bicarbonate secretion
  2. fat and protein in the duodenum; gallbladder contraction and bile release
  3. protein fragments in the stomach; release of pepsinogen by chief cells
  4. rising blood glucose after a meal; insulin release by the pancreas
  5. stretch of the stomach wall; gastrin release and increased acid output
Answer: A. Secretin's job is to neutralize, matching the acid stimulus to a bicarbonate response.Module 13 GI deck, slide 8
Q602. Gastrin is released by the stomach in response to ____ and causes ____.
  1. fat and protein arriving in the duodenum; gallbladder contraction and bile release into the small intestine
  2. food entering the stomach and stretch; increased acid secretion and motility
  3. acidic chyme reaching the duodenum; pancreatic bicarbonate release that neutralizes it
  4. rising blood glucose; insulin release from the pancreatic islet cells
  5. bile salts reaching the terminal ileum; release of pancreatic proenzymes
Answer: B. Gastrin ramps the stomach up when food arrives.Module 13 GI deck, slide 12
Q603. Insulin and glucagon are produced by the pancreas and act to:
  1. Digest dietary starch, fat and protein within the lumen of the small intestine
  2. Neutralize the acidic chyme with bicarbonate as it enters the duodenum
  3. Regulate blood glucose — insulin lowers it, glucagon raises it
  4. Emulsify dietary fat into fine droplets before lipase acts
  5. Absorb the fat-soluble vitamins from the intestinal lumen
Answer: C. These are the pancreas's ENDOCRINE products, distinct from its digestive enzymes.Module 13 GI deck, slide 8
Q604. Salivary glands contribute to digestion by producing all of the following EXCEPT:
  1. Salivary amylase
  2. Mucins for lubrication
  3. Bicarbonate to buffer pH
  4. Pepsin for protein digestion
  5. Lysozyme for antibacterial defense
Answer: D. Pepsin is gastric, not salivary.Module 13 GI deck, slide 8
Q605. The tongue contributes to digestion by:
  1. Producing the bile salts that emulsify dietary fat within the small intestine
  2. Absorbing amino acids, simple sugars, and fatty acids into the bloodstream
  3. Producing the hormone insulin in response to a carbohydrate meal
  4. Neutralizing gastric acid with its bicarbonate-rich secretions
  5. Manipulating the bolus, sensing taste, and secreting lingual lipase
Answer: E. It has mechanical, sensory and chemical roles.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q606. A patient has their gallbladder removed. The most likely lasting effect is:
  1. Difficulty digesting large fatty meals, since concentrated bile can no longer be released in a bolus
  2. Complete inability to produce bile, since the gallbladder is where bile is manufactured
  3. Complete loss of all fat digestion, because pancreatic lipase can no longer reach the duodenum at all
  4. Inability to absorb protein, because peptide uptake in the jejunum depends on bile
  5. Total loss of pancreatic enzyme secretion, leaving starch and protein undigested
Answer: A. The liver still makes bile, but it drips continuously rather than being stored and concentrated.Module 13 GI deck, slide 10
Q607. A patient with chronic pancreatitis would most likely show:
  1. Protein deficiency alone, because trypsin is lost while fat digestion stays normal
  2. Fat malabsorption with fatty stools, because pancreatic lipase is deficient
  3. Isolated vitamin B12 deficiency, because pancreatitis destroys gastric intrinsic factor
  4. Excess bile production, causing greenish stools and rapid fat digestion
  5. Increased carbohydrate absorption from excess pancreatic amylase release
Answer: B. Loss of exocrine pancreatic function impairs digestion of all macronutrients, fat most visibly.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q608. Following surgical removal of the terminal ileum, the patient is at greatest risk of deficiency in:
  1. Vitamin C
  2. Dietary glucose
  3. Vitamin B12
  4. Sodium ions
  5. Dietary protein
Answer: C. The ileum is the only site of B12 absorption, so its loss produces megaloblastic anemia over time.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q609. The four layers of the GI tract wall from lumen outward are:
  1. Serosa, muscularis, submucosa, mucosa
  2. Submucosa, mucosa, serosa, muscularis
  3. Muscularis, mucosa, serosa, submucosa
  4. Mucosa, submucosa, muscularis, serosa
  5. Mucosa, muscularis, serosa, submucosa
Answer: D. Mucosa lines the lumen; serosa is the outermost covering.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q610. The enteric nervous system is notable because it:
  1. Requires constant input from the brainstem to function
  2. Controls only the stomach and nothing distal to it
  3. Contains no neurons, only smooth muscle fibers
  4. Is purely sympathetic, with no parasympathetic input
  5. Can coordinate digestion largely independently
Answer: E. Often called the gut's own brain, it runs reflexes locally.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q611. Which sphincter controls stomach emptying into the duodenum?
  1. Pyloric
  2. Lower esophageal
  3. Ileocecal
  4. Internal anal
  5. Sphincter of Oddi
Answer: A. The pyloric sphincter releases chyme in small amounts.Module 13 GI deck, slide 4
Q612. Rugae in the stomach allow it to:
  1. Absorb amino acids and glucose
  2. Expand as it fills
  3. Produce and store bile salts
  4. Secrete insulin
  5. Filter the blood
Answer: B. Folds flatten as the stomach distends.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q613. Which stomach cell secretes hydrochloric acid?

  2. G cell
  3. Parietal cell
  4. Goblet cell
  5. Mucous neck cell
Answer: C. Parietal cells also make intrinsic factor.Module 13 GI deck, slide 6
Q614. Chief cells secrete:
  1. HCl
  2. Gastrin
  3. Mucus
  4. Pepsinogen
  5. Intrinsic factor
Answer: D. Pepsinogen is activated to pepsin by gastric acid.Module 13 GI deck, slide 6
Q615. Villi and microvilli increase absorption by:
  1. Slowing transit time through the gut
  2. Producing digestive enzymes only
  3. Secreting acid into the lumen
  4. Adding extra smooth muscle layers
  5. Massively increasing surface area
Answer: E. Surface area determines how much can be absorbed per unit time.Module 13 GI deck, slide 7
Q616. Emulsification differs from enzymatic digestion because it:
  1. Physically disperses fat into smaller droplets
  2. Breaks the ester bonds within triglycerides
  3. Requires pancreatic lipase to take place
  4. Occurs in the stomach only, before chyme leaves
  5. Produces free amino acids and short peptides
Answer: A. Bile increases surface area without breaking any bonds.Module 13 GI deck, slide 9
Q617. The hepatic portal vein carries blood from the:
  1. Lungs to the left side of the heart
  2. Intestines to the liver
  3. Liver to the inferior vena cava and heart
  4. Kidney to the bladder
  5. Heart to the stomach
Answer: B. Absorbed nutrients are processed by the liver before entering circulation.Module 13 GI deck, slide 9
Q618. A key function of the liver in metabolism is to:
  1. Produce insulin in response to a rising glucose level
  2. Absorb digested nutrients directly from the gut lumen
  3. Store glucose as glycogen and release it as needed
  4. Secrete pepsin to begin the digestion of protein
  5. Produce chyme by churning food with gastric juice
Answer: C. The liver buffers blood glucose between meals.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q619. Deamination in the liver produces which waste requiring excretion?
  1. Creatinine
  2. Bilirubin
  3. Lactic acid
  4. Urea
  5. Uric acid
Answer: D. Amino group removal yields ammonia, converted to urea.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q620. Bilirubin originates from the breakdown of:
  1. Dietary protein in the small intestine
  2. Triglycerides broken down by lipase
  3. Glycogen stored in liver cells
  4. Bile salts recycled from the ileum
  5. Hemoglobin from old red blood cells
Answer: E. Heme catabolism yields bilirubin, excreted in bile.Module 13 GI deck, slide 9
Q621. Jaundice results from accumulation of:
  1. Bilirubin
  2. Urea
  3. Glucose
  4. Albumin
  5. Bile salts
Answer: A. Excess bilirubin stains skin and sclera yellow.Lecture Outline, Module 13 — p.147
Q622. Lactose intolerance is caused by deficiency of:
  1. Pancreatic amylase in the duodenum
  2. Lactase in the brush border
  3. Pepsin in the gastric lumen
  4. Lipase from the pancreas
  5. Trypsin in the small intestine

Q623. Which best explains why the colon's bacteria matter clinically?

  2. They absorb water and sodium from the stool
  3. They synthesize vitamin K and some B vitamins
  4. They produce bile salts for fat emulsification
  5. They secrete amylase and lipase into the colon
Answer: C. Broad-spectrum antibiotics can disrupt this and affect clotting.Module 13 GI deck, slide 11
Q624. Which is NOT a function of the kidneys?
  1. Filtering blood and excreting waste
  2. Regulating blood volume and pressure
  3. Maintaining acid-base balance
  4. Producing bile for fat digestion
  5. Producing erythropoietin
Answer: D. Bile is a liver product. The kidneys filter, regulate, balance and secrete hormones.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q625. The kidneys regulate blood pressure primarily by:
  1. Directly increasing heart rate and stroke volume
  2. Producing bile that raises plasma protein levels
  3. Increasing red blood cell size to thicken the blood
  4. Altering lung ventilation to change blood gases
  5. Adjusting water excretion and releasing renin
Answer: E. Retaining water raises blood volume, and renin initiates the RAAS cascade.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q626. Erythropoietin, produced by the kidney, functions to:
  1. Stimulate red blood cell production in bone marrow
  2. Raise blood pressure by constricting systemic arterioles
  3. Regulate calcium absorption from the small intestine
  4. Neutralize acid by generating new bicarbonate ions
  5. Increase urine output by blocking sodium reabsorption
Answer: A. This is why chronic kidney disease so often causes anemia.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q627. The kidneys maintain acid-base balance by:
  1. Exhaling carbon dioxide to blow off volatile acid
  2. Excreting hydrogen ions and reabsorbing bicarbonate
  3. Producing carbonic acid to lower the blood pH directly
  4. Storing excess acid in the bladder until voiding
  5. Changing the respiratory rate to adjust carbon dioxide
Answer: B. The kidney is the slower but more powerful partner to the lungs in pH control.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q628. Water movement in the body follows which ion most closely?
  1. Potassium
  2. Calcium
  3. Sodium
  4. Magnesium
  5. Iron
Answer: C. Retaining sodium retains water, raising blood volume and pressure.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q629. If the kidneys excrete large amounts of sodium, the expected effect is:
  1. Increased blood volume and pressure, since sodium loss draws water in
  2. No change, because water balance is regulated separately
  3. Increased potassium retention with no change in blood volume
  4. Decreased blood volume and pressure, since water follows sodium out
  5. Decreased urine output with a rise in plasma sodium levels
Answer: D. This is precisely how diuretics lower blood pressure.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q630. Which waste products are normally excreted in urine?
  1. Glucose and protein
  2. Red blood cells
  3. Albumin and globulins
  4. Bile salts and bilirubin
  5. Urea and creatinine
Answer: E. Urea and creatinine are normal nitrogenous wastes. Glucose, protein and blood cells in urine signal pathology.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q631. Finding protein in the urine most likely indicates:
  1. Damage to the glomerular filtration barrier
  2. Active tubular secretion of albumin into the urine
  3. Excess water intake diluting the urine sample
  4. Low blood pressure reducing glomerular filtration
  5. High potassium from aldosterone deficiency
Answer: A. Proteins are normally too large to be filtered, so proteinuria means the filter is leaking.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q632. Filtration in the nephron occurs at the:
  1. Proximal convoluted tubule
  2. Glomerulus
  3. Loop of Henle
  4. Distal convoluted tubule
  5. Collecting duct
Answer: B. The glomerulus is the only site of filtration; everything downstream modifies the filtrate.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q633. Which substances are normally RETAINED in the blood during glomerular filtration?
  1. Water and sodium, which the filtration barrier holds back completely
  2. Glucose and amino acids, whose molecules are too big to enter the filtrate
  3. Proteins and blood cells, which are too large to pass
  4. Urea and creatinine, which the filtration barrier excludes entirely
  5. Potassium and chloride, which stay bound to plasma proteins
Answer: C. The filtration barrier excludes large molecules and cells.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q634. The efferent arteriole is narrower than the afferent arteriole in order to:
  1. Slow blood flow to protect the renal cortex from damage
  2. Increase urine volume directly by widening the tubules
  3. Prevent blood entering the peritubular capillaries
  4. Maintain the high glomerular pressure that filtration requires
  5. Filter large plasma proteins directly into the filtrate
Answer: D. A restricted outlet sustains back-pressure inside the glomerular capillaries.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q635. Constriction of the afferent arteriole would:
  1. Increase filtration by raising glomerular hydrostatic pressure
  2. Have no effect at all, since the efferent arteriole alone determines filtration rate
  3. Increase blood pressure inside the glomerular capillaries
  4. Cause protein loss by tearing the filtration membrane
  5. Decrease filtration by reducing blood delivery to the glomerulus
Answer: E. Less blood entering means less filtrate formed.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q636. Most reabsorption in the nephron occurs in the:
  1. Proximal convoluted tubule
  2. Glomerulus and Bowman's capsule
  3. Descending limb of Henle's loop
  4. Distal convoluted tubule
  5. Cortical collecting duct
Answer: A. The PCT reclaims the bulk of water, sodium, chloride, and essentially all glucose and amino acids.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q637. Glucose normally appears in the urine only when:
  1. The kidneys are healthy and filtering plasma at a completely normal rate
  2. Blood glucose exceeds the reabsorptive capacity of the PCT
  3. Water intake is high enough to dilute the filtrate moving through the tubule
  4. Sodium intake is low enough to switch off aldosterone secretion
  5. ADH is absent, allowing glucose to escape in the urine
Answer: B. Glycosuria is a hallmark of poorly controlled diabetes mellitus.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q638. The DESCENDING limb of the loop of Henle is permeable to:
  1. Sodium only
  2. Both water and solutes equally
  3. Water only
  4. Neither
  5. Protein
Answer: C. Water leaves, concentrating the filtrate as it descends into the salty medulla.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q639. The ASCENDING limb of the loop of Henle is:
  1. Permeable to water throughout, while blocking all Na+, K+ and Cl- movement
  2. Freely permeable to water and to every solute dissolved in the filtrate
  3. Impermeable to both water and solutes along its length
  4. Impermeable to water, actively pumping out Na+, K+ and Cl-
  5. The main site of filtration and protein reabsorption
Answer: D. Removing salt without water is what builds the medullary concentration gradient.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q640. The overall purpose of the loop of Henle is to:
  1. Filter blood plasma under high hydrostatic pressure so the initial filtrate enters the capsular space
  2. Secrete the hormones renin and erythropoietin into the bloodstream from specialized kidney cells
  3. Reclaim all of the filtered glucose and amino acids by sodium-linked cotransport across the brush border
  4. Store the finished urine inside the bladder until stretch receptors trigger the micturition reflex
  5. Establish the medullary concentration gradient that allows concentrated urine to be produced
Answer: E. Without this gradient the kidney could not conserve water.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q641. The distal convoluted tubule responds to which hormone to reabsorb sodium?
  1. Aldosterone
  2. Vasopressin
  3. Oxytocin
  4. Calcitonin
  5. Erythropoietin
Answer: A. Aldosterone drives sodium reabsorption and potassium secretion at the DCT.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q642. The collecting duct determines final urine concentration under the influence of:
  1. Aldosterone from the adrenals
  2. Antidiuretic hormone (ADH)
  3. Insulin from the pancreas
  4. Glucagon from the alpha cells
  5. Renin from the kidney itself
Answer: B. ADH inserts aquaporins, allowing water to be reclaimed.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q643. High ADH levels produce urine that is:
  1. Large in volume and very dilute
  2. Unchanged in volume and osmolarity
  3. Small in volume and concentrated
  4. Normal in volume but full of protein
  5. Large in volume and full of glucose
Answer: C. This is the dehydration response — conserve water at all costs.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q644. Alcohol suppresses ADH, which explains why it causes:
  1. Water retention and dilute plasma sodium
  2. Concentrated urine with high osmolarity
  3. Sodium retention driven by aldosterone
  4. Increased urine output and dehydration
  5. Reduced thirst and lower urine output
Answer: D. Without ADH the collecting duct cannot reclaim water.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q645. Reabsorption moves substances ____, while secretion moves them ____.
  1. blood → filtrate; filtrate → blood
  2. both processes move filtrate → blood
  3. both processes move blood → filtrate
  4. neither; both stay in the filtrate
  5. filtrate → blood; blood → filtrate
Answer: E. Reabsorption keeps what is useful; secretion dumps extra waste, H+ and K+.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q646. Place the stages of urine formation in correct order:
  1. Filtration, reabsorption, secretion, excretion
  2. Reabsorption, filtration, secretion, excretion
  3. Secretion, filtration, reabsorption, excretion
  4. Filtration, secretion, reabsorption, voiding
  5. Voiding, filtration, reabsorption, secretion
Answer: A. Filter everything small, reclaim what is needed, dump extra waste, then excrete.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q647. Trace the correct path of urine leaving the kidney:
  1. Collecting duct → ureter → renal pelvis → bladder → urethra
  2. Collecting duct → renal pelvis → ureter → bladder → urethra
  3. Bladder → renal pelvis → ureter → urethra → collecting duct
  4. Urethra → bladder → ureter → renal pelvis → nephron
  5. Nephron → urethra → ureter → bladder → renal pelvis
Answer: B. Ureters carry urine down to the bladder; the single urethra carries it out.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q648. Walk RAAS in the correct order:
  1. High BP → aldosterone → renin → ACE → angiotensin II → angiotensin I
  2. Low BP → angiotensin II → renin → aldosterone → ACE → angiotensin I
  3. Low BP → renin → angiotensin I → ACE → angiotensin II → aldosterone
  4. Low BP → ACE → aldosterone → renin → angiotensin I → angiotensin II
  5. Low BP → renin → aldosterone → ACE → angiotensin I → angiotensin II
Answer: C. Low pressure starts it; aldosterone-driven sodium and water retention ends it.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q649. Angiotensin II raises blood pressure by:
  1. Widespread vasodilation of arterioles with no change in aldosterone output
  2. Increasing urine output by inhibiting sodium reabsorption all along the tubule
  3. Reducing heart rate through direct stimulation of the vagus nerve
  4. Potent vasoconstriction plus stimulation of aldosterone release
  5. Blocking sodium reabsorption in the proximal tubule
Answer: D. It works through two mechanisms at once, which is why it is such an effective target for antihypertensives.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q650. ACE inhibitors lower blood pressure by:
  1. Blocking renin release from the juxtaglomerular cells of the nephron
  2. Stimulating the adrenal cortex to release extra aldosterone into the blood
  3. Blocking ADH receptors in the collecting duct of the nephron
  4. Increasing sodium reabsorption in the distal convoluted tubule
  5. Preventing conversion of angiotensin I to angiotensin II
Answer: E. Interrupting the cascade reduces both vasoconstriction and sodium retention.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q651. Aldosterone causes the kidney to:
  1. Reabsorb sodium and water while excreting potassium
  2. Excrete sodium and water while retaining potassium
  3. Excrete both sodium and potassium while retaining water
  4. Reabsorb both sodium and potassium while excreting water
  5. Excrete water only, leaving sodium levels unchanged
Answer: A. Sodium in, potassium out — which is why aldosterone problems disturb potassium balance.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q652. Spermatogenesis occurs within the:
  1. Coiled epididymis on the testis
  2. Seminiferous tubules of the testes
  3. Vas deferens of the spermatic cord
  4. Glandular tissue of the prostate
  5. Seminal vesicles behind the bladder
Answer: B. The seminiferous tubules are the site of sperm production.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q653. One primary spermatocyte ultimately produces how many mature sperm?
  1. One
  2. Two
  3. Four
  4. Three
  5. Eight
Answer: C. Meiosis I gives two secondary spermatocytes; meiosis II gives four spermatids, all of which mature.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q654. Which structure in the sperm head contains enzymes for penetrating the egg?
  1. Nucleus
  2. Midpiece
  3. Flagellum
  4. Acrosome
  5. Centriole
Answer: D. The acrosome is a cap of hydrolytic enzymes that digest the oocyte's outer coating.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q655. The midpiece of a sperm is packed with mitochondria in order to:
  1. Store the condensed genetic material of the sperm
  2. Release enzymes that penetrate the egg coating
  3. Protect the sperm from the acidic vaginal environment
  4. Produce the testosterone that drives maturation
  5. Generate the ATP required for flagellar movement
Answer: E. Swimming is metabolically expensive, so the energy plant sits right behind the head.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q656. Sperm gain motility and the ability to fertilize while stored in the:
  1. Epididymis
  2. Seminiferous tubules
  3. Vas deferens
  4. Prostate
  5. Urethra
Answer: A. Sperm leaving the testis are immature; maturation takes several days in the epididymis.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q657. Spermiogenesis refers to:
  1. The final meiotic division in which secondary spermatocytes split to form haploid spermatids
  2. The remodeling of spermatids into streamlined spermatozoa, with no further division
  3. The storage of maturing sperm inside the coiled epididymis until they are released at ejaculation
  4. The transport of maturing sperm along the vas deferens toward the urethra during emission
  5. The production of testosterone by the Leydig cells of the testis
Answer: B. It is a shape change, not a cell division.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q658. Unlike spermatogenesis, oogenesis:
  1. Begins only at puberty, when germ cells first enter meiosis
  2. Produces four equally sized functional gametes from every cell that enters meiosis
  3. Begins before birth, with all primary oocytes formed by the fetal period
  4. Continues indefinitely throughout the entire adult lifespan
  5. Occurs in the lining of the uterus rather than the gonad
Answer: C. A female is born with her lifetime supply of oocytes, all arrested in meiosis I.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q659. What is actually released at ovulation?
  1. A mature ovum that has completed meiosis II
  2. A primary oocyte arrested in meiosis I
  3. A zygote with a full diploid chromosome set
  4. A secondary oocyte arrested in meiosis II
  5. An oogonium that has not yet begun meiosis
Answer: D. It becomes a true ovum only when a sperm penetrates it and meiosis II completes.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q660. Meiosis II in the oocyte completes only when:
  1. Ovulation occurs
  2. Menstruation begins
  3. FSH levels rise sharply
  4. The corpus luteum forms
  5. Fertilization occurs
Answer: E. This is why fertilization technically precedes the formation of the mature ovum.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q661. The zona pellucida functions to:
  1. Protect the oocyte and regulate sperm entry, blocking polyspermy
  2. Nourish the oocyte with nutrients from surrounding granulosa cells
  3. Produce the estrogen that drives endometrial thickening
  4. Anchor the developing follicle within the ovarian cortex
  5. Transport the oocyte along the fallopian tube by ciliary action
Answer: A. Once one sperm binds, the zona hardens to exclude the rest.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q662. The corpus luteum forms after ovulation and secretes:
  1. Luteinizing hormone
  2. Progesterone
  3. Aldosterone
  4. Thyroxine
  5. Prolactin
Answer: B. Progesterone maintains the endometrium in case implantation occurs.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q663. If fertilization does not occur, the corpus luteum:
  1. Enlarges steadily and keeps releasing progesterone for several months afterwards
  2. Converts directly into a new primary follicle that is ready for the next ovulation
  3. Degenerates into the corpus albicans, causing hormone levels to fall
  4. Produces steadily more estrogen and continues to thicken the endometrium
  5. Persists indefinitely as a fully functioning endocrine gland in the ovary
Answer: C. Its death withdraws progesterone support, triggering menstruation.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q664. Which hormone stimulates follicle growth during the follicular phase?
  1. LH
  2. Progesterone
  3. Oxytocin
  4. FSH
  5. Prolactin
Answer: D. FSH — Follicle Stimulating Hormone — recruits and grows the follicles.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q665. Ovulation is triggered by:
  1. A sudden drop in circulating estrogen
  2. A rise in progesterone from the ovary
  3. A sharp fall in follicle stimulating hormone
  4. The onset of menstrual bleeding
  5. A surge of luteinizing hormone (LH)
Answer: E. Peak estrogen flips to positive feedback, producing the LH surge that ruptures the follicle.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q666. Menstruation occurs because:
  1. Progesterone and estrogen fall as the corpus luteum degenerates, withdrawing support from the endometrium
  2. A sharp rise in estrogen during the follicular phase breaks down the uterine lining without help from any other hormone
  3. A luteinizing hormone surge ruptures the follicle and strips away the uterine lining
  4. Falling follicle stimulating hormone gradually starves the endometrial lining of its blood supply until it sloughs
  5. Rupture of the dominant follicle at ovulation tears the uterine wall and releases blood into the uterine cavity
Answer: A. Bleeding results from withdrawal of hormonal support, not from an active signal.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q667. During the follicular phase, rising estrogen acts on the uterus to:
  1. Shed the functional layer of the lining
  2. Rebuild and thicken the endometrium
  3. Trigger ovulation directly
  4. Maintain the corpus luteum after ovulation
  5. Prevent implantation of the blastocyst
Answer: B. This is the proliferative phase of the endometrium.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q668. During the luteal phase, progesterone acts to:
  1. Thin the endometrium and shed it so a new cycle can begin
  2. Trigger the midcycle luteinizing hormone surge that causes ovulation
  3. Stabilize and maintain the endometrium, making it receptive to implantation
  4. Recruit and grow a new cohort of ovarian follicles for the next cycle
  5. Cause the endometrial lining to break down and bleed immediately
Answer: C. Progesterone means pro-gestation — it holds the lining in place.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q669. Which phase corresponds to approximately days 15-28 of a 28-day cycle?
  1. Menstrual
  2. Follicular
  3. Ovulation
  4. Luteal
  5. Proliferative
Answer: D. The luteal phase follows ovulation and is dominated by progesterone.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q670. Menopause is formally defined as:
  1. The first missed period after the age of forty
  2. Any irregular bleeding after the age of forty-five
  3. The point at which estrogen levels first start to fall
  4. The start of perimenopause with irregular cycles
  5. Twelve consecutive months without a menstrual period
Answer: E. Twelve months of amenorrhea marks the definitive transition.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q671. During perimenopause, the ovaries:
  1. Become less responsive to FSH and LH, so estrogen and progesterone fall and fluctuate
  2. Become more responsive to FSH and LH, so estrogen and progesterone climb steadily
  3. Stop producing any hormone abruptly, so cycles end within a single month
  4. Increase follicle production, releasing several oocytes each cycle
  5. Enlarge significantly and secrete far more estrogen than in earlier years
Answer: A. Declining ovarian responsiveness produces the irregular cycles typical of this phase.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q672. In menopause, estrogen ____ and FSH/LH ____.
  1. rises; rises
  2. falls; rises
  3. falls; falls
  4. rises; falls
  5. both stay constant
Answer: B. This is the counterintuitive point: loss of negative feedback lets FSH and LH climb even as estrogen collapses.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q673. Why do FSH and LH RISE during menopause?
  1. The pituitary gland becomes diseased and secretes hormones without control
  2. Rising estrogen stimulates the pituitary through positive feedback at all times
  3. The ovaries no longer respond, so negative feedback is lost and the pituitary keeps signaling
  4. The hypothalamus itself degenerates and pours out uncontrolled amounts of gonadotropin releasing hormone
  5. They are stockpiled in the anterior pituitary through the reproductive years and released all at once
Answer: C. Elevated FSH is used clinically to confirm menopause.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q674. Which symptom set is most characteristic of menopause?
  1. Weight loss, increased fertility and noticeably heavier menstrual flow
  2. Increased bone density, thicker skin and steadily improved sleep quality
  3. Elevated estrogen levels with low FSH and regular cycles
  4. Hot flashes, night sweats, mood changes and vaginal dryness
  5. Regular heavy cycles with steadily rising progesterone each month
Answer: D. Vasomotor symptoms arise from falling estrogen affecting hypothalamic thermoregulation.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q675. The major long-term health risks of postmenopausal estrogen deficiency are:
  1. Type 1 diabetes and chronic kidney failure
  2. Aplastic anemia and recurrent infection
  3. Liver cirrhosis and pigmented gallstones
  4. Sensorineural hearing loss and cataracts
  5. Osteoporosis and cardiovascular disease
Answer: E. Estrogen restrains osteoclasts and has favorable vascular effects, so its loss raises both risks.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q676. Why does osteoporosis risk increase sharply after menopause?
  1. Falling estrogen removes a brake on osteoclast activity, so resorption outpaces formation
  2. Dietary calcium intake falls sharply, leaving the skeleton undermineralized
  3. The kidneys stop activating vitamin D at menopause, so intestinal calcium absorption ceases entirely
  4. Osteoblasts disappear from bone entirely, so no new matrix is laid down
  5. The kidneys stop filtering, so phosphate builds up and dissolves bone
Answer: A. This connects Module 14 directly back to the bone remodeling covered in Module 4.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q677. The functional unit of the kidney is the:
  1. Renal pyramid
  2. Nephron
  3. Ureter
  4. Calyx
  5. Glomerulus alone
Answer: B. About one million nephrons per kidney perform filtration and modification.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q678. Filtration at the glomerulus is driven primarily by:
  1. Active transport pumps in the capsule wall
  2. Osmosis driven by plasma protein concentration
  3. Glomerular hydrostatic (blood) pressure
  4. Antidiuretic hormone from the pituitary
  5. Aldosterone from the adrenal cortex
Answer: C. High capillary pressure forces fluid into Bowman's capsule.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q679. Normal glomerular filtration rate is approximately:
  1. 1,250 mL/min
  2. 12.5 mL/min
  3. 1.5 L/min
  4. 125 mL/min
  5. 25 mL/min
Answer: D. Roughly 180 liters are filtered daily, and almost all is reabsorbed.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q680. The juxtaglomerular apparatus regulates:
  1. Urine color and concentration
  2. Bilirubin breakdown in the blood
  3. Calcium storage in the renal cortex
  4. Red blood cell shape and lifespan
  5. Blood pressure and GFR via renin release
Answer: E. It senses pressure and sodium delivery, releasing renin as needed.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q681. Which is normally NOT found in filtrate?
  1. Large plasma proteins
  2. Free water molecules
  3. Filtered glucose
  4. Sodium and chloride ions
  5. Urea and creatinine
Answer: A. Proteins are too large to cross the filtration barrier.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q682. Countercurrent multiplication in the loop of Henle serves to:
  1. Filter plasma from the blood into the glomerular capsule
  2. Establish a medullary osmotic gradient
  3. Secrete potassium and hydrogen ions into the filtrate
  4. Produce renin from specialized granular cells
  5. Reabsorb all of the filtered glucose and amino acids
Answer: B. The gradient allows water reabsorption from the collecting duct.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q683. Aldosterone acts on the DCT and collecting duct to:
  1. Excrete sodium and reabsorb potassium
  2. Reabsorb potassium and excrete water
  3. Reabsorb sodium and secrete potassium
  4. Excrete water without moving any sodium
  5. Produce renin and erythropoietin
Answer: C. Sodium retention draws water with it, raising blood volume.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q684. Atrial natriuretic peptide (ANP) opposes RAAS by:
  1. Retaining sodium and water in the blood
  2. Raising blood pressure by vasoconstriction
  3. Releasing renin from the kidney cortex
  4. Promoting sodium and water excretion
  5. Stimulating aldosterone secretion
Answer: D. Released when atria stretch, ANP lowers blood volume and pressure.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q685. Which substance is used clinically to estimate GFR?
  1. Blood glucose
  2. Serum albumin
  3. Serum sodium
  4. Bilirubin
  5. Creatinine
Answer: E. Creatinine is filtered and minimally reabsorbed, so it tracks GFR.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q686. Micturition involves relaxation of the internal sphincter, which is:
  1. Smooth muscle and involuntary
  2. Skeletal and voluntary
  3. Made of hyaline cartilage
  4. Formed from dense fibrous connective tissue
  5. Under conscious cortical control at all times
Answer: A. The external sphincter is skeletal and voluntary.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q687. Spermatogenesis requires a testicular temperature that is:
  1. Above core body temperature
  2. Slightly below core body temperature
  3. Identical to core body temperature
  4. Irrelevant to sperm production
  5. Consistently above 40 degrees Celsius
Answer: B. The scrotum keeps the testes a few degrees cooler than the body.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q688. Sertoli cells function to:
  1. Produce testosterone in response to LH
  2. Store mature sperm until ejaculation
  3. Nourish and support developing sperm
  4. Produce the fluid portion of semen
  5. Secrete FSH from the anterior pituitary
Answer: C. Sertoli cells sustain spermatogenesis and form the blood-testis barrier.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q689. Leydig (interstitial) cells produce:
  1. FSH
  2. Inhibin
  3. Sperm
  4. Testosterone
  5. Seminal fluid
Answer: D. LH stimulates Leydig cells to make testosterone.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q690. Which gland contributes an alkaline fluid that neutralizes vaginal acidity?
  1. Adrenal cortex and medulla
  2. Thyroid and parathyroid
  3. Pineal and hypothalamus
  4. Pituitary and thymus
  5. Bulbourethral and prostate
Answer: E. Alkaline secretions protect sperm viability.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q691. Implantation of the blastocyst normally occurs in the:
  1. Endometrium of the uterus
  2. Ampulla of the fallopian tube
  3. Surface of the ovary
  4. Cervical canal of the uterus
  5. Wall of the upper vagina
Answer: A. Tubal implantation is an ectopic pregnancy and is dangerous.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q692. Human chorionic gonadotropin (hCG) functions early in pregnancy to:
  1. Trigger menstruation by withdrawing progesterone support from the lining
  2. Maintain the corpus luteum so progesterone continues
  3. Cause a second ovulation from the opposite ovary later in pregnancy
  4. Stop implantation of the developing blastocyst in the uterine wall
  5. Reduce estrogen output from the placenta
Answer: B. hCG rescues the corpus luteum; it is what pregnancy tests detect.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
Q693. Which structure supplies the fetus with nutrients and removes waste?
  1. Amnion
  2. Corpus luteum
  3. Placenta
  4. Endometrium alone
  5. Yolk sac only
Answer: C. The placenta is the exchange organ between maternal and fetal blood.Lecture Outline, Module 14 — p.158
